## 4.1 Understanding Strategic Decision-Making

In the quick-moving, multifaceted business world of today and during the strategic decision-making process, those making decisions are continually faced with the problem of taking many factors and options into account to make well-informed decisions. Making tactical business decisions is a critical aspect of running a successful business. It involves making decisions that require significant time and resources to handle and can have a significant impact on the organization's future ([Petrou et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_36)). Although a significant portion of business school research and teaching offers frameworks and methods to enhance decision-making, including strategic decision-making, very little of it meets the standards. However, we do have pertinent and helpful advice for managers regarding strategic decision-making. Effective strategic decision-making can help businesses identify new opportunities, optimize their operations, and stay ahead of their competitors. Therefore, managers need to understand the importance of strategic decision-making and develop the necessary skills to make informed decisions ([Papadakis & Barwise, 1998](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_33)).

Because it seems difficult to incorporate sustainability into the strategic decision-making process, top managers frequently develop business strategies and implement these plans without considering the organizational plans. Therefore, understanding for making the strategic decision will be necessary for these businesses which are integrating sustainable goals ([Calabrese et al., 2019](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_6)). According to studies, a lack of a plan will eventually hurt a company's success. Companies without a clear plan often performed the worst out of all the strategy groupings. More importantly, the success of any business is not always correlated to strategy, since a run of weak decisions may also cause failure ([Hauser et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_20)). In the current highly uncertain business climate, it is very difficult to predict long-term decisions without the sight of future changes considering controllable and uncontrollable factors. For making such risky future decisions, decision-makers are currently granted less time. The need for more and better and better analytical tools to make such judgments increases as the world becomes increasingly more erratic, disorganized, and unpredictable. As a result, with the world as it is now, an extensive, methodical framework built on a scientific foundation is required for analysis and decision-making ([Settembre-Blundo et al., 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_44)).

Therefore, it can be said that the collection of information, the development of alternatives, and the selection of an alternative are the sequence of actions that results in the process of strategic future forecasting. Research on the strategic decision-making process is very important because the knowledge it offers may help executives make more successful strategic decisions, which in turn help organizations succeed ([Soni et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_46)).

### 4.1.1 Role of Data and Analytics in Strategic Decision-Making

Data and analytics play a crucial role in strategic decision-making. By leveraging data, managers can gain insights into market trends, customer behavior, and other factors that can inform their strategic decisions. This can be achieved through the use of various data analytics techniques such as data mining, machine learning, and predictive modeling ([Awan et al., 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_4)). In today's “data science and advanced analytics” era, we are surrounded by a technologically driven life and our small details are kept as data. Therefore, the current technological world is becoming a great trove of riches of different types of data, including commercial, monetary, healthcare, multimedia, social-media networks, technological devices, etc.; the amount of data which is fully organized, partially organized, and unorganized expands each day ([Ahmad et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_1)). The term “data science” is being used very commonly to refer to statistics for the processing information using various methodologies in order to understand daily life events. It might be anything that we do, such as studying the perspective into terms like decision-making, discovering things, estimation, service delivery, suggestions, framework, etc. ([Sarker, 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_43)).

However, data alone cannot serve in strategic decision-making and in order to reach some achievable solutions, data must first be reorganized through massive data sets. Through strategic business decision-making, businesses can better forecast future and prepare for future challenges ([Machado et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_29)). For successful decision-making, managers need to understand the difference between preparing for the company policies and operations management which can be accomplished after the successful analysis of data. Data analysis also comes in handy while planning for other facets of businesses like logistics management, people management and marketing and sales, etc. and can also be used for study and research, and government operations ([Niu et al., 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_31)).

Analytics has the potential to be a significant change agent in organizations. Businesses may learn more about a variety of topics, including performance, recruitment and selection, employee productivity, and the effect of improvement, by using data analytics techniques ([Ranjan & Foropon, 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_41)). This data may be applied in several ways to improve operations and guide strategic choices. The use of data and analytics can also help managers make more informed decisions, reduce uncertainty, and make more rational decisions. For instance, by identifying low-performing employees early on, data analytics can assist increase these employees’ chances of success ([Elgendy et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_14)). Similarly, to this, organizations may utilize data analytics to find patterns in hiring, retention, and performance to enhance their recruiting and retention plans. To make sure that the organization's resources are being spent most efficiently, data analytics may be utilized to determine which training techniques are most successful and to discover which top managers are having the most beneficial impact on workers’ growth ([Hamilton & Sodeman, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_19); [Pessach et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_34); [Garg et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_16)). Data analytics begins by defining the distinctions between the data-driven method and traditional statistical analysis, including model estimates and assessment. Critical challenges including data quality (accuracy, objectivity, and dependability), missing values, outliers, and data standardization are heavily stressed ([Phoon et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_37)).

### 4.1.2 Management of Strategic Planning

Despite the benefits of strategic decision-making, managers face several challenges when making strategic decisions. These challenges include limited information, uncertainty, and the need to balance short-term and long-term objectives ([Papadakis & Barwise, 1998](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_33); [Feng et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_15)). Substantial research on the relative effectiveness of several overall techniques for strategic decision-making, based on a huge database of real choices, provides the most comprehensive set of empirically supported suggestions for managers making strategic decisions. Eberhard classifies managers’ strategies for defining directions, finding possibilities, and—after the strategic decision has been taken—executing them by utilizing initial and final measures of adoption as the markers of success. This results in recommendations for setting goals, choosing choices, and then carrying out decisions ([Haessler, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_18); [Eberhard, 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_13)).

[Janssen et al. (2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_26)) are of the view that there has been a lot of attention lately on how decision-makers’ psychological profiles impact their capacity to make wise strategic decisions. The main intellectual factors are cognitive approach, complexity, needs, and variance. Businesses need to understand how adaptable their resources and opportunities are with in environment, and they need to create business plans while considering both external environment and their organizational resources and strengths. Only variation in creativity characteristics during entrepreneurship is currently considered to be a significant factor in the academic community. However, this is generally considered as an external and uncontrollable factor rather than personal characteristics that have a significant impact on major business decisions and management ([Feng et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_15)).

According to [Haessler (2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_18)), making and managing strategic choices differs. This task has to do with the company's future and the organization's medium- and long-term objectives. These choices often demand a strong commitment of resources and have an impact on the entire business. Having to make these judgments with little information and little experience to draw from is a problem for managers. High levels of uncertainty are present in strategic decisions, and they are seldom reversible. Since they are making strategic decisions for the first time, managers frequently feel insecure and turn to outside assistance. There is nothing wrong with obtaining the aid of outside professionals; managers use consultants more frequently to handle management concerns unrelated to operations ([Vuori & Tushman, 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_50)). Abusing this crutch creates a problem because it just delays the acquisition of this tactical ability. The ultimate accountability for an organization's plans rests with its managers; a subcontractor cannot handle this job. They should simply make their strategic decisions following the organization, learn from the process as they go, and select the models that best fit their business ([Phoon et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_37); [Eberhard, 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_13)).

Questions like “Where am I?” “What should I do?” and “How should I go about doing it?” need to be asked by the managers and decision-makers. Good strategic judgments are based on the answers to these queries. Strategic decision-makers must consider these questions in order to insure accountability and organizational goal achievement.

Studies have suggested a model for strategic decisions that follows three steps of *Analysis, Decision and Implementation* to address these issues. This model of strategic choices is predicated on the idea that the organizational purpose should be taken into consideration during the strategic management or planning process. The three-step procedure summarized below can aid managers and executives in making better strategic decisions:

#### 4.1.2.1 Analysis

The purpose of an analysis is to understand the present business situation, and it also involves the understanding of external and internal factors. A better knowledge of the internal and external environments is the goal of the analysis. The process of analysis includes studying concretely and placing things in some definite orderly manner and must be reinforced by keeping realities in check and looking for future success possibilities.

#### 4.1.2.2 Decision

Decision is the core part of any business strategy as it relates to making most optimal choices for deciding future organizational goals and the paths to achieve those goals. Selecting a timely and most suitable alternative amongst the available choices paves the way for the company's future success.

#### 4.1.2.3 Implementation

Implementation refers to the observance of already selected choices taken for business success and it's the most important part of a strategic business plan. The process of implementation is the particle application of things and choices made earlier in order to achieve business objectives and it involves the selection and participation of talented and deserving people who apply their skills and abilities in order to achieve organizational success ([Planellas & Munni, 2019](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_38)).

Making strategic decisions in such an environment requires managers to have a deep understanding of their organization's goals, the market landscape, and the potential risks and opportunities associated with different courses of action. Therefore, managers need to develop the necessary skills and techniques for effective strategic decision-making ([Garg et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_16); [Phoon et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_37)).

## 4.2 Multidimensional Techniques for Strategic Decision-Making

Multidimensional Techniques for Strategic Decision-Making are the processes of selecting the best practical answer based on predetermined criteria and problems that frequently arise in daily life. When complicated multiple factors need to be taken into account concurrently, multidimensional techniques for strategic decision-making are considered to be the most suitable tactics for this purpose in both modern research and management practices.

Multidimensional techniques for strategic decision-making refer to the selection of most possible actions taken to reach a most suitable outcome of the already selected choices in the business world ([Jahan & Edwards, 2013](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_25)). Numerous non-commensurable and conflicting criteria frequently characterize practical problems, and no solution may satisfy all of these criteria at once. It has emerged as a powerful tool to aid such decision-making processes ([Jafari & Ehsanifar, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_24)). This section aims to explore the key principles, methods, and applications of multidimensional techniques for strategic decision-making in various fields which would aid managers and executives in decision-making processes. In order for strategic decision-making, some of the most commonly used multidimensional techniques are discussed below ([Kajanus et al., 2012](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_28); [Rudolf & Udovč, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_42)).

Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities, and Threats (SWOT) analysis is used to create and analyze significance and comparison in the given scenario whilst considering an organization's strong points, its limitation, prospects for triumph and the factors that can create hurdle in achieving the desired outcome. Additionally, each SWOT factor can be used to evaluate different decision-making options. The qualitative results of SWOT analysis can be transformed to fit the Decision Expert model, using a special technique ([Rudolf & Udovč, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_42)). Additionally, the Fuzzy Group method for multidimensional techniques for strategic decision-making can be integrated into SWOT analysis to improve its usability, as proposed in a study by Hatami-Marbini et al. in 2013. These methods enhance the SWOT analysis results and aid in prioritizing alternative strategic decisions, benefiting the overall utilization of SWOT analysis ([Sonko & Sonko, 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_47); [Hayati et al., 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_21)).

Political, Economic, Social, Technological, Legal and Environmental (PESTEL) analysis spots the outside aspects which affect performance. It helps in foreseeing future challenges and opportunities that the external environment puts forward ([Petrauskiene et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_35); [Cox, 2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_10); [Choubey & Vaidya, 2024](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_8)). The related factors are analyzed to identify opportunities and threats that a business may face. A multi-method approach, including PESTEL analysis, has been used to analyze the dimensions of sustainable healthcare waste management, as demonstrated in a study by [Thakur (2021](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_49)).

Five Forces analysis by Porter is a widely used technique which is used for analyzing the competitive environment in which an organization operates ([Birru et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_5)). It identifies key forces like viable & new competition while keeping forth the importance of consumers, contractors, and available alternates ([Anastasiu et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_2)). This analysis enables business managers to present the position of the business in the market and the limitation of the company that it might face. This analysis also helps companies to understand the nature and role of competition ([Porter, 2008](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_39); [Goyal, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_17)).

### 4.2.1 Multi-Attribute Decision Modeling (MADM)

MADM is a quantitative approach that simplifies complex decision problems by considering multiple attributes or decision criteria simultaneously. It involves evaluating and ranking alternatives based on various criteria to help choose the best-suited option among them. MADM techniques provide a structured framework for handling an array of decision factors, simplifying analyses, and drawing critical insights. MADM consists of the following core principles ([Isabelle et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_23)).

#### 4.2.1.1 Decision Matrix

The foundation of MADM involves building a decision matrix that represents the alternatives considered and their respective evaluations across different criteria or attributes.

Decision-makers use the decision matrix to study and examine the relationship of data and information for the optimal benefit of the organization and presents the information in the shape of rows and columns. It helps in presenting our choices in some certain criteria and is used to examine large size of data and information by critically evaluating the choices in order of merit ([Ding et al., 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_12)).

#### 4.2.1.2 Criteria Weighting

As not all attributes have equal importance, MADM assigns weights to represent their relative significance in the overall decision-making process. It is the most popular and straightforward MADM technique for assessing many alternatives in light of various choice criteria. The weights of the criteria in a MADM problem have been determined using an array of techniques developed by various researchers. Among these is the *Weighted Sum Method (Addition)*, which is regarded as the first and most popular approach. Later, the WSM was changed to the Weighted Product Method (Multiplication) to address some of its shortcomings ([Cinelli et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_9)).

#### 4.2.1.3 Scoring and Normalization

To make meaningful comparisons, raw data needs to be standardized through scoring and normalization techniques. One of the earliest phases in most MADM systems is the normalization process, and the employment of different normalization techniques can result in varied sequence orders of alternatives and deviations from ideal sequencing. As a result, the final result of evaluation tasks is significantly influenced by the choice of the proper normalization approach. A number of studies in literature have recognized the use of this method in the strategic decision-making process. Range of Value (ROV) method is another example of such methods, which is used to choose the best normalization technique ([Pourkhodabakhsh et al., 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_40)).

#### 4.2.1.4 Sensitivity Analysis

Finally, conducting sensitivity analyses helps determine the robustness of choice recommendations by assessing how changes in attribute weights impact final ranking outcomes. Data in MADM problems are frequently inaccurate and unchangeable. So, performing Sensitivity Analysis under the input data is an essential step in many MADM applications. Quantitative criteria's relevance is typically assessed with some mistakes. Although the sensitivity of the result can be examined by varying the parameters, if measurements are inaccurate, the result is also inaccurate ([Odu, 2019](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_32); [Sitorus & Brito-Parada, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_45)).

#### 4.2.1.5 Applications of Multi-Attribute Decision Modeling

[Dean (2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_11)) is of the view that MADM is widely applied across various domains to drive more informed decision-making. To compare the outcomes of various approaches, numerous studies have discussed and used numerous kinds of MADM procedures. Similarly, [Taherdoost and Madanchian (2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_48)) understand that for carrying out analysis, each criterion is analyzed by MADM to decide if it is an appropriate or inappropriate choice for a given application. It helps the managers in comparing the available choices as per the laid down criteria. After sorting the choices, two divisions of MADM may be established i.e., Compensatory Decision-Making and Outranking Decision Making.

##### 4.2.1.5.1 Compensatory Decision-Making

It includes the weighing of both their strong and weak aspects to improve the chances of desired outcome. One such example may be the use of Analytical Hierarchy Process (AHP), which is applied for stimulating and comparing matrices.

##### 4.2.1.5.2 Outranking Decision-Making

This method originated in France for selecting alternates which are higher in rank. ELimination and ChoiceExpressingREality (ELECTRE) is also another example used to study the orderly and ranking alternatives for solving a problem.

Some key applications include:

1.  Business: Supplier selection, performance appraisals, project prioritization
2.  Environment: Waste management policy selection, evaluating sustainable development strategies
3.  Health: Healthcare facility location planning, medical diagnosis support
4.  Government: Infrastructure investment analysis, urban planning
5.  Technology: Software evaluation and selection, system design optimization.

([Mhlanga & Lall, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_30))

## 4.3 Decision Modeling and Algorithms

Decision modeling is a process of representing complex decision-making scenarios using mathematical models and algorithms. Multiple-criteria decision-making (MCDM), also known as multiple-criteria decision analysis (MCDA) in literature, is used in operations research to evaluate various alternate choices selected upon some pre-defined criteria ([Petrauskiene et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_35)). Decision modeling techniques can be used to optimize decision-making processes in various business scenarios, such as supply chain management, strategic decision-making, and human resource management ([Phoon et al., 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_37); [Eberhard, 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_13); [Ding et al., 2023](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_12)). Organizations use decision modeling to take informed decisions while considering the available data and details instead of looking for speculations ([Cinelli et al., 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_9)).

Several types of algorithms are used in decision-making processes including decision trees, random forests, and algorithms of machine learning. Decision trees help to structure strategic decisions and help in complex decision-making environments ([Černevičienė & Kabašinskas, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_7)). Random forests combine multiple decision trees to improve the accuracy of predictions and classifications ([Arsham, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_3)). Neural networks and support vector machines are examples of machine learning algorithms that analyze large datasets and identify patterns that are not easily discernible ([Sitorus, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_45); [Mhlanga & Lall, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_30)). These algorithms can help businesses make informed decisions and stay ahead of the competition.

The use of decision modeling and algorithms in business can help organizations overcome various challenges, such as uncertainty, complexity, and ambiguity. However, the implementation of these techniques can be challenging for managers, as it requires a deep understanding of mathematical models and algorithms ([Huang, 2022](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_22)). Additionally, the use of decision modeling and algorithms may require significant investment in technology and infrastructure. Despite these challenges, the benefits of using decision modeling and algorithms in business are significant, and in order to achieve a significant advantage in the business world, organizations can benefit from these techniques and practices ([Joseph & Gaba, 2020](#ch04.xhtml#ref4_27)).

## 4.4 Conclusion

Multi-Attribute Decision Modeling offers a valuable framework to navigate through complex decision-making scenarios. By leveraging MADM techniques, decision-makers from diverse sectors can systematically evaluate and compare available alternatives while considering their respective strengths and weaknesses based on multiple criteria. As a result, MADM lays the foundation for well-informed choices that drive informed and effective decision-making processes.

# 5 Unmasking Transformations CNNs for Detecting Land Cover Changes in Satellite Imagery

Sejal Thakkar

Assistant Professor CE, Department, Indus University, Ahmedabad, Gujarat, India

Chirag Patel

Associate Professor, Charotar University of Science and Technology (CHARUSAT), Changa, Gujarat, India

Ahmed Shaikh and Divyanshi Detroja

B. Tech Students, CE Department, Indus University, Ahmedabad, Gujarat, India

Ved Suthar

Student, McMaster University, Ontario, Canada

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-5](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-5)

## 5.1 Introduction

Certain regions manifest a prominent agricultural character, characterized by the gradual conversion of forested areas into arable land over time. This dynamic evolution of land patterns necessitates a comprehensive classification scheme for a detailed analysis of land use changes. Additionally, these discernible patterns can serve as crucial indicators for anticipating forthcoming trends.

In pursuit of this objective, our proposed methodology embarks on a rigorous land cover classification endeavor within the precincts of the Meghalaya District. This endeavor leverages advanced deep learning techniques, coupled with post-classification change detection spanning a temporal domain from 2000 to 2019. Despite the historical precedence of land cover classification endeavors utilizing satellite-derived imagery, the integration of remotely sensed data remains an area fraught with inherent limitations. The amalgamation of manifold spectral, temporal, and textural attributes, coupled with the processing of time series data for classification, presents distinctive challenges.

To surmount these hurdles, features distilled from Landsat-7 sources undergo a rigorous classification process. In tandem, various spectral indices, including NDVI \[[1](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_1)\], NDWI \[[2](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_2)\], and Burn index \[[3](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_3)\], are computed to fortify the classification process. The employment of the Random Forest (RF) classifier, in conjunction with diverse band combinations, culminates in the production of a detailed land cover classification map. This map delineates five distinct classes (Barren, Burnt, Forest, Crop, and Others), attaining an average batch accuracy of 95%. Ultimately, a comprehensive change detection analysis spanning an eight-year interval has been executed, revealing substantial alterations characterized by shifts towards urbanization, expansive bare terrain, and agricultural expansion.

### 5.1.1 Introduction of Land Cover of Earth

A thorough scrutiny of land cover transformations yields multifaceted insights into agricultural land dynamics. This discourse entails an exhaustive investigation of methodologies and empirical findings central to the discernment of these alterations. Furthermore, the ramifications of land cover changes serve as a foundational framework for categorizing diverse aspects of land, encompassing, but not confined to, practices such as shifting cultivation, targeted agricultural expansion, desertification trends impacting arable terrain, and the conversion of forested regions into agricultural zones. Recent research endeavors emphasize the pivotal role of such land cover change detection in enabling data-driven prognostications and facilitating judicious decision-making processes within the realm of agricultural land analysis.

### 5.1.2 Introduction of Shifting Cultivation

Shifting cultivation, an established agricultural paradigm, entails a systematic progression involving the clearance and controlled burning of native vegetation. This is followed by the cultivation of new plots for a finite duration. Subsequent to this phase, a designated fallow period ensues, facilitating vegetation regeneration and culminating in the recommencement of the cycle. This practice is predominantly prevalent in the elevated terrains of the region. The duration of this cycle varies, ranging from 7 to 20 years. Through the utilization of multi-temporal satellite imagery, a more precise portrayal of shifting cultivators emerges, delineating their roles as both stewards and cultivators of forested areas. Projections from contemporary research affirm a sustained prevalence of this cultivation method up to the year 2090. Presently, an estimated 1.8 million individuals in northeastern India rely on this technique. Concurrently, the institutionalization of the New Land Use Policy (NLUP) is underway, aimed at the cessation of shifting cultivation practices and the promotion of alternative sustainable land-use strategies.

## 5.2 Literature Review

In the pursuit of mapping and visualizing shifting cultivation practices, there exists an ongoing endeavor marked by notable challenges in validation, primarily stemming from the impracticality of conducting on-site visits to farmlands. This limitation hinders the availability of real-time observations. Pulakesh Das et al. addressed this predicament through a comprehensive approach involving the application of diverse indices, including the burn vegetation index, burn ratio, and the relative difference of the burn ratio. These metrics were integrated into an algorithm, yielding a commendable accuracy rate of 85%. While commendably effective for mapping extensive areas, it is imperative to underscore that these methods serve a specific purpose—namely, the identification of shifting cultivation—hinging significantly on the dataset employed. Given the specialized nature of this issue, research efforts have predominantly gravitated towards elucidating dynamics across spatial and temporal dimensions. The task of detection remains relatively nascent, thus presenting an emerging challenge necessitating resolution. Noteworthy among these endeavors, N.B. Riahtam et al. harnessed the potential of LANDSAT imagery and Resourcesat-2 data to perform classification via segmentation, facilitating the inference of shifting cultivation. Subsequently, they visualized their findings over the East Garo hills of Meghalaya, India, offering a valuable contribution to the body of knowledge in this domain.

## 5.3 Challenges We Faced during Work

### 5.3.1 Various Classes for Model

In this context, we encounter two primary challenges. Firstly, the absence of a definitive framework for land coverage classification introduces a subjective element, leading to potential variations in individual conceptualizations. While this concern can be addressed by employing specific classes tailored to the precise problem under consideration, the second challenge lies in the variability of classes utilized for land cover detection. The crux of the matter revolves around determining the optimal number of classes for a comprehensive analytical approach. In the realm of vegetation mapping, it becomes imperative to consider categories like forested areas, agricultural plots, barren terrain, urban zones, aqueous bodies, and mountainous gradients to ensure precise crop land identification. Nonetheless, expanding the number of classes directly correlates with an augmented volume of input data. The data derived from satellites, even for a relatively modest area, already constitutes a substantial dataset. Further amplification may surpass the computational capacity of our systems. Thus, we address this predicament by judiciously rationalizing the number of classes, while factoring in the data's inherent quality. For example, in instances where urban areas and steep terrains are underrepresented in the dataset, amalgamating these classes may be contemplated, albeit at the expense of a marginal reduction in classification precision.

### 5.3.2 Spatial Resolution of the Data

The next critical challenge in land cover detection lies in the spatial resolution inherent to the available data. Unfortunately, the prevailing resolution is notably constrained, limiting its applicability to specific scenarios exclusively. For example, while the attainment of a successful land cover prediction is feasible, the resultant output encompasses a wide expanse, precluding the acquisition of specific details pertinent to that area. Consequently, the prospect of obtaining granular insights or effecting finely-tuned predictions within a given area using the extant data is deemed unfeasible. Instead, the viable approach entails scrutinizing irregularities in temporal data as a means to extract pertinent information.

### 5.3.3 Irregularities in the Data

Another challenge arises due to irregularities inherent in the acquired data, arising from either sensor malfunctions or natural phenomena. As satellites are human-engineered entities, there exists the occasional potential for components to fail in delivering the expected results. A notable example of this pertains to LANDSAT-7, where data acquisition was affected by missing strips resulting from the malfunction of the satellite's Scan Line Corrector on May 31, 2003. The Scan Line Corrector plays a crucial role in compensating for the satellite's forward motion during its orbit, and its failure led to the adoption of a zigzag ground track, deviating from the intended linear trajectory \[[4](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_4),[5](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_5),[6](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_6),[7](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_7)\]. Moreover, the data may be subject to noise introduced by environmental elements, such as adverse weather conditions, cloud cover, and the obscuration of terrain features due to shadows cast by mountains. These variables serve to compound the challenge of harnessing the data effectively for model training.

## 5.4 A Burning Issue Shifting Cultivation – A Jhumm Farming

### 5.4.1 Introduction

Shifting cultivation, also referred to as “jhumming” in India or slash-and-burn cultivation, embodies an agricultural methodology distinguished by its iterative land use pattern. This approach entails the recurrent cultivation of land until soil fertility reaches a diminished state, at which juncture the plot is either temporarily left fallow or the extant vegetation is subjected to cutting or burning. While typically undertaken at the individual level, communal adoption of such practices is also observable within entire villages. The incendiary facet of this technique serves the dual purpose of mitigating undesirable agricultural residues and suppressing weed proliferation, effectively converting them into a nutrient-enriched ash. This residual ash, in turn, serves to replenish soil fertility for subsequent agricultural cycles. Additionally, the controlled burn process aids in the elimination of pests and pathogens present within the soil matrix.

### 5.4.2 Impact of Shifting Cultivation on Earth and Environment

This cultivation method is associated with a significant drawback in terms of land utilization efficiency. Once a tract of land undergoes the fallow phase, its potential for alternative use is greatly diminished. Typically, forested areas are converted into arable land, subsequently subject to shifting cultivation practices. This exacerbates soil infertility and initiates soil erosion processes. The reduction of fallow productivity periods narrows the window for forest regeneration, resulting in the gradual replacement of mature forest with secondary, nascent growth. The practice of field clearance through slash-and-burn cultivation further hinders the rejuvenation of evergreen forests, leading to pronounced soil erosion and a decline in overall agricultural productivity.

### 5.4.3 Shifting Cultivation in Meghalaya and Various Districts of India

Shifting cultivation remains a prominent agricultural practice in northeastern India, notably in the West Garo Hills district. Recent empirical studies have unveiled a significant forest cover, accounting for approximately 79% of this district's total area. Within this framework, the accompanying land use map provides an overview indicating that 30% of the district encompasses either actively cultivated plots, areas earmarked for shifting cultivation, or designated for wet rice farming. It is worth noting that these regions exhibit sparse tree density and a substantial crown cover. The ensuing tabular representation illustrates similar conditions observed across various states within India.

## 5.5 Gathered Dataset Information

The dataset, provided in .tif format, was systematically procured through the Google Earth Engine platform, employing the following defined parameters.

The dataset comprises atmospherically corrected surface reflectance values obtained from the Landsat 7 ETM+ sensor. These images encompass four bands capturing visible and near-infrared (VNIR) wavelengths, alongside two bands sensitive to short-wave infrared (SWIR) frequencies. All bands have undergone processing for orthorectified surface reflectance. Additionally, a solitary thermal infrared (TIR) band has been processed to yield orthorectified brightness temperature readings. The VNIR and SWIR bands exhibit a spatial resolution of 30 meters per pixel. While initially collected at a resolution of 120 meters per pixel (60 meters per pixel for Landsat 7), the TIR band has been resampled using cubic convolution to attain a 30-meter resolution.

These datasets have undergone rigorous atmospheric correction procedures through the utilization of LEDAPS. Additionally, they encompass masks for cloud, shadow, water, and snow, meticulously generated using CFMASK, complemented by a per-pixel saturation mask.

Accumulated data strips are systematically organized into overlapping “scenes,” with each scene covering an approximate area of 170 kilometers by 183 kilometers, meticulously adhering to a standardized reference grid \[[8](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_8),[9](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_9),[10](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_10),[11](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_11),[12](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_12)\].

Satellite: LANDSAT 7

Region: \[89, 25, 93, 26.2\]

Type: No cloud cover

Interval: 6 months

Range: 2000 to 2019

RGB bands used: Band 1,2,3

Other bands used: Band 4,5,7

Resolution: 30 meters/pixel

## 5.6 Implementation

### 5.6.1 Data Format Conversion

The initial data is supplied in the TIFF format, necessitating a preprocessing step prior to integration into the machine learning model. This preprocessing involves the transformation of the raw TIFF data into distinct representations, specifically, RGB (Red, Green, Blue) and IR (Infrared) images, leveraging designated bands. This procedure provides two notable advantages. Firstly, the resultant PNG images enhance visualization capabilities, thereby facilitating more intuitive interpretation. Secondly, the PNG image format permits seamless loading into Python as an array list, thereby streamlining subsequent processing stages.

### 5.6.2 Data Splitting

Given the substantial volume of amassed data, direct incorporation into the training dataset may engender inefficiencies. Thus, imperative data preprocessing measures are mandated to curtail the magnitude of the training dataset inputted into the model. Subsequently, these images undergo requisite formatting procedures. The resultant visualization images are partitioned into segments measuring 3 kilometers by 3 kilometers. Each segmented image is assigned a distinctive identifier, complemented by a timestamp affixed to the ID, expediting facile retrieval of region-specific images across disparate timeframes ([Figure 5.1](#ch05.xhtml#fig5_1)).

![Figure 5.1 Sample RGB image.](images/fig5_1.jpg)

[Figure 5.1 Sample RGB image.](#ch05.xhtml#Ffig5_1)

### 5.6.3 Visual Changes over Time on Images

Further, the difference between two images with the same area and consecutive time stamp were used to make a delta / change image, to visualize the changes in the period of 6 months over a particular area ([Figure 5.2](#ch05.xhtml#fig5_2)).

![Figure 5.2 Changes over 6 months.](images/fig5_2.jpg)

[Figure 5.2 Changes over 6 months.](#ch05.xhtml#Ffig5_2)

### 5.6.4 Preprocessing

The initial stage in data processing involved the application of the Normalized Difference Water Index (NDWI) \[[2](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_2)\] to eliminate water bodies from the image. Subsequently, the Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) \[[1](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_1)\] was computed, yielding an index value indicative of vegetation presence for each retained pixel. The Burn Index \[[3](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_3)\] was subsequently employed to discern areas affected by combustion, signifying regions where vegetation has been subjected to burning, whether by natural or anthropogenic factors. This sequential procedure was systematically implemented across the entire dataset. Leveraging a modest subset of labeled data, the processed imagery underwent training via a deep neural network, culminating in the generation of a model boasting an impressive accuracy rate of 95%.

### 5.6.5 Visualization and Analysis

A supplementary software application was developed to facilitate the visualization of predicted data, enabling the concurrent display of images alongside their corresponding model-generated predictions.

Subsequent to achieving a high level of accuracy through model training and implementing a dedicated visualization tool, an additional program was engineered to conduct in-depth analysis of multi-temporal data. This program empowers us to systematically examine temporal variations and discern the evolving land cover dynamics on an annual basis for a designated area of interest.

# 6 Leafine An AI Tool to Recognize and Perceive Leaf Illness with Manure Suggestions

S. Prince Sahaya Brighty, M Brindha, and R. Anuradha

Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Sri Ramakrishna Engineering College, Coimbatore, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-6](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-6)

## 6.1 Introduction

Leaf disease is something that can decisively diminish the yield of harvests, especially in field Yields. With a quickly growing population, finding, and diagnosing beginning phase leaf illness is vital to accomplish the greatest yields. Plant illness accounts for around 16% of worldwide reap-yield misfortunes. In this article, we will investigate two profound CNN models, YOLOv8 and ResNeXt50, for crop leaf disease detection. Both models are powerful deep-learning techniques that have shown promising outcomes in image acknowledgment, segmentation, and classification tasks. In addition to distinguishing leaf illnesses, it is essential to take preventive measures to control the spread of the disease. One method for forestalling the spread of leaf illnesses is using manure. Manure is a characteristic compost that gives fundamental supplements to crops and plants, making them better and more impervious to illness. Involving compost correctly can assist the spread of diseases and further develop crop yield. Leafine, a deep learning-based leaf disease detection and manure recommendation system, is designed to be versatile enough to detect and diagnose leaf diseases in various types of plants and crops, not just centered around a specific yield assortment. Furthermore, the novelty of Leafine is that it recommends fertilization suggestions through well-suited compost item interfaces, YouTube care guides, and home remedy excrements that suit both harvest and blooming plants ([Figure 6.1.](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_1)).

![Figure 6.1 Overview of Leafine.](images/fig6_1.jpg)

[Figure 6.1 Overview of Leafine.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_1)

Different illnesses that emerge in most plants are listed below in [Figure 6.2](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_2).

![Figure 6.2 Common problems that emerge in leaves.](images/fig6_2.jpg)

[Figure 6.2 Common problems that emerge in leaves.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_2)

From the above diseases, Leafine focuses on the most common problems ([Figure 6.3](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_3)) that occur in plants which incorporate powdery mildew, bacterial blight, leaf spots, and nitrogen deficiency. Powdery mildew is a kind of fungal disease characterized by the appearance of a powdery gray or white coating on the stems and foliage of infected plants. Leaf spot is a disease caused by fungi, bacteria, or viruses. Bacterial blight can be of dry dark spots, dry leaves, dying branches, leaves and flowers. It is usually spread by rain or wind and may be transmitted by insects. Nitrogen deficiency is an abiotic disorder causing growth inhibition. Plants need nitrogen for leaf growth, so lack of this nutrient causes yellowing.

![Figure 6.3 Leafine focuses on the above-mentioned leaf problems.](images/fig6_3.jpg)

[Figure 6.3 Leafine focuses on the above-mentioned leaf problems.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_3)

Therefore, this project aims to use YOLOv8 and ResNext50 algorithms for leaf disease detection and to provide suggestions for the appropriate use of manure to prevent the spread of diseases in crops. By utilizing these two techniques, Leafine is an effective system for detecting and preventing leaf diseases in plants and crops. The exhibition accomplished in this work outperforms the current cutting-edge works in this domain. Moreover, Leafine is intended to be user-friendly, making it simple for ranchers, groundskeepers, and other agricultural enthusiasts to utilize our system and get precise illness conclusions and manure proposals. This might assist with decreasing harvest misfortunes, save time and exertion in manual disease discovery, and further improve overall harvest yield and quality.

## 6.2 Related Works

The most pressing challenge for ranchers is to control plant-arranged afflictions. Regardless, the division of a cleared-out leaf picture is leaned to convey disproportionate enlightenment and a confused composite environment. This section examines the recently evolved characterization models equipped for distinguishing leaf illnesses and dissecting their efficiency.

The deep learning models classify different types of tomato plant diseases \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1),[2](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_2)\] using plain and segmented leaf images. The modified U-net segmentation model showed high accuracy for leaf segmentation. Consideration of environmental conditions from a plant's surrounding and encompassing areas can shrink the number of false positives while training the models. One impediment of these references is that they center just on tomato plant infections and do not investigate the utilization of the proposed strategy to other plant species.

Characterizing plant illnesses utilizing the Inception Net CNN architecture \[[3](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_3),[4](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_4)\] which incorporates InceptionV3, InceptionResnetV2, and MobileNetV2 is compelling and outperforms the ongoing profound learning methods. The altered and modified version of U-net architecture was found to be predominant in isolating leaf images from the background, and the InceptionNet1 architecture was prevalent in eliminating high-precision highlights from the images and demanding less investment for training. Designing a featherlight \[[5](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_5),[6](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_6)\] and computationally powerful convolutional neural network (CNN) for leave illness classification. Improving the performance of conventional CNN by incorporating attention mechanisms. Reduced network parameters and complexity lead to produce better recognition precision and make it a promising methodology for crop infection distinguishing.

The deep Forest algorithm \[[7](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_7)\] limits the adverse impacts of plant illness on agricultural produce, which is vital for taking care of the world and supporting the economies of emerging countries. Early identification of plant illnesses utilizing NASNet architecture \[[8](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_8)\] assists the leaves with various varieties with infection area and status in the plants and makes enormous contrasts among survival and annihilation of harvests in farmlands impacted by these plant sicknesses.

The restructured residual dense network model \[[9](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_9)\] can acquire huge upgrades over a large portion of the best-in-class models in crop leaf identification, as well as requiring less computation to accomplish high performance to enhance the progression of data and gradients. Also, a blend of shallow CNN \[[10](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_10)\] and classic machine learning calculations are used for identifying plant illness.

Data augmentation and transfer learning strategies \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11),[12](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_12)\] can be applied to decrease the possibility of overfitting. Performance assessment of the critical factors affecting the fine-tuning of pre-trained models, including iteration, and batch size for further developing the accuracy.

Our proposed system uses recent renditions of ultralytics Yolov8 and ResNext50. Yolov8 is reframed with the addition of ConvModule layers in the backbone layer of the darknet CNN layer, to get more precise identification of illnesses in the leaf. The models were trained with different epochs and sizes of the batch. Both models enable to identify the plant disease classification in a more precise and quicker way and also achieve higher precision. To check out the robustness of our models, we utilized various organizations of pictures, to be normal RGB, Segmented, and Grayscale pictures. The Results showed that our carried-out model accomplished better precision and required acquisition and grounding time.

## 6.3 Methodology

### 6.3.1 Preprocessing on Leafine Dataset

The proposed model is assessed utilizing the custom plant dataset comprising 4,245 pictures of different assortments of plant pathogen leaves, some samples of which are shown in [Figure 6.4](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_4). The assortment of datasets is finished with custom handpicking of leaf pictures, and some through the famous datasets Plant Pathology Picture Chronicle and Fungi Net. There are three configurations of images in our dataset to be specific, normal RGB, greyscale and segmented images to improve the robustness of our models. The images from the dataset were divided based on five common problem classes. The entirety of the images was isolated into five classes, one of which was healthy and others flagged as diseased which incorporates Blight, Leafspot, Powdery mildew, and Nitrogen deficiency.

![Figure 6.4 Some random leaf images from the Leafine custom dataset.](images/fig6_4.jpg)

[Figure 6.4 Some random leaf images from the Leafine custom dataset.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_4)

#### 6.3.1.1 Image Rescaling, Resizing and Normalization

In general, ResNeXt50 models take input images of size 224×224 or 256×256. Some implementations support a larger input size of 512×512 which is also supported by the Yolov8 model. So, the images of our Leafine dataset are resized to 512×512. The preprocessing starts with the manual annotation of five-classification diseases involved in Leafine. The type of normalization that can be done for images is to transform a picture into a PyTorch tensor. At this point, the pixel values are scaled somewhere in the range of 0.0 and 1.0. Envision pictures and plot pixel values. Applying the mean and standard deviation (SD) of the images of the dataset, z-score standardization was utilized to normalize the images.

#### 6.3.1.2 Image Augmentation

Our dataset is balanced with a similar number of images for five different categories, an unbalanced dataset may lead to producing a biased model, overfitting, or underfitting issues. An equivalent number of images in every one of the projects (balance dataset) can train a solid model to give better execution precision. The three augmentation techniques (image vertical rotation, image horizontal rotation, picture scaling, and picture translation) were used to adjust the training data. Random brightness contrast and hue-saturation values were also applied to our dataset.

### 6.3.2 Experiments on Integrated Deep CNN Models

#### 6.3.2.1 YOLOv8

YOLOv8 is more proficient than past renditions of YOLO since it utilizes a larger feature map and a more effective convolutional network. This permits the model to identify objects in a more precise and quicker way. With a bigger feature map, the model can catch more perplexing connections between various highlights and can more readily perceive and recognize patterns and objects in the data. Moreover, in YOLOv8, the amount of time it takes to prepare the model can assist with lessening overfitting.

In the backbone section of YOLOv8 architecture, the Stem Layer has been updated with two additional ConvModules which led to efficient neural network architecture ([Figure 6.5](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_5).). The number of channels in the ConvModule is increased to get more precise detections as the output. The Leafine dataset was trained utilizing our improved YOLOv8 model which brings about the exactness of a 0.9% increment in the accuracy of the ultralytics YOLOv8 model. Saliency map segments are acquired by normalizing the inclinations and gradients that feature the main regions of the leaf picture for the prediction that is generally indicative of disease.

![Figure 6.5 Improved YOLOv8 architecture with slight modification in the stem layer of the backbone.](images/fig6_5.jpg)

[Figure 6.5 Improved YOLOv8 architecture with slight modification in the stem layer of the backbone.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_5)

![Figure 6.6 Input images, saliency segmented mask and detections using YOLOv8 model.](images/fig6_6.jpg)

Figure 6.6 Input images, saliency segmented mask and detections using YOLOv8 model.

#### 6.3.2.2 ResNeXt-50

ResNeXt-50 employs a parallel architecture \[[13](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_13)\] with multiple pathways of different cardinalities, to capture multi-scale features effectively. Each pathway consists of a series of convolutional layers with different numbers of groups. The key innovation in ResNeXt is the utilization of a “cardinality“ boundary, which controls the number of independent pathways through each gathering of convolutional layers. By expanding the cardinality, ResNeXt can catch more assorted feature representations without fundamentally expanding the number of boundaries or calculations required. In ResNeXt-50-32x4d, the 50 convolutional layers are coordinated into a progression of blocks, each one of which comprises a few convolutional layers with clump and batch standardization and ReLU activation capabilities. ResNeXt-50-32x4d is a specific variation of the ResNeXt-50 design, where “32x4d“ alludes to the number of groups and the number of channels in each gathering, respectively. A slight modification is done in the Bottleneck layer which is found in each pathway consisting of a 1×1 convolutional layer followed by a 3×3 convolutional layer; this approach reduces the number of channels in the intermediate layers and allows for faster training and inference.

![Figure 6.7 ResNeXt-50 architecture.](images/fig6_7.jpg)

Figure 6.7 ResNeXt-50 architecture.

#### 6.3.2.3 Conception Techniques

The headway of representation approaches has originated from a developing interest in the internal mechanics of CNNs and the rationale that drives the decision-making capabilities of a network. By delivering a more visual understanding of CNN's decision-making processes, visualization techniques enable overall interpretation. This likewise improves model lucidity by showing the purpose for the thinking in a structure that humans promptly comprehend, increasing faith in the neural network's predictions.

By analyzing the heatmap delivered by Grad-CAM, one can distinguish the specific regions of the leaf image that the model is utilizing to make a decision. Grad-CAM for leaf disease detection, first and foremost prepared with a profound CNN model (ResNeXt) to characterize and classify leaf images. Then, at that point, for a given image, the model's output is obtained and the inclination of the target class score concerning the element guides of the last convolutional layer is determined. This creates a weight lattice, which is then used to deliver a heatmap that highlights the regions of the leaf image that are generally significant for the model's characterization decision ([Figure 6.8](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_8).).

![Figure 6.8 Accurate classification and visualization using Grad-cam utilizing ResNext-50 model. The darker spots on the second image (right) indicate the severity of the lesion.](images/fig6_8.jpg)

[Figure 6.8 Accurate classification and visualization using Grad-cam utilizing ResNext-50 model. The darker spots on the second image (right) indicate the severity of the lesion.](#ch06.xhtml#Ffig6_8)

## 6.4 Results

### 6.4.1 Performance Metrics for Classification

The Confusion Matrix is divided into four sections: true positives, true negatives, false positives, and false negatives. We can compute different supportive measurements utilizing this matrix (See [Figure 6.9](#ch06.xhtml#fig6_9).). Here, genuine positive is the quantity of accurately grouped healthy leaf images and genuine negative is the quantity of accurately arranged undesirable leaf pictures. The misclassified healthy and diseased leaf photos are labeled as Misleading positive and False negative, respectively.

*A**c**c**u**r**a**c**y* = (*T**P*+*T**N*)/(*T**P*+*T**N*+*F**P*+*F**N*) (6.1)

![Figure 6.9 Confusion matrix for healthy and diseased leaf classification.](images/fig6_9.jpg)

## 6.5 Conclusion and Future Scope

One of the key contributions in our system implemented with the most accurate and minimum load time models is forming with the integration of significant suggestions, cures, illness management, and consultations with experts, subsequently guaranteeing exceptional harvest yields. Our proposed model is lightweight so farmers and anthophiles could introduce it on any smart gadget with computerized camera capabilities. The future extent of the framework incorporates considering the environmental parameters like humidity, temperature, pH of the soil, and pot size and working out how much water to be provided each day consistently with reminder systems for monitoring the plants. Furthermore, preparing the model on a bigger dataset of images and incorporating different types of data such as spectral data and chemical investigations to further develop disease diagnosis. By integrating new advancements and extending the scope of our system, it can turn into a much more useful asset for present-day horticulture management. We trust that this strategy will significantly impact the perspectives and prescient development in leaf disease prediction domain research in different disciplines.
