# 7 An Expansive Performance Analysis and Comparison between Different Supervised and Unsupervised ML Algorithms for Categorization of ICU Patients at an Indian Hospital

Amrita Ghosh and Partha Das

Independent Research Scholar, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Subrata Jana

Research Scholar, Department of Mathematics, Jadavpur University, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Bibhas Chandra. Giri

Professor of Mathematics, Jadavpur University, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Anirban Sarkar

Professor and Head, Department Management and Marketing, West Bengal State University, Barasat, West Bengal, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-7](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-7)

## 7.1 Introduction

The high cost of intensive care necessitates careful decision-making regarding when to transfer patients to lower-level care units. However, discharging patients from the intensive care unit (ICU) too soon can pose risks such as insufficient monitoring and limited intervention timelines. These risks can lead to avoidable clinical deterioration, increased mortality and morbidity, and higher healthcare expenses.

To address these challenges, leveraging accurate predictive decision-making and machine learning analysis using real-world data can be a valuable, cost-effective solution in healthcare. By analyzing the patient's condition after transfer from the ICU, hospitals can gain insights into the effectiveness of their post-ICU care.

This study focuses on adult patients in the Intensive Care Unit in Kolkata, India. Particularly, this study aimed to make “An expansive performance analysis and comparison between different Supervised and Unsupervised ML algorithms for categorization of Kolkata's Hospital ICU patients.”

## 7.2 Objectives

This study emphasizes the evaluation and comparison of the performance of data mining methods on a hospital ICU database.

*   This paper aims to build ML models that predict patients' outcomes in terms of three categories: 
    1.  Continuing in ICU
    2.  Discharge/shifting in General bed
    3.  Mortality cases
*   Performance analysis between different supervised learning algorithms and same for unsupervised learning algorithms is also aimed to achieve in this paper.

## 7.3 Background Concepts

The field of Machine Learning (ML) is generating considerable excitement in the realm of Artificial Intelligence (AI), with numerous companies aiming to harness its potential for their specific needs \[[1](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_1)\]. ML is gaining popularity as it employs algorithms to facilitate learning from data, making it applicable in various domains such as business and healthcare. The healthcare industry is constantly evolving, driven by advancements in technology and innovative ideas. ML has the potential to aid medical professionals in navigating these new scenarios. Advanced technology available today enables the extraction of valuable insights from unstructured text, which was previously a challenging task on a large scale. With this newfound wealth of intelligence derived from ML, physicians and administrators can make well-informed decisions in a timely manner, benefiting both patient care and operational programs that impact millions of lives.

[Figure 7.1](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_1) illustrates the diverse range of intelligent and compassionate features associated with the ML culture in healthcare. These features encompass the support provided by intelligent tools such as AI and cloud-based data processing specific to healthcare services. The generation of electronic medical records is particularly noteworthy as it significantly aids the healthcare field, even at a reasonable cost \[[2](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_2)\]. ML principles extend their quality services to various impactful areas, including the creation of smart reports, digital note-taking and efficient record management. These contributions showcase the effectiveness of ML in enhancing the delivery of healthcare services.

![Figure 7.1 Machine learning in healthcare services.](images/fig7_1.jpg)

[Figure 7.1 Machine learning in healthcare services.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_1)

In this paper we have used Random Forest (RF), AdaBoost, Bagging, Boosting, Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, KNN for Supervised Learning algorithm and for Unsupervised Learning Algorithm, K-Means Clustering and Gaussian Mixture model are used.

**Supervised Learning Algorithm**: Supervised learning, as its name implies, involves the presence of a supervisor or teacher. In supervised learning, we train the machine using labeled data, which means the data is already tagged with the correct answers. The process begins by providing the machine with a set of labeled examples, which serves as the training data. The supervised learning algorithm analyzes this training data and learns from it to generate accurate predictions or outcomes when presented with new, unlabeled examples of data.

### 7.3.1 Decision Tree

The Decision Tree is a supervised learning technique that can be applied to both classification and regression problems, although it is commonly used for solving classification problems. It functions as a tree-based classifier, where the internal nodes represent the features or attributes of a dataset, the branches represent the decision rules, and each leaf node represents the outcome or prediction. In a Decision Tree, there are two main types of nodes: the Decision Nodes and the Leaf Nodes. Decision nodes are used to make decisions based on the features of the given dataset and have multiple branches, each representing a possible outcome or decision. On the other hand, leaf nodes are the final outputs of those decisions and do not contain any further branches. The decision-making process in a Decision Tree is based on the features of the dataset. Starting from the root node, which represents the initial decision, the tree expands with branches that correspond to different decisions or tests based on the features. This process continues until it reaches the leaf nodes, which provide the final predictions or outcomes. The structure of the Decision Tree resembles that of a tree, hence the name ([Figure 7.2](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_2)). Overall, the Decision Tree algorithm offers a visual and intuitive representation of decision-making based on the features of the dataset, making it a popular choice for solving classification problems.

![Figure 7.2 Decision tree.](images/fig7_2.jpg)

[Figure 7.2 Decision tree.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_2)

### 7.3.2 Random Forest

Random Forest ([Figure 7.3](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_3).) is a widely used machine learning algorithm that falls under the category of supervised learning techniques. It is applicable to both classification and regression problems in the field of machine learning. The name “Random Forest“ stems from its nature as an ensemble classifier, consisting of multiple decision trees trained on different subsets of the given dataset \[[3](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_3)\]. The algorithm enhances predictive accuracy by considering the average prediction from all the individual decision trees in the random forest. Rather than relying on a single decision tree, Random Forest combines the predictions from each tree in the forest and determines the final output based on majority voting. This approach improves the robustness and accuracy of the predictions. Additionally, the inclusion of a larger number of trees in the forest further enhances accuracy while mitigating the risk of over fitting, which refers to the model's tendency to overly adapt to the training data and perform poorly on new, unseen data. In summary, Random Forest is an ensemble classifier that leverages the predictions of multiple decision trees to make more accurate predictions. It offers improved accuracy compared to individual decision trees and helps prevent over fitting by considering the majority vote of predictions from a diverse set of trees.

![Figure 7.3 Random forest.](images/fig7_3.jpg)

[Figure 7.3 Random forest.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_3)

### 7.3.3 Bagging

Bagging ([Figure 7.4](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_4)) is also known as Bootstrap aggregating. It consists of two steps: bootstrapping and aggregation.

![Figure 7.4 Bagging.](images/fig7_4.jpg)

[Figure 7.4 Bagging.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_4)

#### 7.3.3.1 Bootstrapping

The process of resampling subsets of data with replacement from an initial dataset is a key component of the bootstrap method. In this approach, subsets of data, known as bootstrapped datasets or bootstraps, are created by randomly selecting data points from the original dataset. The term “with replacement“ means that an individual data point can be selected multiple times and included in multiple bootstrapped datasets. Each of these bootstrapped datasets is then used to train a weak learner, which is a model or algorithm that may not perform well individually but contributes to the overall predictive power when combined with other weak learners. By training weak learners on different bootstrapped datasets, the ensemble model can capture various patterns and characteristics present in the data. The purpose of resampling with replacement and training weak learners on bootstrapped datasets is to create a diverse set of models that collectively make more accurate predictions. Each weak learner contributes its own unique perspective on the data, and combining their predictions helps to mitigate biases and improve overall performance.

#### 7.3.3.2 Aggregating

Each weak learner in the ensemble is trained independently and generates its own predictions. These predictions are then combined or aggregated to produce the final prediction. The aggregation process can be done through either max voting or averaging. Max voting involves selecting the most commonly predicted class, while averaging calculates the average value of the predictions. The purpose of this aggregation is to leverage the diversity and collective intelligence of the weak learners, leading to an improved overall prediction.

#### 7.3.3.3 Max Voting

This approach is frequently employed in classification problems, where it aims to determine the most frequent prediction, known as the mode. It is referred to as voting due to its similarity to the concept of majority rule in elections. Each individual model in the ensemble generates its own prediction, which is treated as a single “vote.” The prediction that occurs most frequently among the models is selected as the representative for the combined model.

#### 7.3.3.4 Averaging

When dealing with regression problems, averaging is a commonly employed technique. It entails calculating the mean or average of the individual predictions generated by the ensemble models. This average value is then considered as the overall prediction for the combined model.

### 7.3.4 Logistic Regression

Logistic regression, a popular machine learning algorithm, falls under the category of supervised learning. It is specifically designed for predicting a categorical dependent variable based on a given set of independent variables. The primary purpose of logistic regression is to predict the output of a categorical dependent variable. This means that the outcome must be in a categorical or discrete form, such as Yes or No, 0 or 1, or true or false. Instead of providing exact values of 0 or 1, logistic regression produces probabilistic values ranging between 0 and 1. While logistic regression shares similarities with linear regression, its usage differs. Linear regression is employed for solving regression problems, whereas logistic regression is specifically used for addressing classification problems. In logistic regression, instead of fitting a regression line, we utilize an “S”-shaped logistic function that predicts two maximum values, typically 0 or 1. The curve generated by the logistic function indicates the likelihood of a particular event, such as determining whether cells are cancerous or not based on certain factors, or whether a mouse is obese or not based on its weight. Logistic regression is a significant machine learning algorithm because it can provide probabilities and effectively classify new data, using both continuous and discrete datasets. Moreover, logistic regression can be applied to classify observations using various types of data, and it can easily identify the most influential variables for classification purposes. [Figure 7.5](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_5) illustrates the logistic function.

![Figure 7.5 Logistic regression.](images/fig7_5.jpg)

[Figure 7.5 Logistic regression.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_5)

### 7.3.5 KNN

The K-nearest neighbors (KNN) algorithm ([Figure 7.7](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_7)) is a supervised machine learning technique that can be applied to both classification and regression tasks. While it can be used for regression, it is primarily employed for classification purposes in various industries \[[4](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_4)\]. The KNN algorithm exhibits two key characteristics:

![Figure 7.6 Scattered data points.](images/fig7_6.jpg)

[Figure 7.6 Scattered data points.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_6)

![Figure 7.7 KNN.](images/fig7_7.jpg)

[Figure 7.7 KNN.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_7)

Lazy learning algorithm: KNN is considered a lazy learning algorithm because it lacks a distinct training phase. Instead, it utilizes all available data points during the classification process.

Non-parametric learning algorithm: KNN is classified as a non-parametric learning algorithm due to its lack of assumptions about the underlying data. It does not make any assumptions regarding the distribution or characteristics of the dataset.

In summary, the K-nearest neighbors algorithm is a versatile supervised learning technique used for classification and regression tasks. It is characterized as a lazy learning algorithm since it uses all available data for classification, and it is regarded as a non-parametric learning algorithm as it does not make assumptions about the underlying data distribution.

The following is an example to understand the concept of K and working of KNN algorithm.

Suppose we have a dataset which can be plotted as seen in [Figure 7.6](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_6):

Now, we need to classify new data point with black dot (at point 60, 60) into blue or red class. We are assuming K = 3 i.e., it would find three nearest data points. It is shown in the next diagram.

We can see in the above diagram the three nearest neighbors of the data point with black dot. Among those three, two of them lies in Red class hence the black dot will also be assigned to red class.

#### 7.3.5.1 Unsupervised Learning Algorithm

Unsupervised learning involves training a machine without the use of classified or labeled information, enabling the algorithm to process and act upon unstructured data independently. In this context, the machine's objective is to organize and group unsorted data based on similarities, patterns, and differences, without any pre-existing knowledge or training on the data.

### 7.3.6 K-Means Clustering

K-Means Clustering is an example of an Unsupervised Learning algorithm used to group an unlabeled dataset into distinct clusters. The value of K specifies the predetermined number of clusters to be created during the process. For instance, if K=2, there will be two clusters, and for K=3, there will be three clusters, and so on.

This algorithm enables the automatic discovery of groups or categories within an unlabeled dataset, without the need for any prior training. It achieves this by clustering the data into different groups based on inherent similarities, patterns, or characteristics.

K-Means Clustering operates as a centroid-based algorithm, where each cluster is associated with a centroid point. The primary objective of this algorithm is to minimize the sum of distances between each data point and its corresponding cluster's centroid \[[5](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_5)\].

The algorithm takes the unlabeled dataset as input, divides it into K clusters, and iteratively refines the clusters until the optimal clustering arrangement is achieved. It is important to determine the value of K before running the algorithm.

The k-means clustering algorithm performs two main tasks:

1.  Determines the optimal positions for the K centroids through an iterative process.
2.  Assigns each data point to the closest centroid, forming clusters based on their proximity.

As a result, each cluster consists of data points sharing commonalities and being distinct from other clusters.

[Figure 7.8](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_8) explains the working of the K-means Clustering Algorithm:

![Figure 7.8 K-Means clustering.](images/fig7_8.jpg)

[Figure 7.8 K-Means clustering.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_8)

### 7.3.7 Gaussian Mixture Model

Gaussian mixture models (GMM) provide a probabilistic approach to model real-world datasets. GMMs extend the concept of Gaussian distributions and can effectively represent datasets that exhibit multiple underlying Gaussian distributions. In this context, a Gaussian mixture model is a probabilistic model that assumes data points are generated from a combination of unknown Gaussian distributions. GMMs are commonly used for clustering, which involves grouping data points into distinct clusters.

GMMs offer the ability to identify clusters in datasets where the clusters may not be clearly defined. They also allow for estimating the probability of a new data point belonging to each cluster. Moreover, GMMs exhibit robustness to outliers, meaning that they can still provide accurate results even in the presence of data points that do not neatly fit into any specific cluster. This characteristic makes GMMs a flexible and powerful tool for clustering data.

Conceptually, a Gaussian mixture model represents a probabilistic model where Gaussian distributions are assumed for each group, with parameters defined by mean vectors (μ) and covariance matrices (Σ). It is important to note that a Gaussian distribution is a continuous probability distribution that follows a bell-shaped curve, also known as the normal distribution \[[6](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_6)\].

[Figure 7.9](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_9) shows an example of Gaussian mixture models:

![Figure 7.9 Gaussian mixture model.](images/fig7_9.jpg)

[Figure 7.9 Gaussian mixture model.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_9)

Gaussian mixture models (GMM) have diverse applications, including density estimation, clustering, and image segmentation. In terms of density estimation, GMMs can estimate the probability density function of a dataset. When it comes to clustering, GMMs can group data points that originate from the same Gaussian distribution. Lastly, for image segmentation, GMMs can partition an image into distinct regions.

The versatility of Gaussian mixture models extends to various use cases, such as identifying customer segments, detecting fraudulent activity, and clustering images. In each of these scenarios, GMMs excel at uncovering clusters within the data that may not be immediately evident. Consequently, Gaussian mixture models represent a powerful tool for data analysis and should be considered for any clustering task.

### 7.3.8 PCA

Principal Component Analysis (PCA) is a widely used unsupervised learning method that effectively reduces the dimensionality of data. By doing so, it enhances interpretability while minimizing the loss of information. PCA plays a crucial role in identifying the most important features within a dataset and simplifies the process of visualizing the data in 2D or 3D.

Through PCA, a sequence of linear combinations of variables is derived, enabling a comprehensive understanding of the underlying patterns and relationships in the data \[[7](#ch07.xhtml#ref7_7)\].

In [Figure 7.10](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_10), multiple points are displayed on a two-dimensional plane. The representation includes two principal components. PC1 represents the primary principal component, which accounts for the highest variance observed in the data. PC2, on the other hand, is an additional principal component that is perpendicular or orthogonal to PC1.

![Figure 7.10 PCA.](images/fig7_10.jpg)

[Figure 7.10 PCA.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_10)

## 7.4 Data Collection and Preparation

This categorized model performed on the multi parameters of 18 or above aged ICU patients' database, collected from Indian hospitals.

The raw data is prepared as well labeled data for supervised learning and also prepare as unlabeled data for unsupervised learning.

The number of original data consists of 4783 rows and 18 columns. See [Figure 7.11](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_11).

![Figure 7.11 Original data set.](images/fig7_11.jpg)

[Figure 7.11 Original data set.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_11)

After cleaning ([Figure 7.12](#ch07.xhtml#fig7_12)) all the null values the actual data become 4,575 rows and 3 columns which are ‘A4’, ‘PMR’, and ‘HOSP\_OUTCOME’. Based on these the Supervised and Unsupervised learning algorithms are performed.

![Figure 7.12 Cleaned data set.](images/fig7_12.jpg)

[Figure 7.12 Cleaned data set.](#ch07.xhtml#Ffig7_12)

## 7.5 Methodology

70% prepared data are trained and 30% prepared data are tested as whole for Supervised Learning algorithm. After that different Supervised Learning algorithms named: Decision Tree, Random Forest, Bagging, Logistic Regression, KNN are used to classify the data into three categories:

1.  Continuing in ICU
2.  Discharge/shifting in General bed
3.  Mortality cases

After that accuracy score is calculated for each of the algorithm and AUROC curve is drawn based on the accuracy score to compare their performance. Next confusion and performance matrix are calculated for the vast performance of the analysis regarding categorization of data.

In the same way Unsupervised Algorithm like K-Means clustering, Gaussian Mixture model and PCA are applied and all the previous steps are performed except data training and data testing, which are not required in Unsupervised Learning Algorithm. Clustering images show the performance of these Unsupervised Algorithms.

## 7.8 Conclusion and Future Scope

Machine learning (ML) has become an invaluable tool for doctors, scientists, and researchers. Each day brings new breakthroughs and ML applications that address real healthcare challenges. The rapid progress in ML is catching the attention of the medical industry. ML concepts are empowering healthcare professionals to save lives, proactively identify diseases and risks, improve patient management, enhance patient engagement in the recovery process, and much more. Organizations worldwide are utilizing AI-driven solutions and ML models to enhance healthcare delivery.

This model highlights the significance of patient outcomes following ICU transfers, with various scoring and data mining systems available in the medical and data science sectors.

In the future, there is potential to further enhance this model by incorporating new algorithms that are yet to emerge.

# 8 Darknet for Gun and Suspicious Activity Detection and Crime Prediction

V. S. Gulhane and A. M. Bhugul

Research Department of Computer Science and Applications, Vivekanandha College of Arts and Science for Women (Autonomous), Tiruchengode, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-8](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-8)

## 8.1 Introduction

To ensure human safety, video surveillance systems are now accessible everywhere. The primary objective behind implementing these systems is to effectively monitor the designated areas, thereby enabling the identification of potential threats or security hazards that may arise \[[1](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_1)\]. The majority of systems now require human operators to keep an eye on these images. Additionally, each operator is responsible for simultaneously controlling the video feeds from numerous security cameras. This fact and ingrained human traits like exhaustion or a lack of concentration over time might cause people to overlook critical occurrences or react too late, resulting in serious harm and rendering modern video surveillance systems ineffective or useless \[[2](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_2),[3](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_3)\]. The increase in violent crimes has led to an urgent need for effective security measures, particularly in public areas such as airports, shopping centers, banks, and ATMs.

The widespread adoption of closed-circuit television (CCTV) surveillance has significantly increased in order to bolster public safety through the monitoring and identification of potential security risks. Due to the time-consuming nature and inherent susceptibility to errors, manual surveillance has prompted a surge of interest in the advancement of automated systems. These systems aim to detect weapons in real-time CCTV footage, offering a potential solution to enhance efficiency and accuracy in security monitoring. Recently, the J&K Rajouri Terror Attack took place in January 2023 where seven people were killed and a few injured. In January 2023 also, in California, an individual armed with a gun took the lives of 11 innocent individuals and caused injuries to nine others \[31\]. In February 2023, a mass shooting was carried out by a man who killed six people in Mississippi \[[4](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_4)\]. In Punjab, India, robbers looted Rs. 22 lakhs at gunpoint from a bank in 2023 \[[5](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_5)\].

Unfortunately, incidents involving danger and firearms are occurring more frequently. Mass shootings, handgun attacks, shootings on school property, and terrorist strikes are a few instances of these situations \[[6](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_6),[7](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_7)\]. Early identification of potentially dangerous objects or dubious activity in real-time CCTV footage could prevent or significantly reduce many of these instances.

Operator response to these hazards can be greatly aided by the incorporation of intelligent technologies that automatically recognize risky objects or activities. In recent years, notable advancements have occurred in the domain of machine learning systems, with a particular emphasis on computer vision. Computer vision has experienced remarkable progress in recent times, largely attributed to the development and accessibility of deep learning-based architectures. These advanced models have demonstrated exceptional performance across various tasks, including object detection, image segmentation, and even data generation. This success can be attributed, in large part, to the widespread availability and utilization of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs). The increased accessibility of CNNs has significantly contributed to the improved accuracy and efficiency of computer vision systems, opening doors to a wide range of applications and possibilities.

Initially, the focus on automatic weapon detection primarily revolved around the identification of weapons in X-ray images. For the same, various machine learning techniques were used. To address the challenge of object detection in 3D CT imagery, in 2013, a comparative analysis was carried out by Flitton et al. to assess various 3D feature descriptors \[[8](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_8)\]. Conventional machine learning methods have been applied to identify handguns in video surveillance using standard RGB images. In their research, Tiwari and Verma \[[9](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_9)\] proposed an identification pipeline for automatic handgun detection. The pipeline they suggested involved utilizing Harris interest point detectors and Fast Retina Key point (FREAK) descriptors \[[9](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_9)\]. The objective of this approach was to improve both the accuracy and efficiency of firearm detection within a specific context. Apart from the impressive outcomes achieved by CNN-based classifiers, alternative deep learning architectures have also been created for the purpose of identifying firearms. The first methods relied on sliding windows, in which tens of thousands of regions are taken from the original image and each is subjected to a CNN for classification \[[10](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_10)\]. The primary drawback of this family of approaches is its computational complexity, which poses challenges for real-time systems. The requirement to process numerous regions within the images demands significant computational resources, resulting in potential delays and limitations in real-time performance.

To address the computational challenges, alternative methods in automatic weapon detection adopt a strategy of selecting only a subset of candidate regions, also known as region proposals, from the image. Then, for each region, item categorization and bounding box refinement are carried out. Detectors in this category are also referred to as two-stage detectors. The first architecture created using this technology was the region-based CNN (R-CNN) \[[11](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_11)\], while Faster-RCNN \[[12](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_12)\], its most recent version, offers several performance and speed improvements (such as a Region Proposal Network). The sliding window and region approach to firearm detection systems were examined in 2018 by Olmos et al. \[[13](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_13)\]. The best results were achieved by a faster R-CNN trained on their own dataset and employing a Visual Geometry Group16 CNN as the feature extraction base. Bounding box regression and object classification are carried out using one-stage detectors like YOLO \[[14](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_14)\], RetinaNet \[[15](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_15)\], or SSD \[[16](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_16)\] without the usage of ready-made candidate region proposals. These architectures are more suited for mobile devices since they typically have faster inference times. YOLOv3 has been used for pistol detection in recent studies \[[17](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_17),[18](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_18)\] that are essentially straight implementations of YOLOv3 trained on specialized handgun datasets. Furthermore, Salazar Gonzalez et al. \[[19](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_19)\] suggested a method that makes the Faster-RCNN more effective at detecting handguns by employing a two-stage training process based on both fake and real images.

The works do, in fact, rely solely on the visual look of the object of interest, such as weaponry, and do not provide any further context information. There are some methods where temperature-based analysis is also done \[[20](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_20)\]. To detect the weapons, the temperature is calculated to detect metal weapons. Segmentation of the object from the background in medical images using DNN \[[21](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_21)\] and classification using features-based analysis \[[22](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_22)\]. Various techniques have been utilized in other works to incorporate body posture. The effects of altering the training datasets by including body position overlays in the source photos were investigated by Salido et al. \[[23](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_23)\]. The metrics for certain detectors improved, according to the results. To enrich the input photographs, the pose information is employed in this instance during pre-processing, but when training, just visual features are used. A combination architecture that combines the output mask of a YOLOv3 base handgun detector and a body pose detection heat map was proposed by Velasco-Mata et al. \[[24](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_24)\]. As a result, the model generates graphics in grayscale showing possible handgun positions.

To discover the appropriate trade-off between FPs and FNs for each dataset, this method relies heavily on a manually set threshold for the grayscale images. Additionally, Ruiz-Santaquiteria et al. \[[25](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_25)\] presented a technique that leverages body posture data to identify handguns. To extract bounding boxes for the hand area and posture critical points, this technique uses a Calculator for 2D body poses. Then, two separate CNNs are created, one for processing hand regions and the other for body posture images. A detection judgment is then produced by combining the output from the two branches. This strategy is similar to the one we suggested, except it solely uses CNN-based architectures to extract visual and position features. In the next chapter the PSA (proposed system architecture) is illustrated.

## 8.2 Darknet for Object Detection

It refers to a neural network framework that is open-source in nature that has gained widespread popularity in recent years for its speed and accuracy in object detection in real time. Darknet is a software framework that has the capability to implement cutting-edge object detection models like YOLO and YOLOv2. These models are renowned for their ability to detect objects accurately and rapidly in real-world situations. Furthermore, Darknet provides a user-friendly interface for building and training custom models on a variety of datasets, making it accessible to researchers and developers with various levels of expertise. The framework also supports several Deep learning architectures encompassing various neural network models, including Convolutional and recurrent neural networks allowing for the creation of a variety of applications beyond the detection of objects. It is usual practice to add more convolutional layers to neural networks to improve their capacity to recognize smaller objects or finer features in pictures. To achieve this, the network's depth may be increased, the number of filters in each layer can be increased, or skip connections can be added to make it easier for data to flow between levels. By making these changes, the network's ability to extract intricate information from pictures will be improved, leading to better object identification performance.

Modifying Darknet layers allows for the creation of customized network architecture that can improve the performance of the network on specific tasks or datasets. The flexibility and versatility of Darknet make it a powerful tool for developing deep learning applications.

## 8.3 Proposed System Architecture (PSA)

Our proposed system processes input images from a real-time video camera and converts them into video frames. To train the neural network, we developed a customized dataset with various types and sizes of firearms. The target item is then located by running a feature extraction method across the input image. The image (a, b) is subjected to a process of feature extraction to get F\_ψ^i (k, c, d). there is equation 8.1.

$F\_{\\varphi}(k\_{0},c,d)\\frac{1}{\\sqrt{MN}}\\sum\_{x = 0}^{M - 1N - 1}{\\quad.image(a,b)\\varphi\_{j = 0}(a,b)}$ (8.1)

$F\_{\\psi}^{i}(k,a,b)\\frac{1}{\\sqrt{MN}}\\sum\_{x = 0}^{M = 1}{\\quad.\\sum\_{y = 0}^{N - 1}{\\quad.image(a,b)\\psi^{i}j\_{k,c,d}(a,b)\\psi i}}$ (8.2)

[Figure 8.1](#ch08.xhtml#fig8_1) shows the proposed architecture for multiple gun and helmet detection using the Darknet. We have modified the original layers of Darknet-53 by adding residual blocks of size 1×64 and 2×128 to improve the network's performance. The output from this stage is sent to a Dense Architecture, where every layer is connected to every other layer in a dense block to promote feature reuse and enable the network to learn more robust and discriminating features. The outputs from each layer are concatenated and passed to the next dense block to improve the network's information flow. The training process is enhanced by batch normalization and ReLU activation functions, which also prevent overfitting. The front detection layer (FDL) is responsible for identifying the location and presence of objects in an input image. This layer is usually a convolutional layer that examines the input image at multiple scales and locations, searching for regions that may contain the objects of interest.

![Figure 8.1 Proposed system architecture (PSA) for detection of multiple weapons (guns) in a single frame. We have trained our PSA with 8,914 gun images and 9,951 helmet images of different types and sizes.](images/fig8_1.jpg)

[Figure 8.1 Proposed system architecture (PSA) for detection of multiple weapons (guns) in a single frame. We have trained our PSA with 8,914 gun images and 9,951 helmet images of different types and sizes.](#ch08.xhtml#Ffig8_1)

The front detection layer (FDL) uses predefined anchor boxes or prior boxes, which are small rectangles with predefined sizes and aspect ratios, to detect objects in the input image. For each anchor box, the FDL calculates scores representing the probability of an object being present in that box, and bounding box offsets indicate the difference between the anchor box and the actual object's bounding box. The architecture is optimized for detecting small objects, such as a gun in a video that appears small and far away from the camera. Up-sampling layers are used to increase the feature map's resolution and improve detection accuracy. This enables accurate detection of tiny items like firearms.

After each convolutional layer in the outlined architecture, a residual group is used, and these groups can include 1x, 2x, 4x, or 8x residual blocks. Before each residual group, a stridden convolution with a stride of 2 is done to lower the spatial dimension of the feature maps. The technique of downsampling aids in the preservation of low-level characteristics and the encoding of positional data that is useful for object detection. Unlike non-parametric downsampling methods like max-pooling, using stridden convolution with learnable parameters allows for more flexibility and improved detection of smaller objects. By incorporating these design choices, the architecture enhances the ability to capture and detect objects of varying sizes.

At various scales, the output from the three residual groups is recovered as feature vectors. Assuming the input to the network is 416×416, the three obtained feature vectors have spatial dimensions of 52×52, 26×26, and 13×13, respectively. These feature vectors correspond to different scales and are responsible for detecting objects of varying sizes. The 52×52 feature vector focuses on detecting small objects, the 26×26 feature vector handles medium-sized objects, and the 13×13 feature vector specializes in detecting large objects. By utilizing feature vectors at multiple scales, the network can effectively detect objects across different size ranges, enhancing its overall object detection capabilities.

From the older feature maps' finer-grained data and the later, upsampled feature maps' relevant semantic data, the network permits concatenating features with various sizes and upsampling.

We have trained our PSA with 8,914 gun images and 9,951 helmet images of different types and sizes. This architecture is fed into the proposed algorithm given below. A customized classifier has been developed to categorize activities into two distinct categories. When an activity is identified as suspicious, an alert call and SMS notification will be promptly sent to the authorized individual specified \[[26](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_26),[27](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_27)\]. This system aims to enhance the real-time detection and response to potentially suspicious events.

## 8.4 Proposed Algorithm

Following is a proposed strategy for spotting suspicious behavior in persons carrying multiple firearms and wearing helmets. Our Custom classifier divides the activity into two categories: suspicious and non-suspicious.

1.  Initialize camera; initialize WD = 0 (WD=Weapon Detection)
2.  Extract the frame from the camera after every 50 milliseconds.
3.  Pass the extracted frame through the **proposed** weapon and helmet detection architecture ([Figure 8.1](#ch08.xhtml#fig8_1)).
4.  If helmet is detected:
    
    Warning: “Remove Helmet”
    
5.  If weapon is detected:
    
    WD = WD + 1
    
6.  Else:
    
    WD = 0
    
7.  If WD 3:
    
    Warning: “Suspicious Activity Detected”
    
8.  Go to step 2.

The algorithm begins by initializing the camera, which will capture the video stream to be analyzed. We are working on real-time videos. The WD (weapon detection) variable is also initialized to the system and extracts a frame from the camera weapon and helmet detection architecture explained in the algorithm. If a helmet is detected in the frame, the system issues a warning message asking the person wearing the helmet to remove it. The warning aims to ensure that the face of the person is visible to the camera, which can be important for identification purposes.

**To Detect weapon**: If a weapon is detected in the frame, the WD variable is incremented by one. This indicates that a weapon has been detected in the current frame. If no weapon is detected in the frame, the WD variable is reset to zero. This ensures that the WD variable keeps track of consecutive frames in which a weapon has been detected. If the value of the WD variable is greater than three, it indicates that a weapon has been detected in at least three consecutive frames. This could be an indication of suspicious activity, and the system issues a warning message alerting the security personnel to investigate the situation. The system goes back to step 2 and repeats the process, capturing frames, detecting helmets and weapons, and issuing warning messages as necessary. This process continues until the surveillance system is stopped. Every 50 milliseconds. This process is repeated. Each extracted frame is passed through a proposed zero.

## 8.5 Extracting Frames

The technique for extracting frames from a video involves reading each frame of the video file and saving it as an image file. Here is a step-by-step process to extract frames from a video:

1.  Open the video file using a video processing library like OpenCV.
2.  Check if the video file was successfully opened.
3.  Initialize variables to keep track of the frame count and whether the frame was successfully read.
4.  Loop through the video frames using a while loop.
5.  Read the next frame using the read () method of the video object.
6.  Check if a frame was successfully read by checking the value of the success variable.
7.  If a frame was successfully read, increment the frame count.
8.  Save the frame as an image file using the imwrite() method of the OpenCV library.
9.  Continue looping until all frames have been processed.
10.  Release the video file using the release() method of the video object.

The technique of locating and extracting features from an image that may be utilized to portray the picture in a more condensed form is known as **feature extraction**. Four different feature extraction strategies have been utilized here:

1.  **Color-based features**: To describe the distribution of colors in a picture, color histograms, color moments, or color correlograms can be extracted.
2.  **Texture-based features**: Using texture features to extract the texture information from an image, such as Gabor filters, local binary patterns, or gray-level co-occurrence matrices.
3.  ***Edge-based features***: We use this to extract and characterize edges or boundaries in images or other visual data with the help of techniques such as Canny edge detection and Sobel Operator.
4.  ***Shape-based features***: Extracting shape information such as corner points, contours, or geometric moments to represent the shape of objects in an image.

Dimensionality reduction is then used on these extracted characteristics to make them less dimensional. The PCA approach was employed in this case. With as much of the original data as feasible still present, the primary goals of PCA are to find patterns and reduce the dimensionality of huge data sets. By combining the original features from a dataset PCA decreases the dimensionality of the dataset into a new set of features that are linear combinations of the original features. The transformed features are referred to as principal components and are calculated in a way that maximizes the variance of the data along each component and are sorted in order of decreasing variance.

By retaining only, the top k principal components, we are able to successfully decrease the dataset's dimensionality from n features to k features, where k<n. The reduced dataset may be applied to a variety of tasks, such as visualization, compression of data, or as input to machine learning models. Here are the main steps involved in performing PCA for dimensionality reduction.

Equation (8.3), Equation (8.4), Equation (8.5), and Equation (8.6) are used to compute accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score, respectively, in order to determine the performance parameters \[[28](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_28),[29](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_29),[30](#ch08.xhtml#ref8_30)\]. Equation (8.3) is used to generate the confusion matrix.

Accuracy  = TP  + TN/TP  + TN  + FP  + FN  (8.3)

Precision  = TP/TP  + FP  (8.4)

Recall  = TP/TP  + FN  (8.5)

*F*1 Score  = 2 \* TP/ 2 \* TP  + FP  + FN  (8.6)

In our study, we thoroughly compared our results against various established methodologies. We comprehensively evaluated our suggested technique by employing a range of machine learning-based models for object recognition and classification. Specifically, we examined the performance of YOLO V3, YOLO V4, YOLO V5, Haar Cascade Classifier, SVM Classifier, Decision Tree, and KNN Classifier. The proposed method is also able to overcome some limitations of machine learning built-in methods such as the detection of small objects from video, working on large data sets, and detection of objects correctly from blurred or noisy video. Our findings demonstrate significant improvements over the limitations encountered in existing Methodologies.

# 9 Image Edge Detection Using Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks

M. Jansirani

Research Scholar, PG and Research Department of Computer Science and Applications, Vivekanandha College of Arts and Science for Women (Autonomous), Tiruchengode, Tamil Nadu, India

P. Sumitra

Associate Professor, PG and Research Department of Computer Science and Applications, Vivekanandha College of Arts and Science for Women (Autonomous), Tiruchengode, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-9](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-9)

## 9.1 Introduction

Image processing \[[1](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_1)\] is essential in biometric authentication, medical applications, object detection, etc. The images consist of several pieces of information like edges, texture, and patterns which are more valuable for the particular image recognition process. Among the various characteristics, edges play a vital role because it is used to predict the image boundaries and corners that causes to improve the overall object detection efficiency \[[2](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_2),[3](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_3)\]. The edges are divided into horizontal, diagonal, and vertical edges, which are used to perform pattern recognition. In addition, image edges represent the image features and intensity change values. These characteristics help to understand the image structural changes, improving the overall pattern recognition \[[4](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_4)\] efficiency. Generally, gradient and Gaussian operators are widely applied to derive the boundary level details. During this process, Sobel, Prewitt, and Robert gradient operators \[[5](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_5),[6](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_6)\] utilize the first-order derivations to extract the edge information. The gradient operator analyzes the images in different orientations, and edges are predicted with minimum computation time. However, the gradient procedure fails to prevent diagonal features and faces difficulties when the images are influenced by noise \[[7](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_7)\]. The images are analyzed by second-order Gaussian derivatives that derive the edge information with a minimum error rate. The Gaussian Edge Detection techniques \[[8](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_8)\] can extract the edge details from noise-infected images. The derived edge information is widely applied to medical imaging, face recognition, structural analysis, object localization, and traffic control systems. The image edges are derived by considering the image properties such as contrast and brightness. These features are applied to the image formation model by managing several discontinuities like material properties, surface orientation, depth, and scene illumination \[[9](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_9),[10](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_10)\]. Therefore, the image edge segmentation process is highly integrated with pre-processing techniques like a Median filter, Mean filter, Gaussian filter, Weiner filter, and other filtering techniques \[[11](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_11)\]. The filtering approach explores the images pixel by pixel to eliminate irrelevant information. The improper and inconsistent pixel elimination procedure improves the overall edge detection efficiency.

Several edge detection techniques, such as Watershed, Canny edge, Soble edge, and Dual clustering, are widely applied to images to predict the edge information \[[12](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_12),[13](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_13)\]. However, these techniques have a contrast variability issue while analyzing the edge information. In addition, the existing methods consume more time while preserving the in-depth edge details. The improper identification of edge information causes the minimization of the overall image segmentation and object detection efficiency. The research issue is overcome by applying the neural model because it has several learning parameters, functions, and layers to train the edge detection system \[[14](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_14),[15](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_15)\]. This work involves a fine-tuned deep convolution neural network to process the images and extract the edge information. The deep convolution network has a fully connected layer, pooling function, and convolution layers which help to predict the image boundaries with a minimum false prediction rate. In addition, the network uses the fireflies optimization algorithm to update the network parameters for every computation. The effective updation of network parameters leads to minimizing the error rate and maximizing the edge prediction accuracy. The explained fine-tuned neural model-based edge detection system was developed with the help of MATLAB tool, and the system's excellence is evaluated using the Berkeley Segmentation Dataset images. Described methods analyze the collected images, and system efficiency is examined using different metrics. The research objectives are listed as follows.

*   Maximize the image edge detection accuracy by reducing the false edge detection rate.
*   Improving the system performance while processing the noise images is done with the help of a fine-tuned deep neural model.
*   To reduce the computation difficulties and maximize the edge detection accuracy.

## 9.2 Related Works

In \[[16](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_16)\], edge detection of brain tumor images using the Support Vector Machine (SVM) and K-nearest Neighboring (KNN) algorithm is carried out. The system aims to identify disease-infected exact edges from shapeless tumor growth. The images are collected and processed by applying the image enhancement technique that reduces irrelevant feature involvement. Then SVM and KNN approach is applied to predict the active contour from the MRI image. The effective utilization of kernel values maximizes the overall edge detection accuracy.

With the use of a fusion methodology, the authors of \[[17](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_17)\] were able to extract edges from images using multiple threshold R-Convolution Neural networks. The images are investigated using a deep learning method that extracts edge-relevant features via a symmetric feature pyramid. Next, the features used to extract the region from the image are probed with a multiple threshold technique. The neural network employs an optimization strategy to minimize outliers as it identifies edge features. In addition to regional segmentation, the system reaches 75% IoU value.

In \[[18](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_18)\], the Shi-Tomasi Corner prediction algorithm is introduced to improve object detection accuracy. The system aims to maximize the edge computation speed and accuracy by extracting the SURF and SIFT feature descriptors. The images are collected from Caltech-101 images, which are processed by applying the locality-preserving projection process. The algorithm investigates every pixel, and the corner-related information is extracted. The derived edges are processed by SIFT and SURF algorithms to extract the features. The extracted features are analyzed with the help of random forest, decision tree, and KNN approaches that predict the objects with maximum detection accuracy.

The CT and positron emission tomography (PET) images are analyzed by fuzzy Markov random field segmentation method in \[[19](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_19)\] to investigate the lung tumor impacted region. With the help of probability estimates and the Gaussian distribution function, this technique investigates the pixel distribution inside the model. After that, the distribution of pixels is calculated, and many features of manipulative pixel similarity are noted. The same pixel clusters are then shaped into clusters, and the affected area is projected based on the group produced.

Using the deep residual separable convolution network, \[[20](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_20)\] could segregate areas of the lung that tumors have damaged. To assess the effectiveness of the newly developed method, the StructSeg 2019 and medical segmentation decathlon datasets have been deployed. Deep learning and an ensemble classifier, which has a 95% accuracy rate in identifying cancer, are used to process the images that have been collected. From the various researchers' opinions, image segmentation play a vital role in different applications. Several existing methods, such as support vector machines, convolution networks, edges, and canny detections, are widely applied to the images to predict the edges. However, the existing techniques have maximum computation difficulties and false-edge detection rate. The research issue has been overcome by applying the Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks (FFDCN).

## 9.3 Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks Based Image Edge Detection

The manuscript utilizes the Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks (FFDCN) approach to maximize the image edge detection efficiency. The work's main objective is to increase edge detection accuracy and reduce false edge feature detection. The deep convolution neural network uses different characteristics such as filters, kernels, and strides. These characteristics are utilized in various layers, such as the convolution layer, max-pooling function, and fully convoluted layer. These layers have the sigmoid activation function to compute the output value. This analysis requires a fine-tuning process to minimize the false edge prediction rate. The overall working process of the FFDCN approach is illustrated in [Figure 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_1).

![Figure 9.1 Structure of FFDCN-based image segmentation.](images/fig9_1.jpg)

[Figure 9.1 Structure of FFDCN-based image segmentation.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_1)

### 9.3.1 Dataset

The work uses the Berkeley Segmentation Dataset \[[21](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_21)\] images to analyze the FFDCN algorithm-based edge detection's working process. The dataset has image boundary and ground truth details used to train the segmentation system. The effective utilization of image boundary information maximizes the overall detection accuracy. The dataset consists of training (100 images) and testing (200 images), which are processed according to [Figure 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_1) to predict the image edge information. The dataset has 1,000 images from the Corel dataset collected from 30 subjects. From the 1,000 images, 12,000 hand-labelled segmented images help effectively identify the new image edges. Then the sample images are depicted in [Figure 9.2](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_2).

![Figure 9.2 Input dataset gray scale images.](images/fig9_2.jpg)

[Figure 9.2 Input dataset gray scale images.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_2)

[Figure 9.2](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_2) shows the gray scale images gathered from the dataset \[[21](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_21)\]. The dataset has both color and gray scale images which are processed by different processing steps to predict the image edge information. Suppose the collected images are in color and converted into gray scale images to simplify the image edge detection process. The gray scale images reduce the difficulties while analyzing the image boundary information.

### 9.3.2 Image Pre-Processing

The first step of this work is removing noise from the collected images, which is named image pre-processing. Images are corrupted during acquisition, which completely affects pattern recognition or object detection. The image noises are removed by applying different filtering techniques that explore each pixel and compare it with the threshold value. According to the comparison, the noise pixels are eliminated from the image, improving the image quality. The pre-processing is done by applying the Mean histogram equalization approach. The noise removal process eliminates low-quality pixels and improves the image quality, increasing the image edge detection process. The histogram approach explores each pixel distribution, enhancing low-quality pixels and effectively identifying the image edges and boundary details. Initially, the images are decomposed into sub-images by analyzing the pixels in qualitative and quantitative ways. The image threshold value is computed with the help of the OpenCV approach and the computed value is used to analyze the pixel deviations and noise. After decomposing the images, noise pixels are replaced using the mean value. The pixel intensity value is estimated and compared with the threshold value, and the mean value replaces the noise pixel. The image quality is improved by applying the histogram approach. Considering the image is denoted S that has several grey level pixels G, which are represented as *P*[0], *P*[1], ….*P*[*G* − 1]. The pixel distribution is computed according to the pixel density and cumulative distribution value. The calculated density values are arranged, and pixel ranges are computed. The pixel density value is estimated using equation (9.1).

$PDF\\left( P\_{n} \\right) = \\frac{n^{h}}{n}$ (9.1)

In equation (9.1), the image has n amount of pixels and a value from 0 to G-1, *n*^(*h*) is denoted as the marked pixel value and the weight mean value of the pixel is estimated using equation (9.2).

$X\_{t} = \\frac{\\sum\_{l = a}^{b}l \\ast CDF\\left( l \\right)}{\\sum\_{l = a}^{b}CDF\\left( l \\right)}$ (9.2)

The above equation (12.2) computes the sub-interval histogram value and the a, b value changed from 0 to 255. The interval value is defined as (0≤*r*≤*t*−1), the recursion level of images is denoted as T, and the value is 1. In the above computation, a grey color pixel is represented as L, and the range of sub histogram is denoted as r(*X*[*r*],*X*[*r* + 1]). Therefore, the pixel-weighted value is calculated using equation (9.3)

*S*[*k*] = {*S*(*x*,*y*)|*X*[*k*]≤*S*(*x*,*y*)*X*[*k* + 1]∀*S*(*x*,*y*)∈*S*} (9.3)

In equation (9.3), the sub-images are represented as *S*[*k*] and the pixel combination is determined based on the likelihood value, computed using equation (9.4).

$PDF\_{k}\\left( P\_{h} \\right) = \\frac{n^{k}}{n\_{k}}$ (9.4)

In equation (9.4), the sub-image histogram value is defined as *P**D**F*[*k*](*P*[*h*]) and it has *n*[*k*] number of pixels. Then the cumulative distribution is computed using equation (9.5)

$CDF{(G\_{j})} = \\sum\_{j = X\_{0} + 1}^{n}{PDF\_{k}{(G\_{j})}}$ (9.5)

In equation (9.5), the *h* = *X*[*k*] + 1, *X*[*k*] + 2, …*X*[*k* + 1] and, *k* = 0, 1, 2, ….*t* − 1. The weighted average value for each pixel is estimated from the computed distribution values. According to the values, the noise pixels are eliminated, and the histogram value is improved to enhance the image quality. The algorithm removes the noise level from 1 to 25% and increases the image quality from 20 to 35%. Then the sample noise-removed images are illustrated in [Figure 9.3](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_3).

![Figure 9.3 Sample pre-processed images.](images/fig9_3.jpg)

[Figure 9.3 Sample pre-processed images.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_3)

### 9.3.3 Edge Detection Using FFDCN

The edge detection is performed by applying the Fireflies Deep Convolution Neural Model (FFDCN). This algorithm aims to maximize the edge detection accuracy by reducing the misclassification error rate. The Convolution network uses the kernel, stride, and filters that identify the image edges and boundaries. The full and convolution layers analyze image pixels continuously and predict the edge information with a minimum false-positive rate. The network has fully connected max-pooling and convolution layers that work along with the activation and learning functions. The algorithms predict the edges by computing pixels in the image. These layers have local receptive fields, weight replication, and fine-tuning procedure to reduce the false edge prediction rate. The input layer receives noise-free images as input that is examined pixel-by-pixel using the local receptive field. The receptive field maximizes the edge prediction accuracy without affecting the computation process. The convolution network has a sequence of layers that learns the hierarchical features, improving the overall edge detection efficiency. The network's hidden layers are generally convolution, activation, and pooling. Then the structure of the Deep Convolution Layer Network is illustrated in [Figure 9.4](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_4).

![Figure 9.4 Deep convolution neural network structure.](images/fig9_4.jpg)

[Figure 9.4 Deep convolution neural network structure.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_4)

[Figure 9.4](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_4) demonstrates the deep convolution neural network structure. The input image of 256∗256 pixels is analyzed using the first layer (convolution layer-C1). The convolution layer uses the 5∗5 size of six convolution kernels on the input image (64∗64 sizes). The layer identifies the image's basic features, such as corners and edges. The convolution layer output is fed into the sub-sampling layer (S2), also named the max pooling layer. Each square of four pixels in the first layer output is analyzed, and the maximum value is obtained. The S2 layer scale down the 24, 48∗48 images by a factor of 2 and the output is obtained with the size 24∗24. The second convolution layer (C3) has 24 convolution kernels of 5∗5 size and produces 16∗16 images. Then, the second max pooling layer (S4) is applied to get the 16∗16 size of images, and a fully convoluted layer with 256 outputs is obtained by having the 400 nodes in the layer.

Then the final layer analyzes the input to get the 1-D array value as output for the input. The SoftMax activation function processes the array value to get the final output value. During this process, the network weight value has been fine-tuned to minimize the false edge prediction accuracy. Therefore, this work uses the fireflies optimization algorithm to update the network parameters to maximize the edge prediction accuracy. The algorithm works based on the attractiveness, distance, and moment operators. The parameter value collection selects the most relevant value by applying the attractiveness function that minimizes the deviation between the predicted edges. Afterward, the distance and moment function is utilized to choose the optimized network parameters. The fitness function *f*(*x*) is linked with the firefly's characteristics, such as absorption coefficient *γ* and light intensity I. The defined fitness function minimizes the false edge prediction maximization issues by utilizing the bias and weight fine-tuning process. In weight updating, every parameter is compared according to the fitness function, and the best parameter is selected. Then light intensity values are computed to identify the more relevant parameters. The fitness function is selected by multiplication between the number of fireflies (edges-related features) and the generation count. Then the fine-tuning network process is performed according to equation (9.6).

*x*[*i*]^(*t* + 1) = *x*[*i*]^(*t*) + *β**e**x**p*\[ − *γ**r*[*i**j*]²\](*x*[*j*]^(*t*) − *x*[*i*]^(*t*)) + *α*[*t*]*ϵ*[*t*] (9.6)

According to equation (9.6), network parameter updating *x*[*i*]^(*t* + 1) is performed using the *α*[*t*] controlling step size, *ϵ*[*t*] Gaussian vector, fireflies absorption coefficient *β* and *x*[*j*]^(*t*) − *x*[*i*]^(*t*) (difference between two fireflies). The network parameters are continuously fine-tuned based on this process to minimize false edge detection efficiency. Then the algorithm process is illustrated as follows ([Figure 9.5](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_5)).

![Figure 9.5 Algorithm for firefly's optimization algorithm-based network fine-tuning process.](images/fig9_5.jpg)

[Figure 9.5 Algorithm for firefly's optimization algorithm-based network fine-tuning process.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_5)

According to the above process, weight values are updated to minimize the difference between the computed and predicted edge values. Then the sampled edge segmented output images are illustrated in [Figure 9.6](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_6).

![Figure 9.6 Edge-detected sample images.](images/fig9_6.jpg)

[Figure 9.6 Edge-detected sample images.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_6)

After extracting the image edges, respective features are predicted by different approaches and classified with a classifier's help to perform the object recognition process. The introduced Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks (FFDCN) approach efficiency is evaluated using the following performance analysis process.

## 9.4 Experimental Analysis

During the study, the system looks at images from the Berkeley Segmentation Dataset to determine how effectively FFDCN is being used. The effectiveness with which the system recognizes the edges in the input images is evaluated using these one hundred images. The newly developed system is trained with 200 images, from which image edge-relevant features are extracted for use in the training process. The template versions of the derived features are saved away in the database. The retrieved edge features can predict the test image's linked edges with the highest detection accuracy. The FFDCN approach excellency is evaluated using different metrics such as Peak Signal Noise Ration (PSNR), Structural Similarity Index (SSIM), False Acceptance Rate (FAR), False Rejection Rate (FRR), and Detection Accuracy (DA). The BO-ARNM system efficiency is compared with Support Vector Machine (SVM) and Convolution Neural Networks (CNN).

### 9.4.1 Peak Signal to Noise Ratio (PSNR)

The PSNR value is used to determine the quality of the anticipated edges, which is expressed in decibels (dB).

$PSNR = 10\\ \\log\_{10}\\left( \\frac{R^{2}}{MSE} \\right)$ (9.7)

In equation (9.7), MSE (difference between the actual and anticipated edge details) and R are the input image maximum variations. Then the obtained PSNR value is illustrated in [Table 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#table9_1).

[Table 9.1 PSNR Analysis](#ch09.xhtml#Ttable9_1)

| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| **50** || 42.66 || 55.81 || 75.14 |
| **70** || 42.74 || 55.63 || 75.67 |
| **90** || 42.63 || 54.11 || 76.36 |
| **110** || 42.73 || 54.75 || 76.89 |
| **130** || 42.02 || 53.24 || 76.14 |
| **150** || 42.55 || 53.88 || 76.6 |
| **170** || 42.65 || 53.96 || 75.18 |
| **190** || 42.9 || 55.72 || 76.97 |
| **210** || 42.73 || 55.14 || 75.23 |
| **230** || 42.49 || 55.73 || 75.58 |
| **250** || 42.6 || 53.56 || 76.24 |

| **290** || 42.47 || 53.08 || 75.05 |

[Table 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#table9_1) illustrates the PSNR value for the introduced FFDCN-based edge detection process. From the discussion, the introduced system attains a high PSNR value (76 dB) compared to other methods, such as SVM (42.54 dB) and CNN (54.54 dB). Then the respective graphical analysis is illustrated in [Figure 9.7](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_7).

![Figure 9.7 PSNR analysis.](images/fig9_7.jpg)

[Figure 9.7 PSNR analysis.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_7)

[Figure 9.7](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_7) illustrates that the FFDCN approach attains high PSNR value, which means the proposed FFDCN approach effectively removes the noise from the image. In addition, the PSNR value shows that the quality of the image is unaffected by the noise removal process because the output images are identical to the image. Clearly, the great quality of the input image demonstrates that the proposed system can accurately detect the edges of the image.

### 9.4.2 Structural Similarity Index (SSIM)

The SSIM metric determines how similar the real and edge-detected pictures are. Equation (9.8) is used to calculate the SSIM

*S**S**I**M* = \[*l*(*I*[0],*I*[*r*])\]^(*α*).\[*c*(*I*[0],*I*[*r*])\]^(*β*).\[*s*(*I*[0],*I*[*r*])\]^(*γ*) (9.8)

In equation (9.7), *α* = *β* = *γ* = 1. Therefore, the FFDCN approach gives the one as SSIM value; the two images are similar. Then the obtained SSIM value is illustrated in [Table 9.2](#ch09.xhtml#table9_2).

[Table 9.2 Structural Similarity Index Analysis](#ch09.xhtml#Ttable9_2)

  
| Images || SVM \[[16](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_16)\] || CNN \[[17](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_17)\] || FFDCN |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

### 9.4.3 False Acceptance Rate (FAR)

FAR is the metric used to compute how effectively recognize the very edges from the images. The FAR value is computed using equation (9.9)

$FAR = \\frac{Number\\ of\\ features\\ accepted}{Number\\ of\\ features\\ tested} \\ast 100$ (9.9)

### 9.4.4 False Rejection Rate

FRR is a metric used to predict the incorrectly recognized edge features during the edge detection process. It is calculated using equation (9.10).

$FRR = \\frac{Number\\ of\\ original\\ features\\ rejected}{Number\\ of\\ original\\ features\\ tested} \\ast 100$ (9.10)

From [Figure 9.11](#ch09.xhtml#fig9_11), the efficiency of the FFDCN-based edge detection process is evaluated using different metrics such as detection accuracy, PSNR, SSIM, FAR, and FRR. The introduced FFDCN approach efficiency is compared with the SVM and CNN defined in \[[16](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_16) and [17](#ch09.xhtml#ref9_17)\]. The analysis clearly states that the FFDCN approach attains 97.87% accuracy compared to other methods, SVM (94.1%) and CNN (96.054%). The fireflies optimization algorithm is applied to fine-tune the neural network performance in every iteration. The continuous updating of network parameters minimizes the overall deviation between the edge predictions and improves the detection accuracy. In addition, the weighted mean and histogram approach involvement reduces the irrelevant and noise pixel contribution. The image brightness value is also improved to reduce the edge detection computation complexity. Therefore, the deep convolution network successfully uses the network kernel, stride, and filters which predict accurate image edges with minimum FRR and FAR rate. Thus, the introduced system recognizes the edges of the image by reducing the computation complexities and maximizing the overall performance.

![Figure 9.11 Efficiency analysis of BO-ARNM-based edge detection approach.](images/fig9_11.jpg)

[Figure 9.11 Efficiency analysis of BO-ARNM-based edge detection approach.](#ch09.xhtml#Ffig9_11)

## 9.5 Conclusion

The paper explores the working process of Fireflies to Fine-Tuned Deep Convolution Networks (FFDCN) based image edge detection process. Initially, images are collected from the Berkeley Segmentation Dataset, which has numerous images. The gathered images are processed by the weighted mean filter, which investigates every pixel, and the noise pixels are replaced with the help of the mean value. The pixel distribution and intensity value are computed to maximize the image quality. The noise-removed images are processed with the help of a layer of deep convolution neural model. The network uses the fully connected and max-pooling layers to predict the image edges and corner information. The fireflies optimization algorithm-based network parameter selection process is performed during this process. The optimization algorithm uses their food-searching characteristics to select the optimized features. The effective selection procedure minimizes the deviation between the actual and predicted edge value. The model predicts the edges with a 97.87% detection rate, which is higher compared to other methods. In the future, edge prediction will be improved by applying reinforcement learning and optimization algorithms.
