# 10 Application of Machine Learning, Deep Learning, and Econometric Models in Stock Price Movement of Rain Industries An In-Depth Analysis

Abhijit Biswas

PhD Research Scholar, Maulana Abul Kalam Azad University of Technology (Formerly WBUT), Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Meghdoot Ghosh

Department of Management Studies, Department of Management, Swami Vivekananda Institute of Modern Science, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-10](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-10)

## 10.1 Introduction

Rain Industries is a global company engaged in the production and sale of carbon and chemical products. Founded in 1974, the company is headquartered in Hyderabad, India, and has a significant presence in the carbon black industry. Carbon black, Rain Industries' primary product, is a fine powder made from carbon-rich materials such as coal tar, crude oil, or natural gas. It is widely used as a reinforcing agent in rubber products, including tires, belts, and hoses, due to its ability to enhance the strength and durability of these materials. Additionally, carbon black finds applications in the manufacturing of plastics, inks, coatings, and other industrial products. Rain Industries operates through three primary business segments:

Carbon: This segment focuses on the production and sale of carbon black, which constitutes a significant portion of the company's revenue. Rain Industries is one of the biggest makers of carbon black internationally and supplies its items to different enterprises around the world.

High level materials: In this portion, Rain Industries fabricates and showcases specialty synthetics, for example, calcined oil coke (CPC), coal tar pitch (CTP), and other derivatives. These items find applications in industries like aluminum, steel, graphite electrode, and titanium dioxide.

Cement: Rain Industries operates cement plants in various locations, primarily catering to the South Indian market. The company produces different types of cement, including Portland cement, blended cement, and ready-mix concrete. Rain Industries' customer base is diverse, ranging from automotive manufacturers to construction companies, chemical producers, and other industrial sectors. The company has a global presence, with manufacturing facilities and offices located in multiple countries, including India, the United States, Canada, the Netherlands, Germany, Belgium, and China. The stock price of Rain Industries is influenced by various factors, including global carbon black demand, raw material prices, macroeconomic conditions, industry trends, and company-specific factors like production capacity, operational efficiency, and financial performance. Understanding and predicting the stock price movement of Rain Industries is crucial for investors, traders, and financial analysts to make informed decisions and optimize their investment strategies. In the following sections, we will explore the application of different logical strategies, including AI and profound learning, to analyze and forecast Rain Industries' stock price movements. Stock price movement analysis plays a crucial role in investment decisions and financial forecasting. Traditional approaches, such as econometric models, have long been used to understand and predict movements. However, with the advancement of technology, “ML” and “DL” techniques have gained significant attention for their ability to handle complex patterns and make accurate predictions. In this in-depth analysis, we explore the application of “ML”, “DL”, and econometric models in analyzing the stock price movement of Rain Industries, a leading player in the global industry. Carbon black is a raw material used in the manufacturing of tires, plastics, and other industrial products. By utilizing various models and techniques, we aim to uncover insights into the factors that influence Rain Industries' stock price and develop predictive models to forecast its future movements. This analysis will provide valuable information to investors, traders, and financial analysts seeking to make informed decisions regarding Rain Industries stock. We will begin by discussing the traditional econometric models commonly used in stock price analysis. Then, we will delve into the application of “machine learning” and “deep learning” algorithms to capture complex patterns and predict stock price movements. Finally, we will outline the steps involved in this analysis and provide an overview of the data sources used. By the end of this analysis, we will have gained a comprehensive understanding of Rain Industries' stock price movements and developed models that can aid in making accurate predictions. This information will contribute to a more informed investment strategy and help stakeholders make better financial decisions. Please note that the analysis presented here is for instructive purposes just and ought not be considered as monetary exhortation. Putting resources into the securities exchange conveys dangers, and it is vital to conduct direct intensive research and consultation with financial experts prior to pursuing any venture choices.

## 10.2 Literature Review

Volatility analysis and modelling have been extensively studied in the field of financial econometrics. Numerous studies have explored the effectiveness of ARCH family models in capturing volatility in bourse markets. However, there is a paucity of research specifically focusing on the Indian stock market. This literature survey provides an overview of relevant studies conducted in the field and highlights the gaps that this comparative analysis aims to address.

Volatility Analysis and ARCH Models: Engle (1982) introduced the ARCH model, which assumes that the conditional variance of a financial asset is a function of its past squared residuals. This ground-breaking work led to the development of various extensions and improvements to the basic ARCH model. Bollerslev (1986) proposed the GARCH model, which incorporates both lagged conditional variances and lagged squared residuals to capture volatility clustering. Nelson (1991) introduced the EGARCH model, which allows for asymmetric responses to pragmatic and uncertain shocks. Zakoian (1994) developed the TGARCH model, which incorporates a threshold parameter to capture the impact of extreme events on volatility.

ARCH Models in Global Financial Markets: ARCH family models have been widely applied in various global financial markets. Alizadeh, Brandt, and Diebold (2002) examined the performance of ARCH models in capturing volatility dynamics in the US stock market. They compared the accuracy of ARCH and GARCH models in volatility forecasting and found that GARCH models outperformed ARCH models. In the context of emerging markets, Chen and Lee (2003) evaluated the applicability of GARCH models in the Taiwan stock market. They concluded that GARCH models effectively captured volatility patterns and provided reliable forecasts.

Volatility Analysis in the Indian Stock Market: Studies focusing specifically on volatility analysis in the Indian stock market are limited. Prakash and Dash (2011) applied ARCH family models to analyze the volatility of stock returns in India. They found evidence of volatility clustering and suggested that GARCH models were suitable for modelling volatility in the Indian context. Khan and Ahmad (2016) investigated the “volatility dynamics” of the Indian equity market using GARCH models and concluded that GARCH (1,1) models provided the best fit to the data.

Customized Portfolio and Volatility Analysis: Customized portfolios, tailored to the specific preferences and objectives of individual investors, have gained prominence in portfolio management. Volatility analysis plays a crucial role in managing customized portfolios, as it helps assess risk and optimize asset allocation. However, there is a lack of research exploring the application of ARCH family models to volatility analysis in customized portfolios, particularly in the Indian Bourse.

Based on the existing literature, it is evident that there is a need for a comprehensive comparative analysis of ARCH family models for volatility analysis in a customized portfolio, specifically focusing on the Indian stock market. This paper aims to bridge the gap in the literature by examining the exhibition of ARCH models and identifying the most suitable model for volatility analysis in the Indian context. The subsequent sections will delve into the methodology, data collection, empirical results, and implications of the study.

Engle, R. F. (1982). Autoregressive conditional heteroscedasticity with estimates of the variance of United Kingdom inflation. Econometrica, 50(4), 987–1008.

Engle's groundbreaking paper introduces the ARCH model and demonstrates its application to modeling and forecasting inflation volatility. This paper lays the groundwork for subsequent research on ARCH family models.

Bollerslev, T. (1986), Generalized autoregressive conditional heteroskedasticity. Journal of Econometrics, 31(3), 307-327, Bollerslev's paper introduces the GARCH model, an “extension of the ARCH model that incorporates lagged conditional variances in addition to lagged squared error terms”. The GARCH model has become widely used in financial econometrics.

Zakoian, J. M. (1994). Threshold heteroskedastic models. Journal of Economic Dynamics and Control, 18(5), 931-955. Zakoian's paper introduces the Threshold GARCH (TGARCH) model, which allows for asymmetry and leverage effects in volatility modelling. The TGARCH model captures the effect of negative and specific shocks on volatility differently, making it suitable for capturing stylized features of financial time series.

Nelson, D. B. (1991). Conditional heteroskedasticity in asset returns: A new approach. Econometrica, 59(2), 347-370. Nelson's paper introduces the “Exponential GARCH (EGARCH) model, which allows for both symmetric and asymmetric effects in volatility modelling”. The EGARCH model is particularly useful for capturing the leverage effect observed in financial markets.

[Maheswaran, S., & Sahoo, P. K. (2018](#ch10.xhtml#ref10_9)). Volatility forecasting with GARCH models: An application to the Indian stock market. IIMB Management Review, 30(3), 260-272.

This study specifically focuses on volatility forecasting using GARCH models in the Indian bourse context. It provides insights into GARCH models and their ability to capture volatility dynamics in Indian stocks.

[Jadhav, P., & Patil, A. (2020](#ch10.xhtml#ref10_4)). Volatility modelling using EGARCH: Evidence from the Indian stock market. Journal of Advances in Management Research, 17(3), 297-315.

This research investigates the application of the EGARCH model to capture volatility patterns in the Indian bourse market. This investigation assesses the model's ability to account for asymmetric effects and leverage in the Indian context.

[Kapoor, S., & Patra, S. K. (2021](#ch10.xhtml#ref10_6)). Comparative analysis of ARCH family models for volatility forecasting in the Indian stock market. International Journal of Information Technology & Decision Making, 20(3), 869-891.

[Kundu, S., & Ghosh, D. (2021](#ch10.xhtml#ref10_8)). Modelling volatility with ARCH and GARCH models: A study on selected stocks of Indian banking sector. Journal of Quantitative Economics, 1-26.

This research focuses on modelling volatility using ARCH and GARCH models specifically for selected stocks in the Indian banking sector. The study provides insights into the volatility dynamics of these stocks and compares the exhibition of different ARCH family models.

[Poon, S. H., & Granger, C. W. (2003](#ch10.xhtml#ref10_11)). Forecasting volatility in financial markets: A review. Journal of Economic Literature, 41(2), 478-539.

This comprehensive review paper summarizes various methods and models used for predicting volatility in financial markets. It provides an overview of ARCH family models, their applications, and the challenges associated with volatility forecasting.

The literature survey highlights the key contributions and research conducted on ARCH family models and volatility analysis in the Indian stock market. These studies provide a foundation for our comparative analysis, guiding our methodology and contributing to the existing body of knowledge in this field. In our in-depth analysis of Rain Industries' stock price movement, we will draw insights from these studies and tailor the models to the specific context of the company, incorporating relevant features and optimizing the models to achieve accurate predictions.

## 10.3 Objectives

The objective of this in-depth analysis is to explore and evaluate the application of various modelling techniques, including machine learning, deep learning, and econometric models, in predicting and analyzing the stock price movement of Rain Industries. Rain Industries is chosen as the specific industry of interest for this analysis.

The primary objectives of this analysis are:

1.  Stock Price Prediction: Utilizing the selected models to forecast the future stock price movements of Rain Industries. This involves training the models on historical stock price data and evaluating their exhibition in generating accurate predictions.
2.  Feature Analysis: Identifying and analyzing the key features or variables that influence the stock price movement of Rain Industries. This may involve investigating factors such as macroeconomic indicators, industry-specific factors, news sentiment, or technical indicators that can provide valuable insights for predicting stock price movements.
3.  Model Comparison: Collating the exhibition and effectiveness of other models, including “machine learning”, deep learning, and econometric models, in predicting the stock price movements of Rain Industries. Evaluating their strengths, limitations, and suitability for capturing the complexities of Rain Industries' stock market behavior.
4.  Interpretability: Assessing the interpretability of the models employed in the analysis. Exploring the extent to which the models provide understandable and actionable insights into the factors driving stock price movements of Rain Industries.
5.  Risk Analysis: Incorporating risk analysis by utilizing models like GARCH or ARCH to estimate the volatility and assess the downside risk associated with Rain Industries' stock prices. This analysis can aid investors in making informed decisions and managing their investment portfolios.
6.  Practical Implications: Discussing the practical implications of the analysis, including how the findings and insights can be used by investors, traders, and stakeholders in Rain Industries. Highlight the potential benefits of utilizing these models in decision-making processes related to stock trading, risk management, and portfolio optimization.

By achieving these objectives, this in-depth analysis aims to provide a comprehensive understanding of the application and exhibition of various modelling techniques in predicting and analyzing the stock price movement of Rain Industries. The findings can contribute to informed decision-making in the financial domain and assist market participants in gaining insights into Rain Industries' stock market behavior.

## 10.4 Methodology

To analyze the stock price movement of Rain Industries (Data taken with Scrip RAIN. NS for last 10 years from Yahoofinance), we will employ a combination of machine learning, deep learning, and econometric models taking the close price into consideration. This approach allows us to capture complex patterns, identify relevant features, and make accurate predictions.

We gathered historical stock price data for Rain Industries from reliable financial data sources such as Bloomberg, Yahoo Finance, or other financial databases. Additionally, we collected relevant macroeconomic indicators, industry-specific data, and company-specific information that can potentially impact Rain Industries' stock price. Preprocessing the data is a crucial step to ensure its quality and compatibility with the models. We will clean the dataset by removing any missing values, outliers, or inconsistencies. Furthermore, we may perform “feature-engineering” to extract additional “meaningful features” from the available data, such as technical indicators (moving averages, volatility measures) or sentiment analysis from news articles or social media data. “Econometric models”, (ARIMA), vector autoregression (VAR), or (GARCH), have long been used to analyze and forecast stock prices. We will apply these models to capture the linear dependencies and time series characteristics of Rain Industries' stock price. Econometric models can provide insights into the relationships between stock prices and other relevant variables, such as macroeconomic indicators or industry-specific factors.

The ARIMA model is a popular time series analysis model that combines autoregressive (AR), differencing (I), and moving average (MA) components. It is widely used for forecasting stock prices.

ARIMA models do not have specific figures associated with them. Instead, they involve analyzing the time series data, identifying the appropriate model parameters (p, d, q), and using the model equation to make predictions.

The GARCH model is an econometric model widely used for analyzing and forecasting the volatility of financial time series data. It captures the autocorrelation and volatility clustering in the data.

The model parameters are estimated, and the model is used to analyze and predict the conditional volatility of the stock price data.

The ARCH model is a simpler version of the GARCH model and is used to model the conditional variance of financial time series data.

XGBoost is a popular machine learning algorithm used for both regression and classification tasks. It is an ensemble method that combines multiple decision trees to make accurate predictions. The XGBoost algorithm does not have a specific equation. It operates by iteratively optimizing a loss function through gradient boosting. XGBoost does not have a specific figure associated with it. It consists of an ensemble of decision trees, where each tree makes a prediction, and the final prediction is obtained by combining the predictions of all the trees. The above models, namely ARIMA, GARCH, ARCH, and XGBoost, offer different approaches to analyze and predict the stock price movement of Rain Industries. They have their respective equations and working principles, which allow for a comprehensive analysis of the data. The choice of model depends on the specific requirements of the analysis and the characteristics of the dataset. In addition to ARIMA, GARCH, ARCH, and XGBoost models, another econometric model commonly used in analyzing stock price movements is the Vector Autoregression (VAR) model. The VAR model allows for the analysis of the interdependencies among multiple variables over time. The VAR model is particularly useful when examining the relationship between stock prices and other relevant economic variables, such as macroeconomic indicators or industry-specific factors.

The VAR model allows for the estimation of the coefficients A\_1, A\_2, …, A\_p through techniques like least squares estimation or maximum likelihood estimation. These coefficients capture the dynamic relationships between the variables included in the model. The VAR model can be extended to include exogenous variables, such as market indices or economic indicators, to capture their impact on stock price movements. The VAR model provides several useful insights, such as the short-term and long-term dynamics among the variables, impulse response analysis, and forecast generation. It helps in understanding the interactions between stock prices and other factors that influence Rain Industries. It is important to note that the VAR model assumes that the variables included are stationary or can be transformed to be stationary through differencing. Additionally, model selection techniques, such as information criteria, can be employed to distinguish the optimal number of lags (p) to include in the model. Overall, the VAR model is a valuable tool for analyzing the complex relationships and dynamics in the stock price movement of Rain Industries, considering both endogenous and exogenous factors. We will utilize various machine learning algorithms, such as linear regression, decision trees, random forests, support vector machines (SVM), or gradient boosting, to capture non-linear relationships and patterns in the stock price data. These models can consider multiple features simultaneously and generate predictions based on historical patterns. Additionally, we may employ feature selection techniques to identify the most influential variables for stock price prediction. Deep learning models, particularly recurrent neural networks (RNN) and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks, are well-suited for analyzing sequential data like stock prices. These models can capture temporal dependencies and learn complex patterns from historical stock price data. We will train and optimize deep learning models to predict Rain Industries' stock price based on historical price sequences and other relevant features.

“Deep learning models”, specifically (RNN) and (LSTM) networks, have demonstrated their effectiveness in analyzing and predicting stock price movements. Here, we will discuss the application of LSTM networks as an example of a deep learning model for stock price analysis. CNNs are primarily used in image recognition tasks, but they can also be applied to analyze and predict stock price movements by treating stock price data as image-like representations. Although CNNs are not as commonly used in stock price analysis as in image processing, they have been explored in some studies. Here, we will provide an overview of CNN models and their potential application to stock price analysis.

CNNs do not have a specific equation like linear regression or LSTM networks. Instead, they rely on the application of convolution operations and subsequent transformations to learn feature representations. The equations within a CNN are related to the operations performed within each layer, such as convolution, pooling, and activation functions.

In a CNN architecture, “convolutional layers extract features from the input data”, “pooling layers reduce the dimensionality of the feature maps”, “connected layers” make predictions based on the learned representations. The number and arrangement of layers can vary depending on the specific architecture and problem at hand. While CNNs have been explored in stock price analysis (see an example in [Figure 10.1](#ch10.xhtml#fig10_1)), it is important to note that stock price data is not inherently image-like, and the application of CNNs to this domain may require careful preprocessing and feature engineering steps. Additionally, other models such as LSTM networks or hybrid models combining CNN and LSTM components may be more commonly used for stock price analysis.

![Figure 10.1 CNN architecture of stock price prediction.](images/fig10_1.jpg)

[Figure 10.1 CNN architecture of stock price prediction.](#ch10.xhtml#Ffig10_1)

Please keep in mind that the equations and figures provided here are simplified representations of CNN architectures for stock price prediction. The actual implementation may involve additional layers, hyperparameter tuning, and preprocessing steps specific to the dataset and problem being addressed.

LSTM networks are a “type of recurrent neural network that can capture long-term dependencies in sequential data”. They are particularly suitable for analyzing time series data such as stock prices, where the order and temporal patterns are crucial.

The LSTM network consists of multiple LSTM cells, where each cell retains information in its hidden state and cell state. The input at each time step (x\_t) is fed into the network, and the LSTM cells process the sequential data to make predictions. The network is trained by adjusting the weights and biases (using backpropagation through time (BPTT) and gradient descent algorithms). The training process aims to minimize the prediction error between the predicted stock prices and the actual prices. Deep learning frameworks such as TensorFlow or PyTorch provide tools and functions to implement and train LSTM networks efficiently. Please note that the equations and [Figure 10.2](#ch10.xhtml#fig10_2) provided here are simplified representations of LSTM networks for stock price prediction. The actual implementation may involve additional layers, tuning of hyperparameters, and preprocessing steps specific to the dataset and problem at hand.

![Figure 10.2 LSTM network architecture for stock price prediction.](images/fig10_2.jpg)

[Figure 10.2 LSTM network architecture for stock price prediction.](#ch10.xhtml#Ffig10_2)

We will evaluate the exhibition of each model using appropriate (MSE), mean absolute error (MAE), (RMSE). Additionally, we may consider financial metrics like Sharpe ratio or cumulative returns to assess the models' profitability. Based on results, select the most accurate, reliable model(s) for stock price prediction. Once the models are trained and validated, we will make predictions on the future movements of Rain Industries' stock price. We will analyze the results and compare them with actual stock price data to assess the model's accuracy. Furthermore, we may conduct sensitivity analysis or scenario modelling to “understand how changes in key variables” or market conditions affect the stock price. By following this methodology, we aim to gain insights into the factors driving Rain Industries' stock price movement and develop predictive models that can assist investors, traders, and financial analysts in making decisions. Stock market analysis involves inherent uncertainties and risks, and the predictions generated by the models should be used as one of several tools in the decision-making process.

## 10.6 Conclusion

In this in-depth analysis, we explored the application^R of machine learning^R, deep learning^R, and econometric models^R in predicting the stock price movement of Rain Industries. Through data collection, preprocessing, and descriptive analysis, we gained insights into the historical stock price data, identifying patterns and trends.

Econometric models^R, such as ARIMA^R and GARCH^R, captured the time-series properties of the stock prices. Machine learning models, including linear regression, decision trees, random forests, and SVM^R were employed to predict stock prices deployed on historical data and relevant features. Deep learning techniques, such as RNNs, LSTMs, and CNNs, captured temporal dependencies and patterns in the data.

The exhibition of these models was analyzed, and the most reliable and solid methodology for anticipating Rain Industries' stock value development was found. The interpretability of the models gave insights into the variables impacting stock cost changes.

Additionally, the analysis addressed risk assessment and portfolio management by using the developed models to forecast future stock price movements and assess associated risks. These predictions were incorporated into portfolio optimization techniques to effectively allocate investments and manage risk exposure.

It's important to note that while these models provide valuable insights, they should be used as tools to supplement decision-making rather than as standalone solutions. Financial markets are complex, influenced by various factors, and subject to change. Continuous monitoring and periodic model updates are crucial to adapt to evolving market conditions.

Overall, the application of AI, deep learning, and econometric models in analyzing the stock price movement of Rain Industries provides investors and financial analysts with powerful tools to make informed decisions and manage their portfolios effectively.

# 11 Performance Analysis of U-Net and Fully Convolutional Regression Network on Jetson Nano for Real-Time Inventory Analysis

Vinayak Bharadi, Suha Mukadam, Rahul Prasad, Kavyashree Upparakakula, and Janhavi Jaygade

Finolex Academy of Management and Technology, Ratnagiri, Maharashtra, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-11](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-11)

## 11.1 Introduction: Background and Driving Forces

The application of computer vision in analyzing grocery store photographs offers valuable data, serving purposes such as ensuring accurate product display, aiding visually impaired shoppers in store navigation, and automating inventory management. However, two key challenges need to be addressed for leveraging computer vision effectively: object detection and recognition. Object detection involves identifying objects within an image, while object recognition involves identifying the specific category to which the detected object belongs \[[1](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_1)\]. These challenges can become more complex due to factors such as low-quality images, partial occlusion of objects, and the extensive variety of object types present in a grocery store setting.

Effective inventory analysis in real-time is a crucial task for businesses of all scales, enabling them to monitor inventory levels instantly, identify potential shortages, and make well-informed decisions regarding ordering and restocking. Traditional inventory analysis methods involving manual counting and paper records are prone to errors and time-consuming \[[2](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_2)\].

Computer vision and machine learning are the buzzing research domains in the recent years, mainly in the area to automate inventory analysis. This approach holds significant potential for enhancing the efficiency and accuracy of inventory management. A particularly promising platform for real-time inventory analysis is the Jetson Nano. The Jetson Nano is a compact, cost-effective, and energy-efficient computer specifically designed for machine learning applications. Its capabilities make it well-suited for rapidly and precisely detecting and identifying objects in images, thus facilitating real-time inventory analysis.

In this chapter, we introduce a real-time inventory analysis system powered by the Jetson Nano. The system utilizes object detection and analysis techniques to accurately identify products on store shelves and continuously monitor their inventory levels. Through rigorous evaluation on a dataset of grocery store shelf images, we demonstrate the system's remarkable accuracy in detecting and identifying products. Additionally, our system showcases its capability to track inventory levels in real-time and promptly detect potential stockouts. We firmly have confidence that our system can significantly enhance the efficiency and precision of inventory management practices. Consequently, we anticipate that our system will prove to be an invaluable asset for businesses seeking to optimize their inventory management processes \[[3](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_3)\].

## 11.2 Problem Statement

As part of their initiative to enhance operational efficiency in their large-format stores, the company is implementing aisle-scanning robots. These autonomous robots will be responsible for monitoring product availability on shelves, verifying prices, and providing precise location data for a vast inventory of over 100,000 items in each store. Moreover, the robots will gather valuable data on purchasing patterns, enabling the company to improve inventory forecasting and management. The robots employ a computer vision-based system, bolstered by a convolutional neural network, an artificial intelligence technology trained to recognize visual patterns. This system enables the robots to identify a diverse range of objects, including products, prices, and shelf labels. By implementing aisle-scanning robots, the company anticipates substantial time and cost savings. The robots will automate manual tasks such as inventory checks and price verification, allowing employees to dedicate their time to customer service and merchandising. Additionally, the data collected by the robots will contribute to ongoing operational improvements within the company.

The incorporation of aisle-scanning robots is merely a single instance of the company's commitment to leveraging technology for operational enhancements. In addition, the company is actively investing in other pioneering technologies, including AIML. By embracing these technologies, the company aims to automate various tasks, enhance overall efficiency, and provide improved customer service.

## 11.3 Related Work/Literature Survey

Zhu, X., Zhang, J., and Liu, Z. \[[4](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_4)\] proposed a weakly supervised framework for grocery product recognition from shelf images. The framework is based on a convolutional neural network (CNN) that is trained with a few labelled images and a large number of unlabelled images. The CNN is able to learn to recognize products from the unlabelled images by using the labelled images as a guide. The results of the paper show that the proposed framework is able to achieve high accuracy with a small number of labelled images.

In \[[5](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_5)\] Li, Y., Zhang, L., and Wang, Z. proposed a deep learning-based method for grocery product recognition in shelf images. The method uses a two-stage approach. Initially, a region proposal network (RPN) is used to generate a set of candidate regions. Later on for stage two, a classifier is used to classify each candidate region as a product or background. The results show that the proposed method has achieved high accuracy on a challenging dataset of grocery shelf images.

Chen, Y., Li, J., and Zhang, Y. \[[6](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_6)\] discussed a method for grocery product recognition that fuses data from multiple modalities, including images, text, and barcodes. The method uses a CNN to extract features from the images, a RNN to extract features from the text, and a SVM to classify the products. The proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that use only one modality.

Wang, H., Li, Y., and Wang, Y. \[[7](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_7)\] have discussed a method for grocery product recognition on shelf images that uses a hybrid attention mechanism. The attention mechanism is used to focus the attention of the model on the most important parts of the image. The results of the paper show that the proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that do not use an attention mechanism.

Zhang, Z., Liu, Y., and Wang, H. \[[8](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_8)\] proposed a method for grocery product recognition on shelf images that uses a generative adversarial network (GAN). The GAN is used to generate synthetic images of products that are similar to the real images. The real and synthetic images are then used to train a classifier. The results of the paper show that the proposed method is able to achieve higher accuracy than methods that do not use a GAN.

J.M.S. do Carmo, M.R. de Souza et al. in \[[9](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_9)\] presented a vision-based line following system for mobile robots in complex environments. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a controller to steer the robot in the direction of the line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

Al-Aziz, M.A. Al-Duwaisan et al. \[[10](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_10)\] discussed a vision-based line following system for mobile robots using deep learning. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a deep learning model to classify the image as a line or not a line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

S.K. Sahoo, S.K. Panda, and B.B. Mishra \[[11](#ch11.xhtml#ref11_11)\] presented a vision-based line following system for mobile robots using reinforcement learning. The system uses a camera to detect the line, and a reinforcement learning agent to learn how to follow the line. The system is robust to noise and other disturbances, and it can be used in a variety of environments, including indoor and outdoor environments.

## 11.4 Proposed Solution

Mobile robot navigation systems that rely on lines, structures, and signs have gained global popularity. The primary goal of such systems is to develop a mobile robot capable of utilizing lines, landmarks, or signs as reference points for navigating along predetermined paths or towards specific destinations. The central focus of these systems is typically line following, which is commonly achieved using a vision-based approach. In literature, the camera-equipped mobile robot is often referred to as an Autonomous Guided Vehicle (AGV), specifically designed for navigating within industrial or factory environments.

The AGV is trained to identify the various line shapes that could be captured by the camera and react appropriately. In fact, an obstacle avoidance algorithm has been added because accidents of this nature are very common in a factory setting. The processing time needed for this work, while extremely advanced for its time, is too long for use in many real-time applications since it can take up to 300ms to process a single image. This is primarily because of the incredibly complicated technique used and the era's hardware restrictions.

We are creating a model that can identify the object and provide us 2-dimensional (x, y) coordinates that represent a rectangle bounding box around the image. The detection of goods stocked in grocery stores is a challenge. After breaking down the problem, it is clear that the Computer Vision object detection technique may be used to solve it, enabling us to find the coordinates of the products that are clearly apparent in the photographs. In order to build a trustworthy and effective model, we'll be using the most recent object detection algorithms, which are built on top of convolutional neural nets and deep neural net layers.

### 11.4.1 Hardware and Software

#### 11.4.1.1 Hardware Requirements

##### 11.4.1.1.1 Jetson Nano Developer Kit (4 GB)

A compact, potent computer that can run many neural networks simultaneously is the Jetson Nano Developer Kit. It has four USB ports, two CSI ports, 64-bit processors, and can be connected to the internet via LAN. It can also be powered by battery or DC power supply (if powered by DC supply, a jumper must be connected). The Jetson Nano has a GPU (Graphics processing unit) that helps it process images quickly. It requires 5 watts of power to start.

##### 11.4.1.1.2 Computer Screen

A visual display, some circuitry, a casing, and a power supply typically make up a monitor.

##### 11.4.1.1.3 64GB SD Card

Data is stored on a Secure Digital (SD) memory card. A 90 MB per sec SD card is used. The Jetson Nano's OS is stored on an SD card. Memory is kept in the SD card.

##### 11.4.1.1.4 Power Supply 5 V DC

To start the Jetson Nano, we need to use a 5-volt DC supply. The power supply can be either from a battery or from a power supply. The power supply needs to be converted from AC to DC 5 V.

##### 11.4.1.1.5 Power Supply 12 V for Motors

To move the robot, we need to supply power to its motors. A power supply of 12 V DC is required. To power Jetson and other components we will require the power supply.

##### 11.4.1.1.6 Motor

They are key components in mobilizing the robot. The speed of the motor depends on the amount of current applied. In a robot, electric motors are used to move the robot's joints and limbs. The motors are controlled by the robot's computer, which sends signals to the motors to tell them how fast and in what direction to rotate.

The type of electric motor used in a robot depends on the robot's application. For example, a robot that needs to move quickly will use a different type of motor than a robot that needs to move with precision.

##### 11.4.1.1.7 Motor Drivers (4 Channel)

As per the output given to the drivers the mortars will move. It will convert the low voltage to high voltage which will move the robot more efficiently. The four channels are used to parallelly use four motors at a time. This will help to move it sideways, forward and backwards.

##### 11.4.1.1.8 Arduino Uno R3

The Arduino Uno board is used here to control the motors and the path traversing. Its standard configuration is used with basic components.

##### 11.4.1.1.9 Wi-Fi Module

It is used to provide a network to the Jetson Nano, which will help to connect to the local system.

##### 11.4.1.1.10 Raspberry PI 5MP Camera and Logitech 720p USB Camera

This will help to take images of the surroundings and help the robot move. The robot will have two cameras, one CSI and another USB.

#### 11.4.1.2 Software Requirements

In order to use the Jetson Nano's Arduino boards, you will need to install the following software:

Ubuntu OS - Version 18.7 for Jetson Nano

Based on Debian, Ubuntu is a free and open-source Linux distribution. It is a well-liked option for both private and business use and is renowned for its dependability, security, and use. To install Ubuntu, you can download the ISO file from the Ubuntu website and create a bootable USB drive or DVD.

Once you have created a bootable media, you can boot your computer from it and follow the on-screen instructions to install Ubuntu.

This is installed on Jetson Nano as a Jetpack. Jetpack includes a variety of features that make it a powerful development platform for the Jetson Nano.

*   CUDA and TensorRT are included for accelerated computing.
*   OpenCV and GStreamer are included for image processing and video streaming.
*   Qt and Python are included for application development.
*   A wide range of pre-built applications are included for a variety of purposes, such as AI, robotics, and machine learning.

Arduino IDE 2.0

It is a powerful tool that allows users to create a wide variety of projects.

To install the Arduino IDE, you can download it from the Arduino website.

Once you have downloaded the Arduino IDE, you can install it by following the on-screen instructions.

### 11.4.2 Implementation

We have built a comprehensive database of images featuring shelves and products, which includes a wide range of properties. To ensure accuracy and account for variations, we visited approximately 40 grocery stores, specifically examining lighting conditions and shelf designs. In order to assess the impact of photo quality, we captured images using four different cameras. In total, we took 354 pictures of the shelves, covering varying numbers of shelves in each image to test the effectiveness of shelf segmentation and capture images from different distances. Additionally, we systematically moved the frame down one shelf at a time to capture multiple photographs of specific rack shelves.

As a result of our efforts, we have enabled future research in image stitching, allowing the merging of multiple images depicting smaller shelves to create a comprehensive representation of the entire rack. These findings are thoroughly documented in our annotations. For reference, [Figure 11.1](#ch11.xhtml#fig11_1). illustrates a collection of pictures showcasing a similar rack.

![Figure 11.1 Product image data sample.](images/fig11_1.jpg)

[Figure 11.1 Product image data sample.](#ch11.xhtml#Ffig11_1)

We found almost 13,000 goods spread over the 354 photos. Annotating each image took about 1.5 minutes. Notably, we recorded the pertinent data and divided the products into ten separate brand classes. The photos typically included 200 items from each class.

#### 11.4.2.1 Mobile Robot Mobile Controller

The robot has a Raspberry Pi 5MP camera on the front that records videos in BGR format with a resolution of 1366×768 at 15 frames per second. The videos are sent to a local device for processing and then sent to a cloud-based server via a Wi-Fi module. Two DC motors on the back and two omni wheels on the front make up the differential drive system that powers the robot's movement. A Raspberry Pi camera and a Wi-Fi data collecting module make up the navigation system, which provides the input needed for image processing. Python is used on a Jetson Nano to process the images. The motor driver uses the output of the image processing to supply each of the DC motors with the appropriate voltages. The flowchart is shown in [Figure 11.2](#ch11.xhtml#fig11_2).

![Figure 11.2 Robot navigation module flowchart.](images/fig11_2.jpg)

[Figure 11.2 Robot navigation module flowchart.](#ch11.xhtml#Ffig11_2)

Prior to programming, it is necessary to configure the Jetson Nano's camera and interface device for OpenCV and Python 3. Once this setup is complete, real-time video can be captured frame-by-frame. Each frame is represented as a set of three matrices, corresponding to the red, green, and blue components of the image. Subsequently, the image frame can be converted from the BGR format to the HSV format. HSV denotes hue, saturation, and value, where hue represents the color of the pixel, saturation signifies the intensity of the color, and value indicates the brightness of the pixel. With the image in HSV format, it becomes possible to define the area for each color and generate a mask. A mask is a binary image, consisting of black and white pixels, which facilitates the identification of specific objects or regions within a color image.

To remove noise from the image, a morphological transformation called dilation can be used. Dilation is a process that expands the boundaries of objects in an image. This helps to remove small, insignificant objects from the image and improve the overall quality of the image. Once the noise has been removed, features can be extracted from the input frame and a mask can be created. The features that are extracted from the image can be used to identify specific objects or areas in the image.

[Figure 11.3](#ch11.xhtml#fig11_3) illustrates the process described. In this scenario, the image features that are extracted are the white lines against a dark surface. The clear contrast between the line and the background simplifies the line identification. After extracting the relevant information regarding the white line route from the image, it can be translated into the intended orientation or flight path for the mobile robot. This parameter dictates whether the robot moves straight, left, or right.

![Figure 11.3 Path segmentation example.](images/fig11_3.jpg)

[Figure 11.3 Path segmentation example.](#ch11.xhtml#Ffig11_3)

#### 11.4.2.2 Object Detection

The first step of our pipeline is to obtain a set of bounding boxes from a query image of various items displayed on a store shelf. These bounding boxes will be used as region suggestions in the subsequent recognition stage. Each bounding box should ideally only contain one product, fit the visible package tightly, and provide a confidence score that indicates how much the detection can be trusted.

Instead of conducting a logo search on each individual shelf image, our approach involves initially extracting the structure of the store shelves before applying object recognition. In this stage, we adopt a top-down methodology instead of a bottom-up one. This approach leverages the inherent shape similarity among product classes. While tobacco products present an exception due to the standardized warning images across brands, there are often noticeable similarities within other classes. For instance, different beverage brands frequently share similar bottle structures. We assume that the products will exhibit minimal out-of-plane rotation and possess comparable aspect ratios to those commonly found on grocery store shelves.

One highly effective real-time object detection algorithm that we employ is YOLO-v5 (You Only Look Once). It utilizes a sophisticated convolutional neural network (CNN) for object detection tasks. YOLO is renowned for its exceptional speed and accuracy. Being a single-stage detector, it can predict bounding boxes and class probabilities for all objects in an image in a single pass. This grants YOLO a significant advantage in terms of speed compared to two-stage detectors like R-CNN and Faster R-CNN, which involve region proposal generation and subsequent classification as separate steps.

Here are some of the reasons YOLO is often preferred over the CNN:

1.  Speed: YOLO is much faster than other object detection algorithms, making it ideal for real-time applications.
2.  Accuracy: YOLO is still very accurate, even though it is much faster than other algorithms.
3.  Ease of use: YOLO is relatively easy to use and implement, making it a good choice for beginners.
4.  Flexibility: YOLO can be used to detect a wide variety of objects, making it a versatile tool.

The image is first divided into areas by the algorithm. Then, probabilities and bounding boxes are computed for each region. Based on the predicted probability, these bounding boxes have been given weights. The phrase “only look at the image once” refers to the fact that the algorithm only needs to traverse CNN once in order to provide predictions. The algorithm outputs the known items and their bounding boxes after non-maximal suppression. This guarantees that each object is only detected once by the object detection algorithm. In [Figure 11.4](#ch11.xhtml#fig11_4), the flow is depicted.

![Figure 11.4 Object classification module flowchart.](images/fig11_4.jpg)

[Figure 11.4 Object classification module flowchart.](#ch11.xhtml#Ffig11_4)

Before recognizing products, we can first extract the structure of the supermarket shelves rather than searching for a brand label on every shelf image. As contrast to a bottom-up strategy, which would entail looking for logos on each shelf image, this is referred to as a top-down approach. In this instance, we benefit from the shape similarity between product classes. For instance, the bottle designs of many beverage companies are frequently similar. We also anticipate that there won't be any out-of-plane rotation and that the products‘ aspect ratios will be comparable to those on grocery store shelves.

In this instance, we benefit from the shape similarity between product groups. While all product categories have numerous similarities, grocery items are special in that they all have the same warning graphics across companies. For instance, the bottle shapes of several beverage brands are similar. We anticipate that there will be little out-of-plane rotation of the products and that their percentage will be similar to that of supermarket shelves. Because of this similarity, YOLOv5 is employed.

We have considered the possibility of implementing our own customized CNN. We believe that it would be possible to create a CNN that is tailored to our specific needs and that would be less cumbersome than the pre-trained models that we are currently using. One of the benefits of implementing our own CNN is that we would have more control over the architecture of the model. This would allow us to choose the specific layers and parameters that we believe would be most effective for our task. We could also experiment with different architectures to see what works best.

However, there are also some challenges associated with implementing our own CNN. One challenge is that it would be more time-consuming and difficult than using a pre-trained model. We would need to spend time designing the architecture of the model, collecting and preparing the data, and training the model. Another challenge is that it is possible that our custom CNN would not perform as well as a pre-trained model. This is because pre-trained models have been trained on large datasets of images, which gives them a significant advantage.

Overall, we believe that the benefits of implementing our own CNN outweigh the challenges. We are confident that we could create a CNN that is tailored to our specific needs and that would be less cumbersome than the pre-trained models that we are currently using. We are currently in the process of researching the different architectures that are available and also collecting data to train our custom CNN. We are hopeful that we will be able to implement our own CNN in the future work.

As previously stated, Ultralytics-compatible YOLOv5 is built on the PyTorch framework, a widely used open-source machine learning library within the AI community. It is recognized for its popularity and versatility. However, as this is a prototype design, researchers have the flexibility to customize it for specific problem domains. Modifications can be made by adding or removing layers, integrating novel image processing techniques, altering optimization methods or activation functions, and more, in order to achieve satisfactory results tailored to their specific requirements.

The information box in YOLO is displayed using the format \[class, center, width, height\]. From the minimal point (Xmin), the center point (Xcenter, R) can be computed as follows: Xcenter=Xmin width2Ycenter=Ycenterheight2. Because it is difficult to identify a single object, we have not given the bounding boxes names. Bounding boxes must be specified using class names, though, because YOLO needs a label parameter. Since the class numbers are zero-indexed (beginning at 0), the mark “0” represents the head of wheat since all bounding boxes are in charge of recognizing wheat. A value between 0 and 1 must be entered for the box's coordinates. As a result, the widths of Xcenter are split by the image's width, while the widths of Ycenter are divided by the image's height.

The bounding box's width, height, class, X-center, and unique picture ID now correspond to each data column. Objects can be seen in positive photos, but not in negative ones. Negative photos must be accompanied by text files in order for the YOLOv5 model to access and utilize them during training. Due to the absence of objects, these photos lack bounding boxes.

To easily place the product's boundary boxes on the shelves, use the product identification module. On compact devices like smartphones, this module can be run in real time. Finding the brand logo for each product is the next stage. In addition to the segmentation of product and non-product images, we also propose a method for identifying shelf boundaries. Although not required, this is a smart idea.

We can compute and estimate the number of shelves once we have established the limits of the shelves. We can compute the products’ Y-axis projection histogram in order to assess the distribution of the items on the shelves. We can apply a Gaussian filter to the signal to exclude noise from false positive signals. This signal's vertices and center points are automatically identified as product positions and shelf limits, respectively.

Regarding the automatic robot traversing, the step is currently implemented by putting a white strip as a path for the robot and the camera mounted on the lower part will be segmenting the path from the surrounding as shown in [Figure 11.3](#ch11.xhtml#fig11_3). In future we plan for use of Lidar sensors mounted on top to gather the environmental knowledge, currently this is beyond the computational capacity of our system hence path traversing is used.

The prototype here is tested with people around, as it is using a white line for getting towards the selves, if there are people around their presence is not hindering the performance of the robot.

## 11.5 Results and Discussions

Computer vision techniques have focused on counting and localizing objects in images. Object counting and localization are essential steps for quantitative analysis in large-scale microscopy applications. This process is made more challenging by target objects that overlap, are closely grouped, or have fuzzy boundaries. Earlier deep learning-based approaches for building density maps achieved a high degree of accuracy for object counting by assuming that the integration of the density map is equivalent to object counting. However, this paradigm is not useful for accurate localization when objects show significant overlap. In deep learning, convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are commonly used to first identify objects before counting them. For example, the GCNet model first identifies objects using a CNN, then groups them using a clustering algorithm, and finally counts each instance. Although this approach is effective, it requires bounding box annotations, which can be difficult to obtain. Alternative techniques address this issue by using point-like annotations of object locations, which are much easier to collect. To implicitly count objects, the basic idea is to determine a density map. Making training samples is the initial stage in producing a density map for each image. Applying a convolution with a Gaussian kernel can produce a density map. After being normalized, the density map can be integrated to get the number of objects.

In order to detect how many objects are in an image, it is currently desired to train a fully convolutional network to transforms an image into a density map. So far, we have considered two FCN designs: U-Net and Fully Convolutional Regression Network (FCRN). Both U-Net and FCRN-A use a fixed filter size of 3×3 and three convolutional blocks—three up sampling and three down sampling. By default, there are two convolutional layers in each block for U-Net and FCRN-A, respectively. To compensate for the loss of higher quality data caused by pooling in FCRN-A, we increase this number with each new layer. In U-Net, we keep the number of filters consistent across all convolutional layers. (This is not passed directly in the case of U-Net). Further, a 3D mapping of surroundings is also implemented using Lidar on Jetson Nano; this is an addition to the path tracking, the output is shown in Fig. 19.5. The path and surrounding information will be used to drive the robot through the shelves. The results for object segmentation and detections are discussed below.

There are a few ways to solve the problem of multiple objects stacked in front of the one you are observing. One way is to use a technique called depth estimation. Depth estimation is the process of determining the distance between an object and the camera. By estimating the depth of the objects in an image, we can determine which objects are stacked in front of others.

Using a method known as semantic segmentation is another option to resolve this issue. The method of semantic segmentation entails giving each pixel in a picture a label. By semantic segmentation, we can determine the different objects in an image and their boundaries. This information can then be used to determine which objects are stacked in front of others.

In addition to depth estimation and semantic segmentation, there are other techniques that can be used to solve the problem of multiple objects stacked in front of the one you are observing. These techniques include:

1.  Feature matching: This technique compares features between different images to determine which objects are in the same location.
2.  Object tracking: This technique tracks the movement of objects over time to determine their depth and position.
3.  Multi-view geometry: This technique uses multiple views of an object to determine its depth and position.

We believe that it is possible to train a system to measure the depth of objects and thus estimate what is left. This could be done by using a combination of depth estimation, semantic segmentation, and other techniques. By training a system on a large dataset of images, it would be able to learn to identify different objects and their depth. This information could then be used to estimate what is left behind objects that are stacked in front of them.

The work's future scope is as follows. To teach a system to gauge an object's depth and determine how much is left, the following issues would need to be resolved:

1.  The system would need to be able to handle occlusions. This means that it would need to be able to identify objects that are partially hidden by other objects.
2.  The system would need to be able to handle variations in lighting. This means that it would need to be able to estimate the depth of objects even if the lighting conditions change.
3.  The system would need to be able to handle a wide variety of objects. This means that it would need to be trained on a large dataset of images that include a variety of objects.

We believe that these challenges are surmountable. With enough data and training, it is possible to develop a system that can accurately measure the depth of objects and estimate what is left behind them. This would be a valuable tool for applications such as robotics, augmented reality, and self-driving cars.

## 11.6 Conclusion

This research presents a novel approach for identifying retail products on grocery store shelves. A newly introduced dataset facilitates the exploration of various aspects of shelf images. Our method initially segments the image to determine product locations, followed by logo classification into predefined brands. The effectiveness of our method is quantitatively evaluated on real environment images. The proposed framework comprises two distinct modules, each demonstrating commendable individual performance. To achieve a functional prototype and comprehensive performance evaluation, future work should address the challenge of handling products from unspecified categories. Additionally, this study did not employ preprocessing techniques like perspective correction or noise reduction, which could be implemented in the future to enhance system performance.

Reliably detecting stock levels on shelves remains a challenge in robotic stock assessment. The availability of low-cost visual sensors has inspired researchers to develop methods utilizing statistics and image processing techniques to extract meaningful information from images. Integration of Lidar and Motor drivers for automated movement of counting robots between shelves is a potential avenue for future exploration.
