# 15 Identification of Rice Leaf Disease Using Gaussian Mixture Model A Machine Learning Approach Using Image Classification Techniques

Binoy Sasmal

Research Scholar, Department of Computer Science & Engineering, National Institute of Technology, Itanagar, Arunachal Pradesh, India

Rajat Subhra Goswami

Associate Professor, Department of Computer Science & Engineering, National Institute of Technology, Itanagar, Arunachal Pradesh, India

Manas Pratim Dutta

Associate Professor, Department of Computer Science & Information Technology, Cotton University, Guwahati, Assam, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-15](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-15)

## 15.1 Introduction

A disease in a plant can be identified as when it is not healthy or when it is unable to operate normally. Diseases in plants are a widespread problem which impacts the overall growth of the plant and its general health. Numerous things, such as bacterial, viral, and fungal pathogens, environmental stressors, and nutrient deficiencies, can contribute to the development of these diseases \[[1](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_1),[2](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_2)\].

Some signs of leaf diseases are abnormality in the leaf structure, withering, browning, spots of black or brown color on the leaf, etc. If these diseases are left untreated, they may lead to various problems such as low yield, low growth rate and even death in some cases. Identification of the underlying cause and use of appropriate control methods, such as cultural practices, chemical treatments, and biological controls, are crucial for the prevention and management of leaf diseases \[[3](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_3),[4](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_4)\].

### 15.1.1 Types of Leaf Diseases

Below are some of the most common types of diseases which are found in plants \[[5](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_5),[6](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_6),[7](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_7)\]:

### 15.1.2 Brown Spot

One of the most common diseases in plants is the brown spot disease; it is caused mainly by the fungal pathogen, Septoria spp. This fungal pathogen produces a brown, circular or angular spot on the leaves of the infected plants. These patches could have a golden halo around the perimeter and a dark brown center.

The most favored condition for brown spot disease is warm, humid weather conditions and it can spread rapidly under these conditions. [Figure 15.1](#ch15.xhtml#fig15_1) shows rice leaf infected with brown spot. The fungus overwinters on plant debris from sick plants, and during wet weather, spores are formed which can spread the disease to other plants. Brown spot disease can result in defoliation, slowed plant growth, and decreased production if left untreated.

![Figure 15.1 Brown spot in rice plant.](images/fig15_1.jpg)

[Figure 15.1 Brown spot in rice plant.](#ch15.xhtml#Ffig15_1)

### 15.1.3 Leaf Smut

Leaf smut disease is a type of fungal disease which affects various types of crops like corn, wheat, barley, etc. This disease is caused by the fungi Entyloma spp., which causes dark, irregular-shaped smut galls or pustules on the leaves of infected plants.

The yellowing or browning of leaves, stunted growth, and decreased yield are all signs of leaf smut disease. In extreme situations, the illness may result in the plant dying before its time.

Leaf smut disease is caused by spores of fungi which spread through the help of wind or rain splash. Here [Figure 15.2](#ch15.xhtml#fig15_2) represents leaf smut disease in a leaf. The fungus can endure these conditions for several years by overwintering in soil or plant debris that has been infected.

![Figure 15.2 Leaf smut disease in a leaf.](images/fig15_2.jpg)

[Figure 15.2 Leaf smut disease in a leaf.](#ch15.xhtml#Ffig15_2)

### 15.1.4 Bacterial Leaf Blight

The bacterial pathogen *Xanthomonas campestris* is responsible for the widespread plant disease known as bacterial leaf blight. It affects a wide range of plants, including vegetables, citrus, rice, and soybeans.

Bacterial leaf blight can be characterized by water-soaked lesions on the leaves, which can swiftly turn brown or black and occasionally have a yellow halo around the edge. In [Figure 15.3](#ch15.xhtml#fig15_3), it has been shown that the leaf is infected with bacterial leaf blight. In extreme circumstances, the leaves may turn necrotic and pass away.

![Figure 15.3 Bacterial leaf bight disease in leaf.](images/fig15_3.jpg)

[Figure 15.3 Bacterial leaf bight disease in leaf.](#ch15.xhtml#Ffig15_3)

This disease is mostly spread by wind, rain splash, or through contact with dead plant debris which were previously infected with a similar disease. Controlling the illness is challenging because it can persist for years in plant debris or soil \[[6](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_6)\].

### 15.1.5 Rust

A fungus that affects plants and results in patches on their leaves that are yellow, orange, or reddish-brown. It is frequently brought on by high humidity and moisture levels and can damage a wide variety of plant species.

### 15.1.6 Powdery Mildew

A fungus that affects plants and results in a white or grey powdery coating on their leaves, stems, and flowers. Wide-ranging plant species are susceptible to it, which is frequently brought on by high humidity and inadequate air circulation.

### 15.1.7 Anthracnose

A fungus that results in dark or black blemishes on plant leaves. It can damage many different plant types and is frequently brought on by rainy weather.

### 15.1.8 Regions in India Where Diseases Are Mostly Found

In India, leaf diseases can affect plants because they are influenced by a variety of elements, including weather, plant species, soil characteristics, and management techniques. However, leaf diseases are more common in some parts of India than others.

These are the humid and wet regions of coastal areas such as the western and eastern coasts that is, Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Odisha, etc. These regions are more prone to leaf diseases caused by bacterial and fungal pathogens. In contrast, dry regions with low humidity like Rajasthan, Gujrat, and parts of Maharashtra suffer from a variety of leaf diseases which are caused by environmental factors like scarcity of water and high temperature, soil, etc. \[[7](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_7)\].

### 15.1.9 How Do These Diseases Affect the Plant

Leaf diseases can affect the plant in a variety of ways depending on what type of disease it is and how much severity of the infection. Some of the ways the disease can affect the plant are:

### 15.1.10 Wilting and Yellowing

There are several leaf illnesses that can make leaves wilt or turn yellow. This frequently happens because of harm to the plant's vascular tissue, which can obstruct the flow of nutrients and water. This also affects the photosynthesis of the plant which is essential for production of energy in a plant.

### 15.1.11 Stunted Growth

In extreme circumstances, leaf diseases might result in the plant's mortality or restricted growth. This might happen if the illness spreads to other sections of the plant or if it affects a sizable portion of the leaves.

### 15.1.12 Reduced Yield

Some leaf diseases can also reduce the overall yield of the plant, particularly if the disease occurs in the early stages of growth of the plant.

### 15.1.13 Lesions

Some leaf diseases can cause lesions which may be seen as small spots or larger patches on the surface of the leaves. These lesions can cause tissue necrosis or death of plant cells, which can weaken the plant's overall ability to produce and grow.

## 15.2 Related Work

The concept of smart farming is gaining popularity in the agricultural industry, as it uses various sensors and machine learning technologies to improve the efficiency of the farm. According to a survey, over 50% of the industry is experiencing significant losses due to the emergence of diseases on plant leaves. It's important to monitor and prevent the spread of these diseases in order to boost agricultural yields. We can use algorithms to detect diseases on leaves. In this study, we compare the three different models that are used in this field. The CNN model is able to perform better than the SVM and KNN models when it comes to detecting various leaf diseases. When trained with images of soybean leaf diseases, the CNN model is able to achieve an accuracy rate of 96%. It performs better than the SVM and KNN models, which have an accuracy rate of 76% and 64%, respectively \[[1](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_1)\].

The growth of India's population and increasing food demands have resulted in the need for more agricultural activities. This sector plays a vital role in the country's economy. Unfortunately, the lack of yield can be caused by various diseases, such as fungi, bacteria, and viruses. Detection techniques can help prevent these diseases from affecting the crop. In order to identify and detect plant diseases, machine learning techniques are commonly used. These techniques mainly rely on the collected information and offer various effective and efficient methods. For instance, they can analyses data for a specific task and provide a superior result. This paper aims to review the various techniques that are used in the detection of plant diseases using deep learning and machine learning. In addition, deep learning has gained immense popularity in the field of computer vision. This technology has the potential to provide a superior performance in detecting plant diseases. The various applications of deep learning have been widely studied and adopted in the computer vision and machine learning domains. The paper presents a comparative analysis of the two techniques, focusing on their performance in different research papers. Deep learning models can be utilized to identify and detect leaf diseases, which can help prevent major crop losses \[[2](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_2)\].

Economic losses can be caused by post-harvest diseases of apple. To help prevent these diseases from affecting the apple industry, we developed a web-based diagnostic system called DSSApple. This tool allows users to diagnose different types of apple diseases based on their observed symptoms. Through an image-based stream, the user can interact with the system to visualize the various symptoms of diseases that affect different apple varieties. The user can easily interact with the system by choosing the pictures that represent the various symptoms of the diseases. Through the system's expert-based stream, users can collect feedback about the various characteristics of the diseases that affect a target apple. This method is powered by the use of the Bayesian Networks framework. In order to provide a more accurate and human-friendly diagnosis, we developed a knowledge base that is built on the basis of the observations collected by the system. Through the use of the BN framework, we were able to process the conflicting user feedback and provide explanations for the suggested diagnoses. In two studies, the hybrid approach was thoroughly evaluated. The suggested hybrid version of DSSApple performed better than the single streams and user intuition in terms of accuracy \[[3](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_3),[4](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_4)\].

The sugarcane aphid, which is known to cause severe yield loss in the US, is a major threat to the production of sorghum. Although the management of this pest is dependent on the economic threshold levels and monitoring, scouting for it under field conditions is often inefficient and time-consuming. To help improve the efficiency of the program, we developed a deep learning model that can automatically identify the sugarcane aphid on leaves based on their density. Through a combination of field scouting and deep learning techniques, we were able to analyze the performance of four classification models. The models included Inception, Resnet 50, Xception, and the DenseNet 121. Based on the standard criteria for spraying, the models were able to classify the sugarcane aphid into six classes. No sugarcane aphids were found on any plant, and no threat was detected below an action or treatment threshold. They were also able to identify the sugarcane aphids that were above an economic threshold, and they were able to manage them in field conditions. Xception and Inception v3 performed well with an overall accuracy of 86% and a reduced number of misclassified entries. The models were also able to correctly classify the sugarcane aphids at least 97% of the time, demonstrating their potential to be used in remote sensing and sampling protocols. These technologies can help researchers and growers identify and monitor the sugarcane aphids in various resistant and susceptible varieties of sorghum. They can also provide recommendations on the appropriate application of pesticides \[[7](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_7),[8](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_8)\].

The economic losses caused by post-harvest diseases of apples can be severe. To help prevent these diseases from affecting the apple industry, we developed DSSApple, an online decision support system that allows users to diagnose and monitor the status of these diseases based on their observed symptoms. This tool is a hybrid diagnostic that can be used by both non-expert and expert users. The system's image-based stream allows users to interact with the system by selecting pictures of various types of diseases. It then compiles user feedback about the disease's characteristics. This method is carried out through a series of questions that are related to the observed symptoms of the disease. The system's expert-based reasoning framework is based on the principles of Bayesian Networks. In this paper, we talk about the steps involved in building a knowledge base for the system. Through the use of the Bayesian Network, we can also process conflicting or incomplete feedback and provide human-readable explanations for the suggested diagnoses. Two studies were conducted on the proposed hybrid approach of DSSApple, which involved simulated and real infected apples. The results of these studies revealed that the proposed system performed better than the single streams and user intuition when it came to diagnosing diseases \[[9](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_9)\].

The diagnosis and treatment of plant diseases are very important for farmers as they affect the quality and yield of their crops. Unfortunately, visual inspection is often not feasible for most farmers due to the complexity of the situation. An intelligent machine-vision system can help them perform effective and efficient crop inspections. A simple method for identifying and treating plant diseases is to rely on minimal information collected from the images. In a recent study, a new image processing algorithm was used to diagnose and classify different diseases of grapes. The classification process was performed by separating the symptoms from the healthy parts of a leaf using K-means clustering. The features were then extracted from three different color models. The results of the study were then analyzed using the SVM algorithm. The relief features were then selected based on their importance. The suggested method was compared against two deep learning methods: GoogleNet and CNN. The former was able to achieve a classification accuracy of 94.05% and 86.82%, while the latter was able to achieve a classification accuracy of 97.97%. Compared to these two methods, the proposed method had a shorter processing time \[[10](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_10),[11](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_11)\].

## 15.3 Use of Machine Learning for Early Detection

To prevent these harmful diseases Machine Learning techniques are used for early detection. The use of machine learning helps the farmers to detect an abnormality early and more accurately, thus helping them to reduce the overall yield and spread of these diseases to other plantations \[[8](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_8),[9](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_9)\].

One of the most common approaches used nowadays is to create a model using machine learning; in this method images from healthy plants and diseased plants are accrued as data. Then the model can be used to classify new images of plant leaves whether they are diseased or healthy based on the visual characteristics of the processed leaves. The data can be accrued through various methods like remote sensing, field survey, or through a smartphone-based application. [Figure 15.4](#ch15.xhtml#fig15_4) shows the type of machine learning approaches.

![Figure 15.4 Type of machine learning approaches.](images/fig15_4.jpg)

[Figure 15.4 Type of machine learning approaches.](#ch15.xhtml#Ffig15_4)

Deep learning methods such as Convolution Neural Network (CNN) are also effective for detection of diseases in plant leaves. This method can learn more complex features from the provided images thus it can identify more subtle differences between the diseases and healthy leaves \[[12](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_12)\].

### 15.3.1 Methods Used Previously in India

In India, traditional methods for the detection of leaf diseases in plants have included visual inspection by farmers or trained agronomists. The expert would visit the field and then check for visible symptoms of diseases like discoloration, deformity, or lesions. Though this method is effective, it's a time-consuming method and not all abnormalities could be see with the naked eye, so it is susceptible to errors.

Other traditional methods used are the use of chemical sprays or pesticides. To stop or slow the spread of illness, this strategy treats plants chemically. Though, if not used appropriately this technology can be expensive and could have a harmful effect on the environment.

In recent years, a new molecular diagnostic method known as Polymerase chain reaction (PCR) is used for disease detection in leaves. This method involves the amplification and detection of specific DNA sequences from tissue samples, thus, detecting the microorganism responsible for causing the disease at the molecular level. However, this approach can be expensive and necessitates specialized training and laboratory equipment \[[10](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_10)\].

Though the traditional methods are all effective for detection of leaf disease they can be time consuming, costly and might not be so accurate. Thus, with the introduction of machine learning methods it is more cost effective with more accuracy and speed.

### 15.3.2 Vertical Farming Role in Machine Learning

The method where the crops are grown vertically in layers while utilizing A.I controlled lightning and climate control systems to achieve the best possible growing conditions is known as vertical farming. This method of farming has several potential advantages, such as higher crop yields, less water use, and enhanced food security.

Optimizing the growing conditions in vertical farming systems can benefit from machine learning. Machine learning algorithms may gather data on variables like temperature, humidity, and light levels using sensors and other monitoring tools, and then utilize this data to modify the growing environment in real time \[[13](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_13)\].

## 15.4 Gaussian Matrix Module (GMM)

A Gaussian Matrix Model is a type of mathematical model which involves with the study of random matrices where the entries are distributed according to the Gaussian probability distribution. So, it can be said that the GMM is a random matrix model where the entries of the matrix are independent and identically distributed Gaussian random variables.

GMM is often used as a tool for understanding complex structure, to make the correct prediction and to run simulations where the initial process is too complex to be modeled analytically \[[14](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_14),[15](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_15),[16](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_16)\].

### 15.4.1 Probability Density Function

GMM works on the assumption that the data points are generated by a mixture of K Gaussian distributions, where K is a user-specified value. A cluster in the data is represented by a Gaussian distribution, and the GMM model calculates the probability that each data point belongs to each cluster. Blending coefficients, which represent the proportions of each Gaussian in the mixture, are also estimated by the model.

The probability density function of the GMM model is the sum of the probability density functions of each Gaussian distribution, weighted by the associated mixing coefficients. The GMM model can be represented mathematically as follows:

\[*N*(*x*;\_*i*,\_*i*)*p*(*x*)=\_*i**K* *w*\_*i*\**N*(*x*;\_*i*,\_*i*)\] (15.1)

where {p(x)} is the GMM model's probability density function, {w\_i} is the i-th Gaussian's mixing coefficient, {N(x; \_i, \_i)} is the i-th Gaussian's probability density function with mean{\_i} and covariance matrix {\_i}, and (K) is the number of Gaussians in the mixture. The sum of all the mixing coefficients is equal to one, i.e., \[Σ\_i^K w\_i = 1\].

### 15.4.2 Training the GMM

Expectation-maximization (EM) algorithms are commonly used to train GMM models. It is an iterative algorithm between computing the posterior probabilities of data points belonging to each cluster (the E step) and updating the changing parameters of the Gaussian and mixture coefficients (M step). The EM method starts with initial estimates of the Gaussian and mixture coefficient parameters and iteratively tunes the parameters until convergence.

### 15.4.3 Expectation Stage

Step E of the EM algorithm computes the posterior probabilities of the data points belonging to each cluster, based on the current parameter estimates. The following probability of data point x belonging to the i-th cluster is given by Bayes rule:

*γ*\_*i*(*x*) = *w*\_*i* \* *N*(*x*;\_*i*,\_*i*) /*p*(*x*) (15.2)

where {p(x)} is the information point's generally likelihood thickness work and {\_i(x)} is the back likelihood of x having a place to the i-th cluster. The back probabilities are at that point utilized to overhaul the blending coefficient estimations.

### 15.4.4 Maximization Stage

Given current estimates of the following probabilities, step M of the EM method updates the estimates of the Gaussian parameter and the mixing coefficient. The mean value of the i-th Gaussian is updated as follows:

\[*μ*\_*i* = ∑\_*j* ∧ *n* *γ*\_*i*(*x*\_*j*) \* *x*\_*j*/∑\_*j* ∧ *n* *γ*\_*i*(*x*\_*j*)\] (15.3)

where n is the total number of data points, and x\_j is the j-th data point.

The covariance matrix of the i-th Gaussian is updated as:

\[*Σ*\_*i* = *Σ*\_*j* ∧ *n* *γ*\_*i*(*x*\_*j*) \* (*x*\_*j* − *μ*\_*i*) \* (*x*\_*j* − *μ*\_*i*) ∧ *T*/*Σ*\_*j* ∧ *n* *γ*\_*i*(*x*[*j*])\]. (15.4)

The mixing coefficient of the i-th Gaussian is updated as:

\[*w*\_*i* = ∑\_*j* ∧ *n* *γ*\_*i*(*x*\_*j*)/*n*\]. (15.5)

### 15.4.5 Applications of GMM

#### 15.4.5.1 Clustering

Clustering is an application of GMM. Clustering is the method of separating a dataset into bunches or clusters depending on how comparative the information focuses are. By demonstrating the dataset as a blend of Gaussian conveyances and evaluating the parameters of the blend demonstrate to bunch comparable information focuses, GMM may be utilized for clustering. The number of clusters or components within the blend is frequently decided in development in GMM-based clustering. The GMM demonstrates at that point apportions each information point to one of the components depending on the probability that it has a place to that component. Each information point is allotted to the component with the most noteworthy probability to deliver the clustering result.

#### 15.4.5.2 Density Estimation

One of the most important applications of GMM is density estimation. The technique of determining the underlying probability density function of a data set is called density estimation. By modeling the data set as a mixture of Gaussian distributions, GMM can be used to estimate the density of the data set. The probability density function of a data set is represented in GMM as the weighted sum of the Gaussian distributions, with each Gaussian distribution representing a component of the mixture. Each Gaussian component of the mixture has a mean, a covariance matrix, and a weight that determines its contribution to the total density function. Based on the input data set, the GMM model calculates these parameters. By evaluating the probability density function of the mixture at this point, the GMM model can be used to estimate the density of a new data point. The GMM model provides a probability estimate of the density function, which can be used to compute statistics such as the mean, variance, and higher order moment of a data set.

#### 15.4.5.3 Data Generation

GMM may be utilized for information generation in expansion to thickness estimation. Unused information focuses can be made from a prepared GMM as shown by inspecting from the blend conveyance. The GMM demonstrated creates unused information focuses by beginning with selecting a component from the blend depending on its weight. This component is at that point utilized to build a unused information point by inspecting from the Gaussian dispersion that compares to it. The strategy is proceeded until a certain sum of information focuses are produced. The created information focuses will be factually comparable to the first dataset utilized to prepare the GMM model. However, since GMM may be a probabilistic show that makes data points based on the learned dispersion, the created information focuses are not culminate copies of the first dataset \[[11](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_11),[16](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_16),[17](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_17)\].

#### 15.4.5.4 Advantages Of GMM

##### 15.4.5.4.1 Flexibility

GMM may be a profoundly versatile demonstration able to capture a wide extent of dispersions, counting non-spherical clusters, covering clusters, and skewed conveyances. GMM's versatility makes it suitable for a wide extent of applications, including complicated information displaying in fund, science, and picture investigation.

##### 15.4.5.4.2 Probability Estimate

GMM gives a probabilistic degree of information point closeness. This metric can be valuable in a assortment of applications, counting irregularity recognizable proof and exception examination, where measuring the degree of uniqueness from anticipated behavior is crucial.

##### 15.4.5.4.3 Parameter Estimation

GMM can estimate mixed model parameters using either maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) or Bayesian methods. Even for datasets with large numbers of sizes, these methods can produce reliable parameter estimates \[[1](#ch15.xhtml#ref15_1)\].

##### 15.4.5.4.4 Data Generation

GMM can create modern information focuses with factual highlights indistinguishable from the initial dataset. This is useful in an assortment of applications, counting information increase and reenactment, where it is a basic requirement to deliver new information focuses that are better than basic dissemination.

##### 15.4.5.4.5 Interpretability

Since each component of the blend compares to a Gaussian conveyance with its claim cruel and covariance lattice, GMM produces interpretable results. This capability can be useful in a assortment of applications, such as picture division and content clustering, where the clustering comes about must be deciphered.

##### 15.4.5.4.6 Scalability

GMM is adaptable to expansive datasets and may be utilized in conjunction with other optimization procedures to extend the model's computing productivity, such as expectation-maximization (EM) and variational induction.

#### 15.4.5.5 Limitations

##### 15.4.5.5.1 Initialization

GMM can be sensitive to parameter initialization, resulting in inferior clustering results. If the initialization is insufficient, the algorithm may become trapped in local optima.

##### 15.4.5.5.2 Number Of Components

The sum of fixings within the combination must be set ahead of time. Choosing the perfect number of components may be troublesome, especially for high-dimensional datasets, and may require a huge sum of trial and error.

##### 15.4.5.5.3 Computationally Expensive

GMM may be computationally demanding, especially for huge datasets. A few parameters, such as implies, covariances, and weights, must be evaluated by this strategy, which can be time-consuming for huge datasets.

##### 15.4.5.5.4 Curse Of Dimensionality:

The reviled dimensionality, in which the number of parameters vital to legitimately depict the dispersion develops exponentially with the number of measurements, might influence GMM. This could result in overfitting or destitute execution, particularly with expansive datasets.

##### 15.4.5.5.5 Overlapping Clusters:

GMM results in the suspicion that clusters are frequently disseminated and non-overlapping, which may not be genuine for all datasets. Covering clusters can create befuddling discoveries and make assessing clustering comes about challenging.

##### 15.4.5.5.6 Sensitivity To Outliers:

Exceptions within the dataset might cause GMM to be touchy, influencing parameter estimation and clustering results. To decrease the impact of exceptions on the comes about, vigorous approaches such as trimmed probability estimation can be connected.

##### 15.4.5.5.7 Interpretability:

Whereas GMM produces interpretable discoveries, it may be troublesome to translate for datasets with a high number of components or measurements. It can be challenging to discover important clusters and decipher the information in an important way.

## 15.6 Input Dataset

The data set is a collection of unbiased related raw data used to extract information, recognize patterns, analyze, make predictions, etc.

### 15.6.1 Types of Datasets

There are different types of datasets.

*   Cross-sectional data set,
*   Longitudinal dataset,
*   Time-series dataset,
*   Panel dataset,
*   Text dataset,
*   Image dataset,
*   Audio dataset,
*   video dataset, etc.

### 15.6.2 Properties of a Dataset

The primary properties of a dataset are.

*   Data should be unbiased,
*   Quality of data should be good,
*   There should be low noise in the data,
*   Size of data should be large enough to analyze,
*   Data should represent the population.
*   The quantity of outliers should be low.

There are also more properties of a data set.

### 15.6.3 Introduction of Kaggle

Kaggle is one of the most popular and trusted websites for Data Science, Machine learning, and Artificial intelligence field. It hosts competitions for engineers and IT professionals. Kaggle is a reliable source of many kinds of data as many world-famous organizations and companies post data on it. In this project we will use “Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset” which is freely available in [Kaggle.com](http://Kaggle.com). Dataset Link: [https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/vbookshelf/rice-leaf-diseases](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/vbookshelf/rice-leaf-diseases)

### 15.6.4 Why Did We Choose “Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset”?

Rice is by far the most significant food crop for people in low- and lower-middle-income nations out of the three primary crops—wheat, maize, and rice. In low-income nations, both the affluent and the poor eat rice, but the poorest individuals consume comparatively little wheat, making them particularly sensitive to the price and availability of rice.

Rice is a fundamental and typically indispensable food staple in many Asian nations, especially for the poor. Rice makes up about half of the food costs and, on average, a fifth of household expenditures for the poor people in Asia who make less than $1.25 per day. This one group buys rice every year for the equivalent of $62 billion (in purchasing power parity). Many of the world's poor people depend on rice for their food security.

Over half of the world's population relies on rice as a staple food, making it one of the most significant crops worldwide. However, several things, including diseases, can have an impact on rice production. One of the most prevalent and harmful diseases that impact rice crops, leaf diseases result in considerable output losses globally. To discover and investigate these illnesses, researchers have produced databases, one of which is the Rice Leaf Diseases Dataset.

### 15.6.5 About the Dataset

Researchers from Gujrat, India's Dharmsinh Desai University assembled the dataset. To ensure that they were representative of the many illnesses that damage rice leaves, scientists carefully selected the photographs from rice fields in Gujrat. Using a high-resolution camera, the images were taken, and professional plant pathologists identified each disease type.

[Table 15.1](#ch15.xhtml#table15_1) shows the input dataset of spotted leaf images of rice leaves with the four prevalent leaf diseases Brown Spot, Bacterial Blight, and Leaf Smut, included in the Rice Leaf Diseases collection, a publicly accessible collection. There are 120 jpeg photos in the collection, and each one is labeled with the disease it portrays. The goal of this collection is to support the development of automated disease detection tools that can assist farmers in spotting and treating infections early on, hence reducing crop losses.

[Table 15.1 Input Dataset of Spotted Leaf](#ch15.xhtml#Ttable15_1)

![A grid shows 20 grayscale images of spotted leaves, used as input data for a model. Each image represents varying patterns and distributions of spots on leaf samples.](images/inline15_1.jpg)

Each of the 1,024 × 1,024-pixel photos in the collection were taken using a smartphone camera under natural lighting circumstances. Forty photos each of Brown Spots, Bacterial Blight, and Leaf Smut are included in the collection. The dataset's images are each labeled with the name of the disease they depict, making it simple for researchers to teach machine learning models to recognize the many leaf diseases.

## 15.8 Conclusion

In India, a tropical cultivation-focused nation, leaf diseases are a major cause for concern for vertical farming because they can significantly harm both productivity and the economy. In this study, a machine learning model based on a Gaussian mixture model was introduced to quickly and correctly diagnose rice leaf disease. The machine learning model performed effectively and efficiently after classifying a picture from the input dataset due to the sizeable dataset. This high-end strategy has demonstrated a societal impact on people's lives. Thus, it can be concluded that leaf diseases are a major issue for farmers all over the world and can have a big negative influence on yields and the economy. The accuracy, speed, and cost-effectiveness of conventional disease detection methods are constrained, but emerging technologies, such as molecular diagnostics and machine learning, hold the promise of more effective and efficient disease management methods.

# 16 Multi-Objective Optimization of Economic Development and Environmental Issues in the Yangtze River Basin, China

Minh Thu Nguyen

Vietnam Institute of Meteorology, Hydrology and Climate Change, Hanoi, Vietnam

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-16](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-16)

## 16.1 Environmental Issues and Non-Point Source Pollution

Pollutants are regarded as a harmful substance when they are discharged into the environment. Pollution in the environment include water pollution, soil pollution, and air pollution. However, NPS pollution is related to water pollution because they have non-identified sources and they cause pollution via the process of natural precipitation and snowmelt. These substances enter the aquatic environment and affect the environment's physical, chemical, and biological properties. In other words, water pollution is the release of polluting substances into surface water (lakes, streams, rivers, estuaries, and oceans) and groundwater, making water unsuitable for desired use aim or causing water quality impairment. Moreover, pollution of water resources comes normally from either point sources or non-point sources. Pollution of point source is often from power plants, sewage treatment plants, and oil wells ([Harding et al. 1998](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_5); [Wang et al. 2001](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_14)). These sources are collected, identifiable, and are easy to be controlled as well as treated. However, pollution of NPS is from many different sources, such as land use in agriculture, forestry, urban and suburban development, mining, as well as others. Agricultural runoff accounts for a large proportion of NPS pollution because agricultural activities release a big number of contaminants including manure, fertilizers, pesticides, and eroded soil particles ([David et al. 1997](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_3); [Robinson 2005](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_10); [Novotny 1982](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_9)). Their loads are affected by surface runoff, interflow, and ground water base runoff from farms and agricultural land. The most bothersome contaminants are ammonia, nutrients, sediments, bacteria, pesticides, salt, selenium, and thermal modification. They impair water quality and cause disease for organisms like waterborne diseases. If a huge number of chemical fertilizers from agriculture are discharged into a water body, the excess nitrogen factor will go into ground water and streaming branches. Moreover, using pesticide in agriculture can cause it to enter water bodies and result in NPS pollution phenomenon.

NPS pollution relates to human activities and natural factors from excess use of fertilizer as well as pesticide in agricultural areas or from toxic chemicals from urban runoffs, etc. The EPA estimated that NPS pollution from agriculture activities accounts for 60% in impairing and threatening surface water. Subsequently, it can drive property values down ([Shen et al. 2012](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_11); [EPA 2003](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_12)) because runoffs pick polluting substances up and carry them away in a natural way, and deposit them into groundwater, inland water, coastal water, and streaming systems. NPS pollution can affect community and human health, such as the scenic allure and health of surface water ([Ding et al. 2010](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_4)), or kill aquatic organisms due to impairment of oxygen concentration in the water. Thus, affective level of non-point source pollution can cause more serious harm to human health and the environment from water quality impairment due to eutrophication and it is more difficult to control/treat because of its non-identified release sources.

## 16.2 Economic Development and Non-Point Source Pollution in China

Around 1978, China began to open up to the world. In the past four decades, China has been successful in its reform process via eliminating extreme poverty and transforming its economy. The country's sustained growth has created more opportunities to integrate with the world's economy and presently more than 800 million of its people have lifted themselves out of poverty. This economic growth has created a historic transition for China's people from an agricultural economy to an urban economy. This transition has allowed 260 million people to move from working in agriculture to productive economic activities in big cities \[WB 2021\]. Today, China's people are at high middle-income level and China will become a high-income developed country in the future. This is a key goal in China's national development policy.

The real issue of environmental pollution in China is demonstrated by its population growth and economic development. In the past few decades, China's economy has developed quickly and it has come to be regarded as the world's factory. Many countries have since invested in China. China's industrialization and developed economy has helped its people to improve their lives as well as raise GDP growth. According to World Bank data, China's average GDP growth has been over 9% per year \[WB 2019\]. However, a rapidly developing economy combined with population growth has resulted in extreme pressure on its water resources and caused environmental pollution in China. Water shortages and pollution occur in many localities. Although China is not the main source of historical cumulative emissions, today the country accounts for 27% of annual global carbon dioxide release and a third of the world's greenhouse gas emissions \[WB 2014\] and its air as well as water pollution affects China's people and other countries. Thus, China's rapid growth is alongside environmental problems and leading to an imbalance between economic development and environmental protection. This has caused a strain on sustainable growth.

Non-point source pollution also is being paid attention in China because it is really a difficult issue to control and manage well. NPS pollution normally comes from various sources with complex spatial distribution. In 1990 about 30%–50% waterbodies were polluted in China by NPS pollution. Many NPS pollution sources are from agricultural activities such as use of pesticides and fertilizers, which are not controlled leading to excessive concentrations of residues in soil. Additionally, erosion from land leads to increased sedimentation in river systems. Sediment is a suspended component, which can be transferred a far distance in a short time and suspended solids in water bodies make it difficult for aquatic organisms to absorb sunlight. Accumulation of nutrient substances with high concentration in water bodies leads to reduced dissolved oxygen, loss of biodiversity, algal bloom, and eutrophication. These affect aquatic organisms and their living environment, upset the balance of aquatic ecosystems, and cause danger to human health.

Over the past 30 years, China's economy has seen strong growth. Industrialization and modernization have become the hallmarks of the country. However, non-point source pollution issues have become longstanding problems that cannot be solved quickly because water bodies need a long time to recover. Therefore, pollutants accumulate slowly in water resources, the environment and affect human health. The Yangtze River basin is a key economic area that serves 185 towns in China and its population accounts for one-third of the country's population. The economy of the Yangtze River basin is focused on agricultural production. In March 2023, this area was a beneficiary of a program from the World Bank called Yangtze River Protection and Ecological Restoration that aimed to enhance ecological protection and reduce water pollution along the river basin. However, the results are not yet visible; pollution still exists in the Yangtze River basin. Whether there exists an optimal solution between economic development and environmental pollution for Yangtze River basin, is something that we will consider in the next section.

## 16.3 Urban Growth and Multiple Objective Optimizations

Urban growth is accompanied by industrialization and economic development ([Walker 2001](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_13)). Urbanization has allowed China's people more opportunities for working and moving from rural to urban area. Efficient urbanization can help China to best use its productive resources of people, land, and capital. It increases the welfare of people and results in more growth. Rapid urbanization has taken place in China during the past 30 years and it can be called a perfect growth model. With GDP increases, China today is among the largest economy of the world and its economy is second only to the United States. It is also the world's largest manufacturer. However, in parallel with these achievements, urban growth has resulted in many negative effects such as impairment of water quality and water pollution. Moreover, deterioration of water quality has been associated with urban growth because emission and pollution are not controlled. Nowadays, green urban growth is becoming a priority worldwide and in China too. Green economic growth is when a national economy develops/grows but it still ensures environmental issues are taken care of. At the core of green growth is GDP growth but minimum emission intensity. Thus, the question is how can positive growth be maintained while containing its negative impacts on the environment. An optimal model can solve this problem with a balance between the different objectives.

Factually, optimization theory has been applied in urban growth since the 1960s and is applied in different fields of science such as engineering, economics and logistics. Optimization can occur with two objectives or multi-objectives. For instance, if urban growth sees conflicts between different land-use groups, the optimization will become a multi-objective problem that needs optimal decisions to achieve a tradeoff between two or more conflicting objectives. The tradeoff exists between the objectives via the Pareto optimal solution. The Pareto front contains a set of non-dominated solutions and are feasible solutions. It moves from one feasible solution to another, which moves at least one objective function to a better value. Moreover, a combination between genetic algorithms in the optimal process allows for exact solutions in optimization. Genetic Algorithm (GA) is based on selection of natural genetics and survival of the fittest. It is found in different sciences, such as computational science, mathematics, economics and many other fields. Initially, a lot of individual solutions is made randomly in order to create a population. The number and size of population depend on each different issue. However, the number of a population usually contains several hundreds or thousands of feasible solutions. A next generation concludes a new population via a selection process. Certain selection results in more best solutions and a new generation is formed. From here, a population of solutions can be offered via Pareto optimization.

Urban growth in China has improved people's lives and also makes it difficult to control pollution because of rapid development as well as defects in treatment technology. In the Yangtze River Basin the non-point source pollution phenomenon is from an excess of nutrient substances from different sources, particularly from agricultural activities. This pollution not only affects the environmental landscape, but also influences living organisms. In order to control and manage the flow of these nutrients into the Yangtze River basin as well as protect the basin's water quality, a multi-objective optimization of genetic algorithm is researched based on GDP objective, changing cost objective, compatibility objective between land use, and NPS pollution load objective to find the optimization between urban growth and environmental pollution. In particular, optimal solutions of two-dimensional genetic algorithm are combined with multiple objectives:

1.  Maximization of GDP value
2.  Minimization of NPS pollutant load
3.  Minimization of the cost of changing from the status quo
4.  Minimization of incompatibility between the land uses

And constraints:

1.  Urban construction land > a certain level
2.  Agricultural land > a certain level
3.  Water bodies unchanged

## 16.4 Objective Evaluation

### 16.4.1 Maximization of GDP Value

Maximization of economic benefit is calculated as:

$MAX\\left( Z\_{1} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{Unit\\\_ GDP\_{InbasinAd\\\_ i} \\ast A\_{i}}$ (16.1)

Where:

1.  Z[1]: Total GDP value in the basin;
2.  *Unit\_GDP*[*InbasinAd\_i*:] GDP unit for the *i*\-n land-use (RMB/m²);
3.  A[i]: Total area on the *i*\-th land-use (m²);

In order to calculate GDP value for each land-use type (agricultural land, wetland, desert land, urban construction land, grassland, water area, forest land, and barren land), the research used weighted method. Firstly, the research used the GDP value of urban fields from the statistical yearbook for the Yangtze River basin. Then, rate of area as administrative scope which belongs to the basin via equation:

$GDP\_{InbasinAd\\\_ j} = GDP\_{Ad\_{j}} \\ast \\frac{AreaInbasinAd\_{j}}{AreaAd\_{j}}$ (16.2)

In which:

1.  *AreaInbasinAd*[*j*] is administrative scope;
2.  *AreaAd*[*j*]: GDP value is extracted for the basin,

Next, *GDP*[*InbasinAd\_i*] and land-use area are extracted from raster data; GDP value is made on each i-th land-use kind in the j-th administrative scope. From that, *Unit\_GDP*[*InbasinAd\_i,j*], can be calculated by the following equation as:

*U**n**i**t*\_*G**D**P*[*I**n**b**a**s**i**n**A**d*\_*i*, *j*] = *G**D**P*[*I**n**b**a**s**i**n**A**d*\_*i*, *j*]/*A**r**e**a**I**n**b**a**s**i**n**A**d*[*i*, *j*] (16.3)

Weighting method is used and *Unit\_GDP*[*InbasinAd\_i*] is calculated as:

$Unit\\\_ GDP\_{InBasinAd\\\_ i} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{19}{Unit\\\_ GDP\_{InBasinAd\\\_ i,j} \\ast \\frac{AreaInBasinAd\_{j}}{AreaBasin}}$ (16.4)

Where: *Unit\_GDP*[*InbasinAd\_i*] is the GDP value for each i-th land-use kind and 19 is the number of provinces on the Yangtze River Bain.

### 16.4.2 Minimization of NPS Pollutant Load

To estimate NPS pollutant load, a relationship between land-use types and NPS pollutant load is established, the export coefficient model (ECM) is used again in the optimization of urban growth. Therefore, the diminishing to minimum level of NPS pollution is the desired natural environment protection:

$MIN\\left( Z\_{2} \\right) = \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}{\\alpha\\beta E\_{i}A\_{i}}$ (16.5)

Where:

1.  *Z*[*2*:] is pollutant load in the basin;
2.  *E*[*i*] (*ton/km*^(*2*)) is the export coefficient for *i*\-th land use;
3.  A[i]: Area of each land-use type (km²);
4.  α: precipitation impact factor
5.  β: terrain impact factor

With export coefficient E[i] for *i*\-th land-use type extracted in different patterns (8 types of land-use) for the Yangtze River Basin from many studies on NPS pollution by ECM ([Ding et al. 2010](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_4); [Liu et al. 2009](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_8)); Precipitation impact factor and the terrain impact factor are identified via rate of precipitation and slope at location (*i,j*). They are calculated as:

$\\alpha\_{x,y} = \\frac{{Precipation}\_{x,y}}{averagePre}$ (16.6)

$\\beta\_{x,y} = \\frac{{Slope}\_{x,y}}{averageSlo}$ (16.7)

Where: (*x,y*) is the location of the cell; Precipation[*x,y*] and Slope[*x,y*] are the precipitation and slope at location (*x,y*), respectively; Average Pre and average Slo are average precipitation and average slope.

### 16.4.3 Minimization of Changing Cost Index

Changing cost index is calculated via dimensionless value from *i*\-th land use to j-th land-use because dimensionless cost can present the relationship of different land-use changing and approaching nearly its real cost ([Zhang et al. 2010](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_18)). It is presented as:

$MIN(Z\_{3}) = \\sum\\limits\_{(x,y) \\in \\cup}{{change}\\\_{index}\_{(x,y)}}$ (16.8)

Where:

1.  Z[3:] Cost value in land-use changing;
2.  *U*: Basin area;
3.  (*x,y*): Land-use cell in the basin;

In which, change\_index[(*x,y*)] is the changing cost index for land-use cell (*x,y*), and change\_index[(*x,y*)] is identified via current land-use situation and the optimized land-use for land use cell (*x,y*). It ranges from 0–1, and with higher value will get higher cost in land-use changing. This changing cost index is referenced from existing research ([Balling et al. 2004](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_1)).

### 16.4.4 Minimization of Incompatibility Index

Incompatibility is presented via total incompatibility index of center land-use cell and neighboring cells including 9 cells and is identified by review materials ([Ligmann et al. 2008](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_7); [Cao et al. 2012](#ch16.xhtml#ref16_2)). This index ranges from 0–1. A higher value reveals more big conflict happening between land-use types. Calculation is showed as:

$MIN(Z\_{4}) = \\sum\\limits\_{(x,y) \\in U}{\\sum\\limits\_{({ii},{jj}) \\in N\_{x,y}}{{IncompleteIndex}({Landuse}(x,y),{Landuse}(ii,jj))}}$ (16.9)

Where:

1.  Z[4]: incompatibility value;
2.  *U*: Basin area;
3.  N[x,y]: 8 neighboring cells of center cell (*x,y*);
4.  Landuse(*x,y*): Land-use type for cell (*x,y*), and Land-use (*ii,jj*) is represented for land-use type of cell (*ii,jj*);
5.  Incomplete Index (Land-use (*x,y*) and Land-use (*ii,jj*)): incompatibility index between Landuse (*x,y*) and Landuse (*ii,jj*).

### 16.4.5 Constraint 1: Urban Construction Land

Based on the population in 2010 and area value of per capita urban construction land to make a statistical population data in the basin. From that finding of annual growth rate and using this statistical population data to prove urban construction land in the basin, which is no less than a certain value.

### 16.4.6 Constraint 2: Agricultural Land

According to Regulations on Basic farmland Protection, of total agricultural land area in 2010, agricultural land in the basin was more than 80% of total land area. Therefore, total agricultural land is not less than a certain value.

### 16.4.7 Constraint 3: Water Bodies

In the basin, except for the main river flow, many branches and lakes supply/enter the Yangtze River basin. It is very important to the water ecological system and in order to conduct this research, the water space should not be occupied by urban development process.

### 16.4.8 Genetic Algorithm Process

Multiple objectives can be combined with one-dimensional genetic algorithm (GA) or two-dimensional GA. Normally, GA for one-dimensional is applied in land-use when it is divided similar to traffic lines. This significant trait makes it easy to identify and assess non-spatial objectives. However, with spatial objectives, it is difficult to apply one-dimensional GA because there is a gap between two land-use type and neighbor cells. Therefore, applying two-dimensional GA will resolve this problem and it becomes easier to optimize urban growth, in which land-use grid in a raster map is denoted by genes. Each gene is set up as an integer and is ranged from 1 to th-land-use number. A genetic algorithm is started by 100 random land-use patterns and is presented as a generation, in which constraints and satisfying are ranked by fitness. The next generation is the second 100 land-use patterns and it is gained via selection; crossover; mutation; and elitism process. Such a process takes place until the last generation. The processes of two-dimensional genetic algorithm are revealed as:

![Figure 16.1 Two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization process.](images/fig16_1.jpg)

Figure 16.1 Two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization process.

1.  **Selection:** from the set of solutions, a number of fitter individuals are selected and their name is called as mother and father.
2.  **Crossover:** offspring from more than one parent is selected, then one or more offsprings are produced via crossover and they use the genetic material of the parents.
3.  **Mutation:** the mutation happens in natural genetics when the children have features that are not the same as their parents and this process is conducted to avoid a local optimum.
4.  **Elitism:** to gain the best solution in each generation, the first solutions that have the highest fitness value will be transferred to next generation without any operation.

## 16.5 Optimization of the Objectives

### 16.5.1 Optimization of Non-Point Source Load and Changing Cost Index

From two-dimensional GA for optimizing, the research proposes optimal solutions of non-point source load and other objectives, in which these optimal solutions satisfy the objective functions although conflicts occur between objectives.

[Figure 16.2](#ch16.xhtml#fig16_2) presents optimal solutions between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives with condition of objectives being minimization of NPS pollution load and minimization of changing cost index objectives. In fact, it is very difficult for a relationship to exist between two these objectives because to keep NPS pollution to a minimum value, a high cost must be paid for treatment and controlling it. Nevertheless, via the optimal process of two-dimensional genetic algorithm, the research indicates the existence of two feasible solution sets for the first generation and the last generation between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives.

![Figure 16.2 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and changing cost index.](images/fig16_2.jpg)

[Figure 16.2 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and changing cost index.](#ch16.xhtml#Ffig16_2)

In the first generation, the research finds four optimal solutions and two weakly feasible points belonging to criterion space. They are laid on the Pareto frontier. However, they only have four optimal solutions and are regarded as a set of optimal solutions with the best value as red Pareto front line, which satisfy conditions on minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index objectives. Thus, this optimal solution set gains the tradeoff of objectives by minimum changing cost index and it still ensures no pollution from non-point source. Optimization of this first generation also indicates two weak optimal solutions. They are not regarded as the best solutions belonging to the set of optimal solutions because they only satisfy minimization of non-point source load when changing cost index is high. The set only satisfies one objective while optimal condition is satisfied on two objectives. Moreover, the research shows also Pareto frontier in the second rank including 12 optimal solutions, which are non-dominated individuals in the population than the first rank. However, the second rank has more optimal points than the first rank and the individuals that lie below space are more optimal than above space of the second rank frontier. The individuals are located further on the Pareto frontier, which has diminishing optimization between non-point source load and changing cost index objectives in the criterion space.

[Figure 16.3](#ch16.xhtml#fig16_3) is a population including individuals and is achieved after two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimizing process. The last generation of optimal process for non-point source load and changing cost index objectives indicates that the best solutions in this generation is more than the first generation as 2 optimal solutions. These six optimal solutions make a set of optimal points with the best value and they satisfy objective conditions of minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index. The last generation especially does not appear to show a weakly optimal solution. Thus, in the last generation exists a perfect Pareto set with six optimal points that lie on the red Pareto frontier, and gains the tradeoff between objectives. Furthermore, the research finds also six optimal points in the second rank with optimal value less than the first rank Pareto frontier. Therefore, in multi-objective optimization of non-point source load and changing cost index we present four optimal solutions in the first generation and six optimal solutions in the last generation as Pareto frontiers that satisfy the tradeoff between objectives.

![Figure 16.3 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and changing cost index.](images/fig16_3.jpg)

[Figure 16.3 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and changing cost index.](#ch16.xhtml#Ffig16_3)

### 16.5.2 Optimization of Non-Point Source Load and GDP

When considering objectives in maximization of GDP and minimization of non-point source load, conflicts are revealed between the objectives. These objectives have high conflicts because a country with higher GDP and development has people with high living standards; high consumption; increased pollution emission; as well needing more urban construction areas to develop and produce. This development leads to increased pollution in the environment, especially in emission of non-point sources. However, people are now directing their energy in national development to mitigate environment pollution while still ensuring economic development. Thus, there is conflict between these objectives but optimal solutions can be be found. They are demonstrated by two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization process in this research via minimization of NPS pollution load and maximization of GDP objectives. [Figure 16.4](#ch16.xhtml#fig16_4) is the optimal map of two these objectives in the first generation and the last generation, where individuals make up a population and are located in the criterion space. The map indicates that in the first generation, a set of optimal points includes five of the best solutions that lie on red Pareto frontier and located on the left of the criterion space. These optimal solutions have the best value and satisfy both maximization of GDP and minimization of non-point source load as well as achieving the tradeoff between objectives. This is a perfect Pareto set. Furthermore, the research proposes eight optimal points in the second rank that are non-dominated than optimal solutions in the first rank. However, optimal solutions in the first rank are less than the second rank in the criterion space.

![Figure 16.4 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and GDP objectives.](images/fig16_4.jpg)

[Figure 16.4 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and GDP objectives.](#ch16.xhtml#Ffig16_4)

After the selection process, crossover, mutation, and elitism of individuals in two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimization, the last generation of two these objectives reveal a set of feasible decision space, which includes 12 optimal solutions located on the left of the criterion space as red Pareto frontier. [Figure 16.5](#ch16.xhtml#fig16_5) shows that the number of Pareto optimal solutions in the last generation is more than the first generation as seven best solutions and of course they satisfy conditions on maximization of GDP with minimization of non-point source load, while still achieving the tradeoff of objectives. Moreover, in the second rank of optimal solutions, the research indicates there are 15 optimal points, which are non-dominated individuals than the first rank. Thus, 12 optimal points is the best solution of Pareto optimization set in the last generation for GDP and non-point source load objectives. Therefore, multi-objective optimization of non-point source load minimization and maximization of GDP objectives indicates 5 the best solutions in the first generation and 12 optimal solutions in the last generation. They lie on the red Pareto frontier and gain the tradeoff between objectives.

![Figure 16.5 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and GDP objectives.](images/fig16_5.jpg)

[Figure 16.5 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and GDP objectives.](#ch16.xhtml#Ffig16_5)

### 16.5.3 Optimization of NPS Load and Land Use Incompatibility Index

Land use compatibility is understood as attaining the highest and best in land-use. Land-use compatibility can reduce the conflicts between neighboring land-uses and result in less change around land use areas. Moreover, land use compatibility is also regarded as an index to assess conflicting land uses about economy and society in community. In particular, it has become more important in urban development, and minimization of land use incompatibility means maximum diminishing of conflicts in land use. If compared to non-point source load, this means something is not factual because urban development results in increasing and expanding land uses leading to a rise in conflicting land uses and also emits more pollution. Thus, minimization of land use incompatibility and minimization of non-point source load are difficult to find in reality because they are highly conflicted with each other. Nevertheless, selecting individuals and genetic algorithm optimal process propose two sets of optimal solutions for the first generation and the last generation for non-point source load and land use incompatibility index objectives. [Figure 16.5](#ch16.xhtml#fig16_5) indicates those optimal solution sets.

In the first generation, a set of Pareto optimal points is located on the left of the criterion space including four optimal points with the best solutions. They are laid on the red Pareto frontier and satisfy optimal conditions of minimization of land use incompatibility and minimization of non-point source load objectives.

![Figure 16.6 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and incompatibility index.](images/fig16_6.jpg)

Figure 16.6 Pareto fronts of the first generation for NPS pollution load and incompatibility index.

Moreover, via optimal process, two weak optimal points are found lying on the red Pareto front line which only satisfy minimization of non-point source load but do not satisfy minimization of land use incompatibility index. Except for the best optimal solutions, the research also indicates the existence of non-dominated individuals in the second rank including 11 optimal points whose values are less than optimal than the first rank, which is black Pareto frontier. Thus, the first generation of above objectives proposes four optimal solutions as a set of Pareto optimization.

In the last generation of multiple objectives of optimization with minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives, the research indicates a set of feasible solutions concluding six optimal points that satisfy the conditions of objectives of minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load and form a red Pareto front line. Moreover, the figure also shows there are three weak optimal solutions lying on the blue Pareto frontier too of the first rank. They are not regarded as the best solutions because they only satisfy the condition of one objective of minimization of non-point source load but they have high land use incompatibility index, as well as gaining the tradeoff of objectives. Furthermore, the research presents also optimal points in the second rank with ten feasible solutions, which include more non-dominated individuals than ones in the first rank. From ten of these individuals, there are five individuals that satisfy secondary optimization solution. The remaining individuals can be regarded as very weak optimal points because they only satisfy minimization of non-point source load objective (five optimal points) or minimization of land use incompatibility index objective. Thus, in the last generation of these objectives there exists six optimal solutions with the best value which are shown on the black Pareto frontier. The optimization process on minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives denotes four optimal solutions in the first generation and six best solutions in the last generation. They are presented by the Pareto frontier and reveals as a perfect Pareto set.

## 16.6 Summary

Generally, multi-objective optimization is the tradeoff between objectives. Via two-dimensional genetic algorithm optimal process for minimization of NPS pollution load, minimization of changing cost index, maximization of GDP value, and minimization of incompatibility index objectives, this research indicates that although these objectives are conflicting with each other, optimal solutions still exist between them, which create the tradeoff of objectives. In particular, there are four optimal solutions in the first generation and six optimal solutions in the last generation in minimization of non-point source load and minimization of changing cost index objectives; five best solutions in the first generation and 12 optimal solutions in the last generation in minimization of non-point source load and maximization of GDP value objectives; four optimal solutions in the first generation and six best solutions in the last generation in minimization of land use incompatibility index and minimization of non-point source load objectives. They are laid on the red Pareto frontier and revealed as perfect Pareto sets.

![Figure 16.7 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and incompatibility index.](images/fig16_7.jpg)

Figure 16.7 Pareto fronts of the last generation for NPS pollution load and incompatibility index.

# 17 Qualitative Study on E-Commerce and Brick-and-Mortar Stores A Machine Learning Approach

Sanjay Das

Research Scholar, Department of Management and Marketing, West Bengal State University, Barasat, North 24 Parganas, West Bengal, India

Soumen Santra

Assistant Professor, Department of MCA, Techno International New Town, Chakpachuria, West Bengal, India

Anirban Sarkar

Professor and Head, Department of Management and Marketing, West Bengal State University, Barasat, North 24 Parganas, West Bengal, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-17](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-17)

## 17.1 Introduction

Electronic commerce (e-commerce) refers to digitally enabled commercial transactions between and among organizations and individuals ([Laudon and Traver 2018](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_12)). Brick and Mortar stores have been used by all customers before e-commerce platforms became available.

With the spread and popularity of the internet, together with the availability of smartphones at an affordable price, a larger proportion of the Indian population got the opportunity to use e-commerce. With the benefits offered by the combination of the internet and smartphone, e-commerce has been adopted by a growing number of customers and marketers. In 2022, the Indian e-commerce market was predicted to increase by 21.5%, reaching US$ 74.8 billion i.e., Rs.5,98,400 crores. The Indian e-commerce market is expected to grow to Rs 16, 20,000 crores (US$ 200 billion) by 2026 ([IBEF 2022](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_10)). For the 2021 festive season, Indian e-commerce platforms generated sales with a Gross Merchandise Value (GMV) of US$ 9.2 billion i.e., Rs.73,600 crore, a 23% increase from the previous year's US$ 7.4 billion. This trend indicates that more and more customers are adopting e-commerce to fulfill their purchase requirements.

With the adoption of e-commerce, its comparison with B&M stores has become a subject of discussion among customers as they begin to notice the pros and cons of both. Due to this increasing popularity of online sales, the revenue of pure B&M retailers has been reducing gradually which is a warning for them ([Dahiya 2017](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_4)). Operators and stakeholders of B&M stores are becoming apprehensive about the future of retail business in B&M mode. Whether they would be able to survive at all with their pure B&M stores and if they survive, how long would they be able to continue become a pertinent question to many of them? To understand the factors behind the concern of the B&M retailers, a Direct Interview (DI) of B&M retailers was conducted followed by an analysis of the responses.

In this study, reasons behind customers’ preference for e-commerce in comparison with the reasons behind visiting B&M retailers have been captured through focus group discussion (FGD). FGD is used as a qualitative approach to gain an in-depth understanding of social issues when the aim is to obtain data from a purposely selected group of individuals rather than from a statistically representative sample of a broader population ([O.Nyumba et al. 2018](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_16)). Key reasons were identified as “Themes” using “Thematic Analysis” (TA) which is a method for identifying, analyzing, and interpreting patterns of meaning (“themes”) within qualitative data ([Clarke and Braun 2016](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_3)). NLP i.e., natural language processing ([IBM Cloud Education 2020](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_11)) has been used on the extract of FGD and DI to identify the features thereby validating the themes.

Selection between B&M and e-commerce depends on various factors (Themes). Based on the analysis done in this study recommendations for B&M retailers are mentioned in the conclusions.

## 17.2 Review of Literature

[Clarke and Braun (2016](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_3)) contended that thematic analysis (TA) is a method for identifying, analyzing, and interpreting patterns of meaning (“themes”) from qualitative data. They also mentioned that TA can be used to identify patterns within and across data obtained from the experience, views, and perspectives of the participants. According to the authors, TA can be used to analyze both large and small datasets (from 1 to 2 participants to large interview studies with 60 or more participants) of both homogenous and heterogeneous samples.

Customers’ attitudes toward the internet have changed significantly during the last two decades ([Chaffey and Chadwick 2016](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_2)). As a result, in consumer markets, customers are becoming more used to buying online and digital technologies enable them to be well-informed while deciding on purchases. As consumers are becoming more informed, the struggle for power between consumers and retailers will continue. Moreover, an increasingly important role is being played by social networks in linking consumers and potential business partners. This clearly states that the business environment is in favor of the e-commerce players and therefore, the B&M retailers should be aware and alert to initiate suitable actions to remain relevant.

[Singla, Randhawa and Jain (2018](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_20)) described customers' reliance on online reviews as positive or negative which has significantly expanded because of an evolutionary shift from offline to online markets. Online reviews have evolved into a tool for fostering confidence and influencing consumer purchasing behavior. With such dependence, it becomes necessary to manage a vast volume of reviews and provide the buyer with reliable reviews. Following data balancing with a nearly equal number of favorable and unfavorable ratings, three different classification models have been employed. The authors showed how machine learning models such as Bayes theorem, decision tree, support vector machine, etc. are getting high accuracy.

With technological advancements and ease of internet accessibility, it has become possible to conduct “Focus Group Discussion” using internet-based ([Daniels et al. 2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_5)) audio-visual software. Online platforms overcome any geographical limitations and enhance opportunities for real-time discussions in groups that otherwise might not be feasible. During this research “Focus Group Discussion” i.e., FGDs were conducted online with consumers used to buy online as well as from B&M retailers.

As the data emerging out of customers’ statements during the FGD is a pertinent source of information, data intensive approach deploying machine learning as mentioned by [Peters et al. (2014](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_17)) was considered during this research for information retrieval to support business decision-making. The author emphasized the approach to identify and filter data of mixed quality from disparate sources. Using machine learning algorithms, it is possible to fetch the intensive nature of data which is known as intensive data learning approach from the perspective of big data concepts.

[Leelawat et al. (2022](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_13)) showed that machine learning algorithms are capable of accurately predicting the goals and feelings of the source material. After categorizing the text into sentiment and intention classes, the word within each category can be used to determine the sentiments and travel-related intentions of readers. The authors have shown the process of word pulling and the method to find out the sentiment polarity from the word cloud. Based on the customer's status and the data set received from tweets, their sentiment about the journey specifications was found.

[Vojvodić (2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_22)) mentioned that advanced in-store technologies are available for B&M retailers which can be deployed to interact with customers. These interactive technologies can also provide insight into the information on trends in consumer behavior bringing out the details about their expectations, needs, and preferences. The author categorically mentioned that, despite the perceived diminishing role, B&M retailers have the potential to be powerful players in the future of shopping.

Industries have been highly affected by digitalization and have had to transform their business models because technology has been transforming consumer behavior and the relationship between consumers with businesses is getting reshaped ([Gerea, Gonzalez-Lopez and Herskovic 2021](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_7)). Therefore, it is expected that customers will also behave accordingly in this environment. To meet the requirement of the customers, many traditional retailers and service-based companies transformed themselves to omnichannel mode to respond to serve the customers to their satisfaction in the competitive market.

Omnichannel businesses have emerged due to extensive dissemination of digital technologies along with changing consumer behavior ([Lehrer and Trenz 2022](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_14)). The marketer can offer continuous and consistent customer experience over multiple digital and physical channels. Omnichannel businesses operate channels in an integrated fashion to get synergistic advantages and elevate the level of customer experience by offering the customers an opportunity to seamlessly move across digital and physical environments.

Omnichannel retailing aims to deliver a seamless shopping experience to customers by providing product information display, information search including comparison, and an online payment system ([Thaichon et al. 2022](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_21)). Retailers can thus redefine both in-store and online experiences of the customers in choosing a product by using the technology thereby improving the relationship quality.

## 17.3 Objectives of the Study

The objectives that emanate from the review of the literature are listed below:

1.  To have a theme-based comparison between e-commerce and B&M retailers from the perspective of customers who have exposure and experience with both systems.
2.  To understand from the B&M retailers the weakness and the threat as perceived by them with increasing acceptance of e-commerce along with their strength areas based on which they have served the customers.
3.  To identify a path for sustenance for the B&M stakeholders to remain relevant to prospects and customers based on their strengths discovered through “Thematic Analysis.”

## 17.4 Methods

The study was conducted in Kolkata deploying qualitative methods in three parts.

Focus Group Discussions (FGD) with customers: As an FGD must be conducted involving multiple participants who share common characteristics that are relevant from the research perspective, participants (Van [Eeuwijk and Angehrn 2017](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_6)) were carefully chosen to ensure that they are conversant with both B&M as well as an online purchase. To obtain a wider range of information two separate FGDs were conducted taking a different set of participants. Considering the difficulties of having face-to-face group discussions during the COVID period, online FGDs ([Daniels et al. 2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_5)) were conducted. From the proceedings of the discussion held in the group, the reasons that came out for choosing e-commerce, as well as brick-and-mortar, were identified as “Themes.” These themes were found to be further constituted of “Subthemes” which were the detailed supporting reasons under the main reason. Details of Themes and Subthemes as came out in FGDs are shown in [Table 17.1](#ch17.xhtml#table17_1).
