Direct interviews (DI) of 14 B&M retailers of consumer electronic goods were conducted using a semi-structured questionnaire to understand their concerns about the prevailing market scenario. A purposive sampling technique was used for the selection of retailers. As many B&M retailers have developed their e-commerce channel also to become multi-channel ([Savila, Wathoni and Santoso 2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_19)), both types of retailers i.e., pure B&M and multichannel retailers have been covered while interviewing retailers. Key factors of the concern voiced out by the retailers were flagged as “Themes” which were supported by “Subthemes.” Details of Themes and Sub-themes as indicated by retailers have been listed in [Table 17.2](#ch17.xhtml#table17_2).

 

[Table 17.2 Themes Identified Through Direct Interviews of Retailers](#ch17.xhtml#Ttable17_2)

 
| Theme (No.) || Subtheme || Applicable to || Quotation || Sub Theme No. |

| Price (R1) || Aggressive online offers || B&M || “… Online traders break MOP-we have to counter it by other factors– Challenge to be handled.” || R11 |

| “We have people in stores to meet, greet & find the exact requirement of the customer.” |

Natural Language Processing known as NLP ([IBM Cloud Education 2020](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_11)), a versatile wing of Artificial Intelligence (AI), has been used on the qualitatively processed text captured during the FGD of customers and interviews of retailers. Our study proposed a model (as shown in [Figure 17.1](#ch17.xhtml#fig17_1)) wherein:

![Figure 17.1 Process for identification of “Themes” with natural language processing (NLP).](images/fig17_1.jpg)

[Figure 17.1 Process for identification of “Themes” with natural language processing (NLP).](#ch17.xhtml#Ffig17_1)

1.  The corpuses from each sentence captured from the customers and retailers were determined using Natural Language Tool Kit (NLTK) ([Mukherjee et al. 2018](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_15))
2.  The model vectorized each word from the statements of customers and retailers and evaluated the feature of each corpus ([Wang and Hu 2021](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_23)) using TensorFlow.
3.  Using the sentiment analysis ([Yao 2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_25)) approach in TensorFlow the hidden features were found from the extracted features of the corpuses.
4.  Evaluating the feature factors, our model generated a list of features to work on.
5.  These features have been matched and compared with the themes identified manually to arrive at the final list of themes that needs to be addressed by the stakeholders of B&M retailers.

After getting the features the model used Google Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) and data analytics tool panda (python data analysis library) to get the tensor value of each corpus i.e., the tensor value of each word selected as a feature. The whole data set was converted into a dataframe. Based on this dataframe the TPU applied vectorization method and evaluated the tensor value of each corpus of which top 14 values have been considered for matching with the “Themes” and “Sub-themes” found during FGD (of customers) and DI (of retailers).

## 17.5 Results and Discussion Outcomes

Focus Group Discussion (FGD) sessions indicated four “Themes” i.e., Price, Information, Convenience and Confidence as shown in [Table 17.1](#ch17.xhtml#table17_1). Emphasis was made by the speakers on two aspects of price i.e., transparency and comparability which have been shown as “Subtheme.” The third subtheme of price was found to be the “Offer.” Offers are placed before the customers in the form of discounts or special prices with a closely approaching timeline during different occasions. The release of such offers on regular basis creates an expectation in the minds of prospects who plans to purchase the desired products. Thus, the e-commerce retailer creates an invisible engagement with the customers even before they order and attempt to prevent them from going to the B&M retailers.

“Information,” the next “Theme” identified during FGD, is about useful details that are made available to customers before, during, and after the purchase. This encompasses the “Subthemes,” “variants and specifications,” “delivery status” and “reviews.” The details regarding product variants, specifications, and prices that are available from e-commerce sites are not available to the prospects from B&M stores. This came out clearly during FGD as a reason to visit e-commerce websites. Real-time information on delivery status available to the customers is also exclusive for e-commerce and pointed out to be useful by FGD participants. “Reviews” are shown as a “Subtheme” within the group of the theme “Information.” Prospects check reviews to get the perception and feedback of customers (who purchased the product earlier) before buying. As the information is available 24×7 without any human intervention it attracts prospects from all segments creating aspiring customers. Not only the online customers, but the prospects who are used to buying from B&M also visit the e-commerce sites for product information, prices, and reviews.

Convenience, the third “Theme” identified in FGD, is because of the wide range of payment methods from payment by credit/debit card, app-based payment, and cash on delivery. The flexible and customer-oriented delivery system of e-commerce is the reason for having delivery as a “Subtheme.”

It was mentioned during FGD that, the “Touch & Feel” of a product that is only available at the B&M stores instils confidence in the mind of the buyer and motivates them to visit the store even if the purchase is finally made online. It was also mentioned that customers not only value the personal touch that is there in a brick-and-mortar store, but this is also something the customers miss while using e-commerce. Guidance of the sales personnel in the stores towards the selection of the right product is also found to be playing a role in attracting even those customers to B&M who are familiar with online purchases. For some specific products e.g., medicine where the expiry date is critical some customers prefer B&M only. Thus “Confidence” evolved as the fourth “Theme” of FGD comprising “Touch & Feel,” “Personal touch & advice” and “Product type specific need” as “Subthemes.”

Based on the business approach of the retailers as perceived from the responses received in Direct interviews (DI), B&M retailers have been categorized into three types as below-

**Aggressive**: These are Multichannel / Omnichannel retailers operating in multiple locations spread across Kolkata and its suburb, they have created a culture of challenging e-commerce players in their organization which inspires the customer-facing employees to maximize conversion by offering anything the customer may need. out of the walk-in. These retailers believe in the strength of face-to-face human interface which is not there in the case of e-commerce.

**Competitive**: These are pure B&M retailers located as neighborhood stores or at the market where customers visit regularly. These stores have been regularly reviewing the products and services that they offer in comparison with the trends and needs of the market. They update the product and service categories to continue to offer something which keeps them relevant to their target segment. They are aware of the competition of e-commerce not to surrender but to remain competitive.

**Worried**: These are pure B&M retailers who are worried about their future. They are not planning to adopt technology-based tools to start any internet-based activity. They consider it an expensive proposition and they feel that they are not familiar with the web-based activity. They believe that such B&M stores of consumer electronic goods will have to close within the next few years.

Though the retailers belonged to different categories, their areas of concern with respect to competition from e-commerce could be grouped into three factors designated as “Theme.” They are Price, Convenience and Situation. Price has been pointed out by many brick-and-mortar retailers as the major concern area. According to “Worried” retailers, e-commerce operators break the Market Operating Price i.e., MOP (price at which a product is usually sold by the B&M retailers) which is the main reason for the customers to move away from B&M retailers.

Convenience is the next theme as identified during the DI of retailers. “Trust-based purchase” along with “Touch and feel” are among the “Sub-Themes” under “Convenience.” These factors are largely responsible for the walk-in that happens in a retail outlet. B&M retailers engage people to interact with customers to get the exact requirement and gain trust by meeting the requirements over a period. Trust has a major influence on offline loyalty ([Savila, Wathoni and Santoso 2019](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_19)). This has been categorically mentioned by a retailer during the interview by stating “People believe people.” The brick-and-mortar retailer offers the “Touch and Feel” of the product while giving a demonstration to the family members when they come together and helping them to decide collectively thereby offering them good family time and making shopping a pleasure. B&M stores provide services like installation, buyback, and installment payment due to which buying from B&M is convenient for many customers. Their service also includes meeting specific requirements of customers in a particular situation e.g., taking care of home delivery and urgency.

Themes evolved during the FGD, and DI are found to be interlinked through certain “Subthemes.” To draw focus on themes that will be used to take further action, an NLP based model has been used. Relationships among the themes across FGD and DI and their matching with the features indicated by the NLP-based model have been shown in the Relationship diagram in [Figure 17.2](#ch17.xhtml#fig17_2). The diagram reveals that Price is the Theme that is common for FGD and DI. It is a parameter that prospects normally compare before purchasing. E-commerce operators break the Market Operating Price (MOP) i.e., the price at which a product is usually sold by the B&M retailers and attempts to attract customers. Online sellers provide information of different types including “Reviews.” Convenience is also a Theme that is common for FGD and DI. This is supported by Subthemes “Touch & Feel” along with “Personal touch & advice.” This is linked with the “Requirement” of customers which can change depending on the “Situation.”

![Figure 17.2 Relationship diagram: FGD themes and NLP features.](images/fig17_2.jpg)

[Figure 17.2 Relationship diagram: FGD themes and NLP features.](#ch17.xhtml#Ffig17_2)

Further analysis done using Google Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) and data analytics tool panda (python data analysis library) brought out the tensor value of each corpus out of which, tensor values of the top 14 pertinent words have been shown in [Table 17.3](#ch17.xhtml#table17_3). In this table these words have also been matched with the “Themes” and “Sub-themes” as found during FGD (of customers) and DI (of retailers) and mentioned in [Tables 17.1](#ch17.xhtml#table17_1) and [17.2](#ch17.xhtml#table17_2) as well as the relationship diagram in [Figure 17.2](#ch17.xhtml#fig17_2).

| Table 1 || Table 2 || Relationship Diagram |

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

The bar graph in [Figure 17.3](#ch17.xhtml#fig17_3) shows the order and comparison of the tensor values. Based on the order of the tensor values, the nature of the words and their impact on the sentence can be understood. As it is not possible to understand the inner meaning of all the words and their implication in customers’ mind, tensor, or vector of each word of the feedback was needed. This intensive study of data by going deeper into the tensor values has shown the importance of the “Themes” and the “Subthemes” obtained regarding the B&M and e-commerce business through FGD and DI based process of the qualitative research.

![Figure 17.3 Graphical representation words according to “tensor value.”](images/fig17_3.jpg)

[Figure 17.3 Graphical representation words according to “tensor value.”](#ch17.xhtml#Ffig17_3)

## 17.6 Conclusion

Price has been an area of competition where e-commerce players are likely to be in advantageous position due to various reasons including economies of scale and logistics. B&M retailers need to apply their top strengths which include “Touch & Feel” facility of products and “Personal Touch” of their people and available at their stores. But these strengths can only be put to action if the customer comes to the store. This can be done by creating a website as first step of digitalization ([Hokkanen, Walker and Donnelly 2020](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_9)) to provide information about the products available and the services offered. Information should be made available online to customers in such a way that they are motivated to visit the retail stores with friends and family. The move that is needed is more for the mindset as compared to the process reengineering that is needed. It can be a dedicated website for the store or a common website for the market where multiple B&M stores can be present making the venture cost effective. B&M retailers having different types of product portfolios can use a common website for operational advantages. The approach should be Online to Offline (O2O) or vice versa providing the advantages of rich online information, online payment, best online price, offline physical experience and personalized customer service ([Yang et al. 2020](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_24)). Enterprising B&M retailers can go ahead and start offering online ordering that provides online sales followed by offline delivery. Delivering product at customers place is a known operation for retailers. After starting online sales with offline delivery, the B&M retailer can start need based door delivery in phased manner. They may not have the resources to offer everything on their own, but they can use services of third-party platforms ([He, Gupta and Mirchandani 2021](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_8)). The website will open a communication channel through which promotional input can be given to the customers in a cost-effective manner. It has been found that, customers react positively when retailers offer promotions that matches their shopping objective and the customers’ satisfaction level gets enhanced ([Blom, Lange and Hess 2021](#ch17.xhtml#ref17_1)).

For any requirement, everyone reaches out to the individuals and organization of confidence. The B&M retailer needs to keep track of customers’ requirements and cater accordingly to remain as a preferred supplier for them. “Touch & Feel” and “Personal Touch” of human beings are the potential winning tools which the B&M retailers should deploy consistently. Thus, the B&M retailers can migrate to a new way of doing business.

# 18 Design of Novel Energy Management System in Solar PV Powered EV Charging Station Using Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimization

M. Sathesh

Assistant Professor, Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Vel Tech Rangarajan Dr. Sagunthala R&D Institute of Science and Technology, Avadi, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

Banu Priya Prathaban and R. Priyanka

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-18](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-18)

## 18.1 Introduction

In order to monitor electricity use and achieve energy savings, utilities and end users have both used Energy Management Systems (EMS). Along with assessing current systems and appliances, the methodology also takes into account the growing use of EMS in the most recent smart grids \[[1](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_1),[2](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_2)\]. These grids reduce manpower usage by implementing smart meters in the household, but they require security.

The ideal conditions for power plants vary depending on their efficiency and power. The present type is effective up to a 150 °C separator temperature. The high pressure is influenced by the turbine concentration. Low collector cost is produced by high solar beam radiation and optimal cycle conditions \[[3](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_3),[4](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_4)\].

## 18.2 Energy Management System (EMS) Implementation Setup

### 18.2.1 Solar PV Generation Monitoring

The EMS monitors the real-time solar PV generation capacity, including the energy produced by the solar panels and the current weather conditions. This data is used to determine the available solar energy for charging EVs \[[5](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_5)\].

### 18.2.2 Load Monitoring

The EMS tracks the power demand from the EV charging stations, considering the number of connected vehicles, their charging rates, and the charging schedules. This information helps determine the load requirements of the charging station \[[6](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_6)\].

### 18.2.3 Battery Storage Integration

The EMS is in charge of monitoring the batteries' state of charge (SOC) if the charging station includes battery energy storage. It maximizes the use of solar energy by optimizing battery charging and discharging, and it provides effective power management during times of high demand \[[7](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_7)\].

### 18.2.4 Demand Response

The EMS can take part in demand response programs in cases when the charging station is wired into the grid. It enables the charging station to alter its power consumption in response to grid signals, grid conditions, or pricing signals. This aids in grid stabilization and cost control for electricity \[[8](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_8)\].

### 18.2.5 Optimal Charging Scheduling

The EMS optimizes the charging schedules of the EVs based on various factors such as the availability of solar energy, EV owners' preferences, and grid conditions. It aims to minimize grid impact, maximize the utilization of solar energy, and ensure efficient charging for all connected vehicles \[[9](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_9)\].

### 18.2.6 Grid Integration and Balancing

The EMS ensures grid integration by monitoring the grid conditions, including voltage levels, frequency, and grid stability. It adjusts the charging station's operation to maintain a stable grid connection, preventing power quality issues and grid overloading \[[10](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_10)\].

### 18.2.7 Energy Monitoring and Reporting

The EMS collects and analyzes energy data from the solar PV system, battery storage, EV charging stations, and grid interactions. It generates reports on energy consumption, solar PV generation, grid utilization, and overall system performance. These reports can be used for energy accounting, billing, and performance evaluation \[[11](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_11)\].

## 18.3 Mean Square Error

It is common practice to assess the efficacy of a regression model using the Mean Square Error (MSE) statistic. It calculates the average squared difference between a dataset's actual values and projected values. The MSE is calculated by dividing the entire sample count by the sum of the squared differences.

Mathematically, the MSE is computed as follows:

${MSE} = (1/n) \\ast \\sum{(y\_{i} - \\overline{y})}^{2}$ (18.1)

Where:

The dataset contains n samples in total.

The actual value of the i-th sample is represented by yi.

The i-th sample's expected value is represented by the symbol.

A method to gauge the typical magnitude of the model's errors is provided by the MSE. It shows larger errors and is hence sensitive to outliers because of the squared disparities. Better model performance is indicated by a lower MSE score, which implies that the model's predictions are more in line with the actual values \[[12](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_12)\].

The MSE is frequently used in a variety of fields, including machine learning, statistics, and signal processing, to assess the accuracy and efficiency of regression models. It serves as a benchmark for comparing many models or iterations of the same model to see which one best matches the data \[[13](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_13)\].

## 18.4 MSE Significance of EV Charging Stations

### 18.4.1 Charging Efficiency Evaluation

MSE can be used to assess the accuracy of charging models or algorithms used in EV charging stations. Charging models predict factors such as charging time, energy consumption, or charging power required. By comparing the predicted values with the actual values obtained during charging, MSE can provide a measure of how well the model performs. A lower MSE indicates higher accuracy and efficiency in predicting charging behavior \[[14](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_14)\].

### 18.4.2 Load Management Optimization

MSE can be utilized to optimize load management strategies in EV charging stations. Load management involves balancing the charging demand with the available power capacity. By predicting and optimizing charging profiles for multiple EVs, an EMS (Energy Management System) in the charging station can minimize the discrepancy between the predicted and actual charging loads. MSE can be used as an objective function to minimize the difference and improve load management efficiency \[[15](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_15)\].

### 18.4.3 Battery State-of-Charge (SoC) Estimation

It is frequently necessary for EV charging stations to precisely determine the batteries' state of charge. MSE can be used to assess the precision of SoC estimate models or algorithms. The accuracy and dependability of the estimating process can be determined by MSE by comparing the estimated SoC values with real measurements. Better SoC estimation performance is shown by lower MSE values \[[16](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_16)\].

### 18.4.4 Grid Interaction and Energy Management

EV charging stations can participate in grid-level energy management programs, such as demand response or grid-balancing initiatives. In such cases, the MSE can play a role in assessing the accuracy of the charging station's load prediction and response to grid signals. By minimizing the MSE between the predicted and actual load or power consumption during grid interactions, the charging station can optimize its contribution to grid stability and minimize grid-related costs \[[17](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_17)\].

MSE serves as a useful metric in EV charging stations for evaluating charging models, optimizing load management, estimating battery SoC, and improving grid interaction strategies. By minimizing the MSE, charging stations can enhance efficiency, accuracy, and reliability, leading to better utilization of resources and improved integration of EVs into the power grid.

## 18.5 Soft Computing Requirements in EV Charging Station

Soft computing techniques are often required in various applications, including Electric Vehicle (EV) charging stations, due to their ability to handle uncertainty, imprecision, and complex problems that may not be easily solvable by traditional algorithms \[[18](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_18)\].

### 18.5.1 Handling Uncertainty

Soft computing techniques, such as fuzzy logic and probabilistic methods, are well-suited for dealing with uncertainty in EV charging scenarios. Factors like user behavior, traffic conditions, and renewable energy availability can introduce uncertainty into the charging process. Soft computing allows for modeling and decision-making under uncertain conditions, enabling more robust and adaptive charging strategies.

### 18.5.2 Managing Complexity

EV charging stations involve multiple interconnected components, including EVs, batteries, renewable energy sources, and the power grid. Soft computing techniques, such as neural networks and genetic algorithms, can handle the complexity of these systems and assist in optimizing various aspects like load management, charging schedules, and grid integration.

### 18.5.3 Handling Non-Linearity

The charging behavior of EVs and the relationship between different charging parameters often exhibit nonlinear characteristics. Soft computing techniques excel at modeling and capturing non-linear relationships, allowing for more accurate predictions and control in EV charging stations.

### 18.5.4 Adaptive Decision Making

Soft computing techniques can adapt and learn from historical data or real-time feedback, enabling the EV charging station to make informed decisions based on changing conditions. These techniques can self-adjust charging strategies based on factors like user preferences, energy availability, grid signals, and environmental conditions.

### 18.5.5 Optimization and Search

Soft computing techniques, such as evolutionary algorithms and swarm intelligence, are effective in optimization and search problems. They can help find optimal charging schedules, load balancing strategies, and energy management policies in EV charging stations, considering multiple objectives and constraints.

### 18.5.6 Human-Like Decision Making

Soft computing techniques, particularly fuzzy logic, can emulate human-like decision-making processes by incorporating expert knowledge and linguistic rules. This can be beneficial for handling subjective criteria, user preferences, and accommodating different charging scenarios or policies.

EV charging stations' efficiency, dependability, and adaptability will all increase thanks to the use of soft computing approaches. These techniques allow for the management of complex systems, the making of wise judgments, the handling of uncertainty, the optimization of many elements of EV charging, and finally the integration of electric vehicles and renewable energy sources into the power grid.

In this work, the utility and control of a photovoltaic production arrangement with an electric voltage charging of a tiny superconducting magnetic energy storage arrangement is analyzed and the gaps are filled by the proposed system methodology. The management of energy in the electro voltaic chargeable locations is done via transferring the energy through photovoltaic elements, superconducting magnetic energy storage arrangements, electro voltaic batteries and grid. The validation of the proposed energy management system is done via evaluating the system on the basis of efficiency in Simulink of MATLAB package \[[19](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_19)\]

## 18.7 The Grid Application of Photovoltaic Charging Stations for Electric Vehicles

The variety and volume of EV batteries connected to the grid keep expanding quickly, placing a significant load on it. Vehicle to Grid (V2G) is a concept which was offered as a solution to this issue to allow electric vehicles to function which serves to be an altered source for charging in the power network. Vehicle to Grid technology has greater potential to dramatically improve the smart grid system's adaptability and storage of sporadic renewable energy sources like wind and solar \[[20](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_20)\].

Several EV charging station-related studies have been published in the previous five years with the concept of LMA search algorithm and the Grid Partition algorithm based solar PV system for actual economic load dispatch difficulties. In contrast to the almost 31 articles published in Google Scholar, nearly 133 articles were published in IEEE Xplore.

## 18.8 Proposed AGTO

The majority of metaheuristics were inspired by the collective intelligence of natural species in nature and are crucial for addressing optimization problems. This work suggests the Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimizer (GTO), a new metaheuristic algorithm that draws inspiration from the social intelligence of gorilla troops in the wild. In this program, the gorillas' social structure is mathematically described, and new methods are developed to conduct exploration and exploitation. 52 common benchmark functions and seven technical challenges are used to evaluate the performance of the GTO. The proposed approach, along with a few current metaheuristics, were statistically analyzed using Friedman's test and Wilcoxon rank-sum statistical tests. The outcomes show that, especially for high-dimensional problems, the GTO outperforms comparable algorithms on the majority of benchmark functions. The results demonstrate that, in comparison to other metaheuristics, the GTO can produce superior results \[[21](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_21)\].

## 18.9 Neighborhood Nodes Training

Although frequently employed as a neural network training method, the Levenberg Marquardt Algorithm (LMA) has the drawback of being quite costly, since it requires huge storage space for its operation, which in turn increases the epochs to reach the minimum error of the model. Such a hitch in the algorithm hikes the computational complexity of the model since the size of the model is exponentially increasing with the increase in the number of weights to be adjusted. LMA is an optimization algorithm used for solving nonlinear least squares problems.

LMA is particularly effective for minimizing the sum of the squares of the residuals between the observed and predicted values in a nonlinear regression problem. It is commonly used in fields such as curve fitting, data modeling, and optimization. LMA combines the benefits of both the Gauss-Newton algorithm and the steepest descent method. It adjusts the parameters of a nonlinear model by iteratively updating them in a way that reduces the error between the model predictions and the observed data. At each iteration, the algorithm computes the Jacobian matrix, which represents the partial derivatives of the model with respect to each parameter. It then uses this matrix along with the current parameter values to compute an update direction for the parameters.

The update direction is determined by solving a modified linear least squares problem, where a damping factor is introduced to control the step size. LMA continues iteratively until a convergence criterion is met, such as reaching a specified tolerance level or a maximum number of iterations. Upon convergence, it provides an estimate of the optimal parameter values that minimize the sum of squares of residuals. It is an effective optimization technique for solving nonlinear least squares problems. It strikes a balance between the Gauss-Newton method and the steepest descent method, providing a robust and efficient approach for parameter estimation in nonlinear regression and optimization tasks \[[22](#ch18.xhtml#ref18_22)\].

The AGTO inference system, due to its simplicity in error selection and its mean square error method, achieved better performance. EV charging station for an energy management system using neural networks and achieved better performance in mean square error. The charging station used solar panels and achieved the significant mean square error using LMA neural network. The system promotes the charging performance of the E vehicle using a neural network based self-charging algorithm and achieves better mean square error performance.

In the existing methods the mean square error of the power charging stations is very high and unstable due to the load requirement. So, the proposal system focused on using a Bayesian, LMA neural network in comparison with the AGTO to provide a better mean square error performance of the network.

## 18.10 Methodology

The number of samples and the G power were calculated using the G power calculator. Utilized G power with 80% for each test and 10 samples for each group. Group 1 includes the Bayesian method, Group 2 the LMA neural network algorithm, and Group 3 the AGTO network algorithm.

### 18.10.1 Sample 1 Preparation – Bayesian Neural Networks

Bayesian neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of the data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

1.  Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
2.  Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
3.  Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
4.  Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

### 18.10.2 Sample 2 Preparation – LMA Neural Networks

LMA neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of the data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

1.  Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
2.  Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
3.  Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
4.  Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

### 18.10.3 Sample 3 Preparation – AGTO Network Algorithm

AGTO neural network was preferred to overcome the non-linear function of data extracted from the grid surface. Computational methods can be used to develop a better mean square error value.

The steps carried out in this proposed work are as follows.

1.  Step 1: The regression line is identified between the linear and nonlinear data.
2.  Step 2: The estimated average value of the mean square error is calculated from the actual values.
3.  Step 3: The estimated values are extracted from the original value to get the new error value.
4.  Step 4: The obtained error mean values are squared to get the mean square value.

The Mean Square Error of the EV charging station was analyzed using the Bayesian method, LMA neural network algorithm, and AGTO network algorithm using the MATLAB R2018a program. The testing setup was carried out utilizing the Intel i5 processor and 4GB RAM of the MATLAB R2018a SIMULINK program. It combines computing and programming in a user-friendly environment where problems and solutions are expressed using recognizable symbols.

## 18.11 Statistical Analysis

Using an independent sample t-test, the IBM SPSS statistical tool was utilized to analyze the Bayesian, LMA, and AGTO neural networks. For the analysis of independent sample t-tests, the dependent and independent variables are employed. The epoch values at various points are regarded as independent variables, while the mean square error is a dependent variable.

## 18.12 Result

A comparison of the Bayesian, LMA neural network, and AGTO algorithms' group statistics is implemented. The mean square error (MSE) of the Bayesian neural network is 0.079158. **As shown in** [Table 18.1](#ch18.xhtml#table18_1), the LMA neural network achieved a mean square error (MSE) of 0.055524 and the AGTO achieved a mean square error (MSE) of 0.011789.

X-axis: Epochs, Y axis: MSE. At epoch 9, the model performs well gaining an average MSE of about 0.011789 while at the maximum epoch of 100 the MSE value reaches to 0.01514. The data that is trained, validated and tested in the proposed model is highlighted as the blue colored line, green colored line and red colored line, respectively, and the best data is represented as dotted lines during the model implementation.

![Figure 18.3 MSE curve for artificial gorilla troop optimization algorithm.](images/fig18_3.jpg)

[Figure 18.3 MSE curve for artificial gorilla troop optimization algorithm.](#ch18.xhtml#Ffig18_3)

## 18.13 Discussion

In this work, it was concluded that the AGTO has a better mean value and mean square error when compared with the Bayesian neural, LMA neural network by performing an independent t-test. LMA neural network algorithm using PV grid and achieved the mean square error value of 1.008. The energy management uses solar panels to achieve the mean square error of 1.003 using Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm for efficient utilization of available resources. Developed the energy management system in electric vehicles and its components' analysis in various epochs with a better mean square error at 146 epochs. The electric propulsion system and battery management system using Bayesian neural network and achieved the mean square error of -8.043. Developed the battery management system using LMA neural network and swarm optimization method and achieved the mean square error of -9.452. The power allocation approach for load management in electric vehicles using power allocation algorithm and achieved a better mean square error at 565 epoxies.

The limitation in the proposed system is that if the number of electric vehicles (demand) necessitates additional (electric vehicles) EV and subsequently the number of grids needed also increases, resulting in complexity. In future, high-quality substrate and high voltage outcome-based grids can be installed to avoid the complexity.

## 18.14 Conclusion

An Artificial Gorilla Troops Optimization (AGTO) for minimizing the mean square error of the energy management system in solar PV powered EV charging stations using Levenberg-Marquardt (LMA) Neural Network, Bayesian Neural Network Neural Network is proposed and implemented. The proposed AGTO model gains significantly better quantification results when compared to the LMA and Bayesian network. The generation of around 10,137 kWh energy on an annual basis is sufficiently abundant for charging around 450 vehicle batteries of size 27 kWh.

# 19 School Students' Cataract Prediction Using Machine Learning

Nagadevi Bala Nagaram, R. Narmada Devi, Kala Raja Mohan, and Sathish Kumar Kumaravel

Department of Mathematics, Vel Tech Rangarajan Dr. Sagunthala R&D Institute of Science and Technology, Avadi, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-19](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-19)

## 19.1 Introduction

The waves of the Covid pandemic resulted in many school children having to use electronic devices like mobile phones, computer systems, TV, etc. These electronic devices have been essential in the pandemic situation. So children are encouraged to use these devices without knowing the dangers of using them. Every day children are required to use mobile devices for their classes. It leads to only their presence in physical mode but they are not mentally observed. This leads to eye infection or eye damage in their childhood.

The most common eye sight problem among school children is blurry vision. It may be long sight or short sight issues. To succeed in their goal every children need good health and good vision. In their daily attitude they need to read, write and watch; if they have issues in these and get distracted then they may lose their confidence.

Cataracts are uncommon among school students and are more prevalent among older individuals; however, in rare cases cataracts can occur in younger individuals due to various reasons. Here are some unique details about cataract issues among school students.

Congenital Cataracts: During the first year of life, congenital cataracts may begin to form or are already present at birth. They may be brought on by genetic issues, infections contracted while pregnant (like rubella), metabolic problems, or stress.

Traumatic Cataracts: Some schoolchildren who have experienced eye injury or trauma may acquire cataracts. Accidents that occur while playing sports or engaging in other physical activities might cause this.

Secondary Cataracts: Rarely, other eye illnesses or systemic diseases in children might cause cataracts to form as a side effect.

Cataracts can have a substantial negative effect on a child's vision, causing cloudy or hazy vision. Their capacity for reading, writing, and academic success may be impacted by this.

Symptoms: Students with cataracts may experience symptoms like light sensitivity, poor visual acuity, frequent changes in eyeglass prescription, or concerns about having trouble reading or seeing the board in class.

Early Detection and Intervention: Managing cataracts in school pupils requires early identification and prompt treatment. Cataracts can cause irreversible vision loss and amblyopia (lazy eye) if they are not corrected.

Surgical Treatment: In cases where cataracts severely affect vision, surgery may be recommended to remove the damaged lens and fix an artificial lens (IOL). Pediatric cataract surgery requires expertise with specialized care.

Impact on Daily Activities: Cataracts may make it more difficult for kids to play with their friends, participate in sports, and carry out other daily activities.

Social and Emotional Repercussions: Cataracts and other vision issues in school-aged children can have an impact on a child's mental health and social connections with other kids.

After cataract surgery, children may need vision therapy or rehabilitation to improve their visual abilities and visual development.

It's crucial to remember that school-age students seldom get cataracts, and the majority of vision problems in this age range are caused by refractive errors or amblyopia. However, it is crucial to seek expert eye care and have a thorough eye examination by a paediatric ophthalmologist or an eye care specialist with experience in paediatric eye health if a kid displays indications of cataracts or any other vision issue. A child's vision and general health can be greatly enhanced by early detection and suitable treatment.

Researchers used the ML algorithm to analyze and develop their strategy in their field. [Rabaa'I et al. (2022](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_4), pp. 247–258) applied machine learning (ML) techniques to analyze and predict the main factors that influence the continuous usage of mobile food delivery apps. The eye disease detection model is trained ([Shaikh et al., 2022](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_6)) on a huge number of parameters so that it can predict eye disease quickly. [Han (2022)](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_3) observed the superior performance of their technique in both detecting any DR and identifying eyes with referable DR (RDR) in retinographies of type 2 diabetes mellitus (DM) cases, showcasing high levels of accuracy (ACC), sensitivity (S), specificity (SP), positive predictive value (PPV), and negative predictive value (NPV).

The authors ([Bhat et al., 2020](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_2)) challenge addressed in the given scenario is the early detection of cataracts using normal lens images. The goal is to enable individuals to test for cataracts on their own, potentially eliminating the need for people in remote areas to visit ophthalmologists for initial screening. To achieve this, the paper proposes the use of CNN (Convolutional Neural Network) models that take normal lens images as input for the detection of cataract problems.

[Tong et al. (2020)](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_7), discussed ML applications in ophthalmic imaging modalities. [Bernardes et al. (2011](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_1), pp. 161–81) focused mainly on evaluating the accuracy, efficiency, and reliability of these methods in detecting DR from retinal images. This comparison can help in determining the most suitable approach for integrating automated DR detection into screening programs for early diagnosis and intervention. [Saraswat et al. (2022](#ch19.xhtml#ref19_5)) described the real time eyesight power prediction using deep learning method.

## 19.2 Methodology

Generally, machine learning can be divided into various subfields and techniques depending upon paradigms, problem types, and methodologies used. The following is a taxonomy of machine learning:

Supervised Learning: In trained learning, a labelled dataset with input features and their associated output labels is used to train the model. In order to generate predictions on original, unforeseen data, the model must learn a mapping from inputs to outputs.

1.  Classification: The task of predicting a discrete class label. Examples include spam detection, image classification, and sentiment analysis.
2.  Regression: The task of predicting a continuous numerical value. Examples include house price prediction and stock market forecasting.

Unsupervised Learning: In this the model is trained on an unlabelled dataset, and it tries to find structures in the data without any explicit output labels.

1.  Clustering: Grouping similar data points together based on their similarities. Examples include customer segmentation and image segmentation.
2.  Dimensionality Reduction: Reducing the number of input features while preserving meaningful information. Techniques like Principal Component Analysis (PCA) fall into this category.

Combining supervised and unsupervised learning, semi-supervised learning involves training the model using partially labelled data. For supervised learning, it can utilize labelled data, while for unsupervised learning, it can use unlabelled data.

Reinforcement Learning: In reinforcement learning, an agent discovers how to interact with its surroundings in order to accomplish particular objectives. It uses the information to make better decisions. It receives response in the form of incentives or penalties based on its activities.

Deep Learning is a branch of machine learning that models complicated relationships in data using neural networks that are constructed with several layers (deep architectures). In areas including image identification, processing natural language, and gaming, deep learning has made tremendous strides.

Transfer Learning: Leveraging knowledge learned from one task or domain and applying it to a related or new task or domain. This can help improve model performance with limited data.

Ensemble Learning: Combining multiple models (e.g., decision trees, neural networks) to create a more robust and accurate prediction. Techniques like bagging, boosting, and stacking are common in ensemble learning.

An overview of the wide range of machine learning approaches and applications is provided by this taxonomy. Each category has advantages and disadvantages, and the best course of action depends on the nature of the current issue and the data which is available.

Predicting cataracts using machine learning involves building a model that can analyze relevant features from patient data and predict the likelihood of cataract development. Here's a proposed approach for a cataract prediction model using supervised learning.

## 19.3 Taxonomy

Data Collection and Preprocessing: Gather a dataset containing patient information, including age, gender, family history of cataracts, lifestyle habits (e.g., smoking), medical history, and any relevant ocular health measurements. Ensure the data is cleaned, and missing values are handled appropriately.

Feature Selection: Use feature selection techniques to identify the most relevant attributes for cataract prediction. Features like age, family history, and lifestyle habits may play a significant role in predicting cataracts.

Model Selection: Choose a suitable machine learning algorithm for binary classification (has cataracts or does not have cataracts) based on the dataset size, complexity, and computational resources available. Support vector machines (SVM), decision trees, random forests, logistic regression, and gradient boosting models are notable options.

Data Splitting: To assess the model's performance on untested data, divide the dataset into both testing and training sets.

Model Training: Train the selected machine learning algorithm on the training data.

Model Evaluation: Use metrics like accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and area under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) curve to assess the model's performance on the testing data. Cross-validate to evaluate the generalization of the model to do further.

Hyperparameter Tuning: Fine-tune the hyperparameters of the chosen model using techniques like grid search or random search to optimize its performance.

Model Interpretation: Analyze the model's feature importance to understand which features contribute the most to cataract prediction. Interpretability is crucial in the medical domain to gain insights into the prediction process.

Deployment: Deploy the model in a production setting when it has been trained and assessed, keeping data protection and ethical considerations in mind. To keep the model accurate and current, update it frequently with fresh data.

It's essential to emphasize that this is a proposed framework, and developing a cataract prediction model requires a well-curated dataset, domain expertise, and collaboration with healthcare professionals. Medical applications require careful validation, and any model predictions should be used to complement clinical assessments rather than replace them. Additionally, ensuring the privacy and security of patient data is of utmost importance.

## 19.4 Cataract Prediction Model

This cataract prediction model has three different modules which include

1.  Dataset collection
2.  Construct a Model
3.  Evaluation

The description of the proposed model:

1.  **Dataset collection**

The dataset helps in studying the model in prediction and evaluating the outcome. This dataset contains 100 records and 10 attributes.

In [Table 19.1](#ch19.xhtml#table19_1) the category of the data is described.

[Table 19.1 Dataset Category](#ch19.xhtml#Ttable19_1)

  
| **Attributes** || **Type** |
| --- | --- |
| **Age** || **Numeric** |
| --- | --- |
| **family history of cataracts** || **Category** |
| **Diabetes** || **Yes/No** |
| **lifestyle habits (e.g., smoking)** || **Yes/No** |
| **Long time sun exposure** || **Yes/No** |
| **Eye injury** || **Yes/No** |
| **Congenital factors** || **Category** |
| **Medications** || **Category** |
| **Nutritional factors** || **Yes/No** |
| **Systemic diseases** || **Category** |

2.  **Construct a Model**

Using a pipeline in machine learning helps streamline the workflow by combining multiple steps into a single process. Pipelines can be beneficial for tasks like cataract prediction, where data preprocessing and model training need to be performed in a systematic and organized manner. In this example, we'll create a pipeline to handle data preprocessing and build a cataract prediction model using the Random Forest classifier.

Algorithm: Cataract Prediction using pipeline

1.  Step 1: Import the appropriate libraries
2.  Step 2: Import Cataract dataset
3.  Step 3: Create pipeline for algorithm giving accuracy
4.  Step 4: Add this pipeline in dataset
5.  Step 5: Fit the pipeline
6.  Step 6: Compare the accuracies
7.  Step 7: Prediction and identification of the most accurate model will be done by taken data

By creating a pipeline, one can easily modify or extend the workflow without repeating steps. For example, one can add more preprocessing steps, try different models, or include hyperparameter tuning techniques using GridSearchCV or RandomizedSearchCV within the pipeline.

3.  **Evaluation**

Evaluation is the final stage of prediction model. It uses various evaluation processes, such as Classification accuracy, Confusion matrix and F1-score.

Machine learning uses classification accuracy as a standard evaluation parameter for binary classification problems like cataract prediction. It determines the percentage of correctly identified samples (including true positives and true negatives) when compared with the total number of samples in the dataset.

The following equation (19.1) represents the accuracy formula:

${Accuracy} = \\frac{Number\\ of\\ correctly\\ classified\\ samples}{Total\\ number\\ of\\ samples\\ }$ (19.1)

The score of accuracy provides a comprehensive performance measure of how effectively the model predicts both cataract-positive and cataract-negative cases in cataracts.

Confusion Matrix: A table known as a confusion matrix is used to evaluate the efficacy of a classification model. The degree to which the model's predictions coincide with the actual labels is fully described. The confusion matrix is especially helpful for binary classification tasks like cataract prediction, where the target variable has two classes: positive (like a cataract) and negative (like no cataract).

The four outcomes that can result from a binary categorization are shown in a confusion matrix:

True positives: The number of samples for which the model's positive (cataract) prediction was accurate is known as the true positives (TP) rate.

False Positives: The proportion of samples that the model mistakenly predicted to be positive (cataract) when they are actually negative (no cataract).

True Negatives: The number of samples for which the model's prediction of a negative result (no cataract) was accurate is known as the “True Negatives” (TN).

False Negatives: The proportion of samples for which the model made an incorrect prediction of a negative result (no cataract), but where the actual result is positive (cataract).

[Table 19.2](#ch19.xhtml#table19_2) is a common representation of the confusion matrix.

 [Table 19.2 Confusion Matrix](#ch19.xhtml#Ttable19_2) 

 
| Predicted values ||  || Actual Values |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cataract || Not Cataract |
|   || Cataract || TP(32) || FP(20) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|   || Not Cataract || FN(34) || TN(14) |

Accuracy: It is measured as the percentage of accurate predictions made out of all samples (TP + TN) divided by (TP + TN + FP + FN).

Precision: TP/(TP + FP) represents the ratio of real positive predictions to all positive forecasts. It measures the model's ability to avoid producing false-positive results.

Recall (Sensitivity): TP / (TP + FN) is the ratio of TP predictions to all real positive samples. It assesses how well the model can recognize positive samples.

F1-Score: The F1-score, which is the harmonic mean of accuracy and recall (2 \* precision \* Recall) / (Precision + Recall), provides a balanced measurement between the two.

Precision is a fantastic area to concentrate on if you intend to reduce false positives. Recall is essential in fields like medicine where you want to reduce the possibility of missing positive instances (or forecasting false negatives). These are often situations in which failing to identify a good example has a considerably greater impact than misclassifying something as positive. The tests show that the F1-score, which combines precision and recall, is effective even when the datasets are unbalanced since it necessitates that both precision and recall have a decent value.If the precision or recall of the positive class is less than l, the formula will weigh the metric value less heavily. The true positive, true negative, false positive and false negative values are obtained through machine learning algorithm as 32, 20, 34 and 14, respectively.

# 20 Minimization of the Threat of Diabetic Kidney Disease through the Lens of Machine Learning

Sudipta Adhikary

Assistant Professor, Department of Law, Brainware University, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Kaushik Chanda and Gunjan Mukherjee

Associate Professor, Department of Computational Science, Brainware University, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

Kaushik Banerjee

Professor, Department of Law, Brainware University, Kolkata, West Bengal, India

DOI: [10.1201/9781032725444-20](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781032725444-20)

## 20.1 Introduction

A significant rise of Type 2 diabetes along with obesity on a global scale has become prevalent on a drastically high scale. This has therefore resulted in a worrisome escalation of the occurrence of diabetic kidney disease (DKD) \[[1](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_1),[2](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_2),[3](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_3)\]. Nevertheless, scholars are currently endeavoring to comprehend the genetic alterations in structure and regulation that contribute to the heritability of those traits \[[4](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_4),[5](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_5)\]. In the period following Genome-Wide Association Studies (GWAS), the utilization of machine learning techniques \[[6](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_6),[7](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_7),[8](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_8)\] and traditional statistical approaches, for example logistic regression, offers valuable tools for uncovering new relationships. The consequent affection of the vital organ kidney due to such prevalence of diabetics has also been observed in the current decades at some alarming rate. Nevertheless, the complete exploration and harnessing of their potential and utilization in prevalent conditions such as Type 2 diabetes has to be achieved. To address this, we conducted a study using seven ML approaches on a substantial database containing phenotypic and genotypic information on potential genes related to vasculopathy. The goal was to determine the most effective groupings of clinical characteristics that can predict DKD and potentially aid in early intervention and treatment decisions.

## 20.2 Methods

### 20.2.1 Subjects

Remarkably, during the first 12 months following a diagnosis, none of the patients experienced ketoacidosis without any apparent reason, indicating the importance of early detection and management. To ensure ethical considerations, the study's methodology underwent a thorough review and approval by the Institutional Review Board for Clinical Research at the Chinese University of Hong Kong.

### 20.2.2 Evaluation in a Clinical Setting and Analytical Testing

Every individual in the study got a thorough examination, both clinically and biochemically, which lasted for a duration of four hours. The specific procedures and findings of this evaluation have been extensively documented in reference \[[9](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_9)\]. The drug utilization and cardiovascular disease treatment history of the patient were also recorded. A comprehensive physical examination was administered in order to detect any indications of retinopathy, sensory neuropathy, and peripheral artery disease.

In order to evaluate different indicators of health, blood samples were obtained after a period of fasting to quantify plasma glucose, glycated haemoglobin, lipid profile, and kidney function. The estimation of glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) in China utilized the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease Formula \[[10](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_10)\]. An eGFR that comes under 60 ml/min/1.73 m² is indicative of diabetic renal failure, as stated in reference \[[11](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_11)\]. The implementation of censorship occurred on 30 July 2005, with the primary objective of safeguarding patient privacy and facilitating data accessibility for all those seeking medical care.

### 20.2.3 Genotyping

A study about genotype contributes a lot towards unearthing genetic information and also helps to uncover the trends of diseases. In order to achieve genotyping, ‘Roche Molecular Systems’ line arrays with 107 single nucleotide polymorphisms were used. These genes were chosen based on published research that established their connection to cardiovascular disease, immunological response, and inflammation \[[12](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_12),[13](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_13)\]. Furthermore, given the known association between certain genetic variations and diabetic kidney disease (DKD), the study also included genotyping of ALR2 (aldose reductase) polymorphisms. The method for determining one's ALR2 polymorphism genotype has been described in detail elsewhere \[[14](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_14)\]. To assess the quality of the genotype data, PLINK was employed to evaluate genotype call rates.

### 20.2.4 Patient Selection

Patient selection depends on traits of disease syndromes. Any flaw in the diagnosis can result in a false choice for the patient. To achieve a valid and trustworthy analysis, we used stringent criteria for our patient population. From the initial cohort of 1,386, we excluded 500 patients with type 2 diabetes who lacked both baseline and final eGFR measurements. Additionally, we excluded individuals who experienced fluctuations in their renal function during the study period. In order to accomplish this, we had to exclude patients who began with normal renal function but developed diabetic kidney disease (n = 80) and those who started out with diabetic kidney disease but improved to normal renal function (n = 6). The final analysis carried out in this paper focused on patients with stable eGFR throughout the baseline and follow-up periods. Specifically, patients with diabetic kidney disease (n = 119) were included if their eGFR was less than 55 ml/min/1.73, while those without diabetic kidney disease (DKD) were included if their eGFR remained above 65 ml/min/1.73 m². The selection process aimed to ensure that the chosen individuals represented stable and distinct renal function groups for a more accurate examination of our research objectives.

### 20.2.5 Selection of Variables

To identify novel predictors, we carefully refined our dataset by excluding parameters related to renal function, such as urinary ACR and serum creatinine levels. We were mindful of potential bias introduced by pharmacological indications, where patients with more risk factors might have received treatment, thus not including drug data in our analysis.

We removed closely correlated variables from the dataset to avoid redundancy and improve model performance. This method resulted in a final set of 87 features for use in our models, including 17 clinical traits and 70 SNPs from 54 candidate genes. During this step, we also removed potentially irrelevant variables. Subsequently, we divided these attributes into three groups to apply various machine learning algorithms. The first group combined clinical and genetic factors, the second focused on genetic factors alone, and the third utilized only clinical factors. By categorizing the data in this manner, we aimed to explore the contribution of different variables in predicting diabetic kidney disease and optimize the performance of our models.

### 20.2.6 Data Imputation for Missing Values and Skewed Distributions

The performance of the machine learning model is vastly dependent on the accuracy of data. Missing values of data can pose a serious threat to the final result which further can hamper in the decision scenario. Considering such aspect, we employed a case-by-case similarity approach to handle missing values in our dataset. Our approach included imputing the missing values by identifying the ten most identical cases and computing the Euclidean distance among their output data. We then estimated the midpoint value for the missing data point. We used the Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique to correct for the underrepresentation of the minority class, which includes those who suffer from Duchenne muscular dystrophy \[[15](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_15)\]. Simultaneously, we under-sampled the majority class to balance the dataset effectively. This ensured that our machine learning models were not biased towards the majority class and could provide more accurate predictions for both classes with proper precision value for the classification \[[16](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_16),[17](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_17),[18](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_18),[19](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_19),[20](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_20),[21](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_21),[22](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_22),[23](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_23),[24](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_24),[25](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_25),[26](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_26)\].

### 20.2.7 Statistical Analysis

Statistical analysis is important for assessment of the research result. In this paper the analysis of such clinical data has been done with the help of SPSS Statistics 17.0 software from SPSS Inc., based in Chicago. Clinical data were presented in either median (IQR) or percentage formats, depending on the nature of the data. The random variation of presented data has judiciously been sorted to annul its effect on the end result achieved.

### 20.2.8 Tuning through Modelling

The overall accuracy and its enhancement are the main sought-after issue. This can be achieved by means of proper tuning of model parameters. In our study, we employed various machine-learning techniques. The R programming language was utilized for all machine learning procedures, and specific package versions and settings for each technique are detailed in Additional file 2. To ensure reliable results, we partitioned the data into a training set (comprising 75% of the total data) and a testing set (representing the remaining 25%). Each machine learning technique underwent ten different parameter combinations, and ten-fold cross-validation was applied.

We used the area under the curve maximization in a Receiver Operating Characteristics (ROC) analysis to select the best model for further use. Subsequently, the testing dataset was employed to evaluate how well the trained models could predict DKD. In the next step, we employed the top-performing machine learning techniques to identify the essential variables needed to build models that matched the efficacy of those trained using the complete dataset. These efforts allowed us to derive meaningful insights and build robust models for predicting DKD accurately.

## 20.3 Result Analysis

Patients with diabetic kidney disease (DKD) tended to be older and heavier than others. They were more likely to be on hypertension, cholesterol, or insulin medications. Additionally, DKD patients had higher blood pressure, urinary ACR, and worse lipid profiles. Patients with DKD had a median eGFR of 38.0 ml/min/1.73 m2 after a median follow-up of 7.8 years, while those without DKD had a median eGFR of 119.9 ml/min/1.73 m². We conducted a comparison between the baseline and final follow-up data of patients included in the analysis and those excluded from the study due to incomplete phenotypes. By excluding respondents with these characteristics, we aimed to detect concealed patterns early and enable timely interventions. We assessed the efficacy of different machine learning techniques using three sets of variables: 1) a combination of clinical and genetic data, 2) genetic data only, and 3) exclusively clinical data ([Figure 20.1](#ch20.xhtml#fig20_1)).

![Figure 20.1 Ten-fold cross validation for predictive performance by different machine learning methods in the DKD training dataset using a) clinical and genetic attributes b) genetic only attributes and c) clinical only attributes.](images/fig20_1.jpg)

[Figure 20.1 Ten-fold cross validation for predictive performance by different machine learning methods in the DKD training dataset using a) clinical and genetic attributes b) genetic only attributes and c) clinical only attributes.](#ch20.xhtml#Ffig20_1)

[Figure 20.2](#ch20.xhtml#fig20_2) illustrates that SVM Radial and Random Forest performed exceptionally well in fitting the best models to the test data. When considering purely genetic characteristics, the net performed slightly better than the forest. Our findings indicated that providing various features for model development and prediction enhanced the performance of several machine-learning approaches. Both clinical and genetic factors played crucial roles in predicting DKD, with clinical factors being particularly influential, as seen in the model selection process, except for the C5.0 Tree. Systolic blood pressure. Polymorphisms in genes and uteroglobin (UGB G38A) were frequently utilized in model construction. Given the superior prediction performance of svmRadial and forest, our focus got shifted towards identifying a subset of clinical and genetic qualities that could be cast off to shape the models using all 87 features. This step was crucial in optimizing the model and making it more efficient without compromising its accuracy. [Figure 20.1](#ch20.xhtml#fig20_1) shows the ten-fold cross validation using the three different types of attributes.

Prediction accuracy by different machine learning methods is represented in [Figure 20.2](#ch20.xhtml#fig20_2).

To ensure a balanced and unbiased approach, we began by extracting the top 20 ranking variables from the ‘svmRadial’ and ‘forest’ models in the “genetic,” “clinical,” and “both” factor subgroups. By using a unified machine learning strategy, we hoped to reduce the possibility of bias. Next, we hand-picked the variables from each model that showed up in both the “genetic” and “both” lists, as well as the “clinical” and “both” lists. After a thorough analysis, we identified 15 consistently present features in both the svmRadial and forest models. As a result of this reduced set of features, we observed a slight decrease in accuracy for the ‘forest model’, while the ‘svmRadial’ model's accuracy remained relatively stable ([Figure 20.3a](#ch20.xhtml#fig20_3)). The comparative analysis of the results obtained from the respective models vouches for the potentiality of svmRadial over the other concerned models. This carefully curated combination of clinical and genetic attributes allowed us to create robust models that retained their predictive power.

![Figure 20.3 (a) Accuracy value (b) Prediction performance by support (svmRadial) and (c) random forest (cforest) using ten and five most frequently selected clinical and genetic attributes, respectively.](images/fig20_3.jpg)

## 20.4 Discussion

We applied resampling analysis and ten-fold cross-validation to optimize model performance to compare different techniques. From these top-performing models, we identified ten clinical and five genetic attributes as the strongest predictors of DKD. These attributes provided valuable insights into disease's development and could aid in early identification and intervention strategies.

Notably, the Naive Bayes classifier showed significant variation in performance even during training, indicating high sensitivity to the data used. While the neural network exhibited high prediction accuracy, it only matched the support vector machine and random forest when both sets of attributes were considered. Overall, the support vector machine emerged as the best-performing method in predicting DKD, making it a valuable tool for clinical applications. The prediction performance was carried out by svmRadial and random forest using ten and five most frequently selected clinical and genetic attributes, respectively.

New doors have opened as a result of the quick development of molecular technology. Therefore, it becomes essential to identify the most effective combination of genetic and clinical variables for predicting diabetic kidney disease (DKD). These results raise the possibility that genetic determinants are less predictive than previously thought, particularly in people who have DKD-predictive symptoms. Our study further reaffirmed the importance of early diagnosis and optimal blood pressure management as effective preventive measures for reducing DKD risk, even after accounting for age. As we have seen in earlier research, metabolic disorders characterized by central obesity, dyslipidaemia, and inflammation are strongly correlated with lipids, WHR, and WBC as the most predictive clinical variables for DKD.

Moreover, our results supported the role of specific genetic polymorphisms (LIPC -514C > T, APOB Thr71Ile, and UGB G38A) in DKD, corroborating earlier findings from regression analysis. While our study had strengths, including a prospective design, comprehensive phenotype and genetic data, and a well-defined DKD, there were some limitations. Notably, we excluded eGFR and urine ACR to identify new predictors, and drug indications likely influenced drug treatment selection by machine learning approaches, necessitating their exclusion. Additionally, this study served as a proof of concept, and further validation in larger samples is required. Lastly, the findings may not be generalizable to populations beyond Chinese participants or those who do not have diabetes.

## 20.5 Conclusions

In our study, we intended to determine the most effective combination of clinical and genetic indicators for predicting DKD. To achieve this, we conducted an analysis of a prospective database utilizing seven popular machine-learning approaches with a comparative study of outcomes for each of the classifiers. These findings hold promise for identifying high-risk individuals who may benefit from routine surveillance and personalized treatment, particularly in managing inflammation and dyslipidaemia, which are crucial to preventing DKD \[[27](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_27),[28](#ch20.xhtml#ref20_28)\]. To fully harness the potential of these genetic markers, it is essential to validate their efficacy in individuals without clinical symptoms of renal disease. By doing so, it can be ensured that these markers can accurately predict DKD risk even before manifestation of noticeable symptoms, enabling early intervention and improved patient outcomes. As a future scope for such research work, it can be extended to implementation of a deep learning based model which can perform the analysis on the basis of images of samples rather than raw medicinal data.

# 21 A Novel Segmentation and Feature Extraction-Based Plant Disease Diagnosis Method Based on Stacked Ensemble Learning

Karthickmanoj. R.
